\sum_{J\in\mathcal{J}(\lambda)}(\#\mathbb{T}[J])^{e(\iota)}\lesssim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}(\lambda/\rho)^{-(1+1/\kappa)}\rho^{-(1+1/\kappa)}\delta^{-(n-1+\varepsilon/2)e(\iota)+1}.

Thus, by Hölder’s inequality,

\sum_{J\in\mathcal{J}(\lambda)}\#\mathbb{T}[J]\lesssim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}(\lambda/\rho)^{-B_{1}}\rho^{-B_{1}}\delta^{-(n-1)+\kappa_{\star}-\varepsilon/2}

where
\kappa_{\star}:=1/e(\iota)
and
B_{1}:=1+\kappa_{\star}/\kappa
agrees with the above definition. It is easy to check that
\kappa_{\circ}\leq\kappa_{\star}
, so the desired bound (
4.8
) follows.

Step 3: Constructing the parametrisation, part 1.
The goal for the remainder of the proof is to establish (
4.10
) under the stated hypotheses. Let
J\in\mathcal{J}_{2}(\lambda)
. Following the strategy of
[
28
]
, we shall use the family of tubes
\mathbb{T}[J]
to construct a parametrisation of the set
N_{10\delta}G
, and use this parametrisation to estimate the volume.

We shall now assume
\mathbb{T}
is a collection of
\delta
-tubes which is either direction-separated or centre-separated and, without loss of generality, that
\mathbb{T}[J]\neq\emptyset
. The two cases are similar and so, for the next two steps, we treat them in parallel. At this stage, we shall not make use of the additional assumptions featured in Case I) and Case II) defined above.

Fix
J\in\mathcal{J}(\lambda)
and let

\mathcal{L}:=\big\{(\omega;y)\in\Omega_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}:\widetilde{T}_{y,\omega}[\delta/2,J]\subseteq N_{2\delta}G\big\}.

As in
[
28
]
, the Tarski–Seidenberg projection theorem (see Theorem
A.4
) can be used to show
\mathcal{L}
is a semialgebraic set of complexity
O_{d,\varepsilon}(1)
. Define the projections
\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
by

\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}\colon(\omega;y)\mapsto\omega\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}\colon(\omega;y)\mapsto y,\qquad(\omega;y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}.

If
(\omega;y)\in\Omega_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
is such that
\widetilde{T}_{y,\omega}[\delta,J]\subseteq N_{2\delta}G
, then not only do we have
(\omega;y)\in\mathcal{L}
, but also that

\big(\{\omega\}\times B^{n-1}(y,c_{\phi}\delta)\big)\cap(\Omega_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ})\subseteq\mathcal{L};\quad\big(B^{n-1}(\omega,c_{\phi}\delta)\times\{y\}\big)\cap(\Omega_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ})\subseteq\mathcal{L},

for some constant
c_{\phi}>0
depending only on
\phi
. Therefore,

•

|\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}(\mathcal{L})|\gtrsim_{\phi}\delta^{n-1}\#\mathbb{T}[J]
if
\mathbb{T}
is direction-separated;

•

|\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}(\mathcal{L})|\gtrsim_{\phi}\delta^{n-1}\#\mathbb{T}[J]
if
\mathbb{T}
is centre-separated.

By
[
28
, Lemma 2.2]
(for the reader’s convenience, this is reproduced as Corollary A.5 in the appendix of this article), we can find semialgebraic sections for
\mathcal{L}
under the projections
\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}
, of bounded complexity. More precisely, there exist semialgebraic subsets
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ctr}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dir}}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
, each of complexity
O_{d,\varepsilon}(1)
, such that

•

\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dir}})=\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}(\mathcal{L})
and
\#\big[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dir}}\cap\big(\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}\big)^{-1}\big(\{y\}\big)\big]=1
for all
y\in\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}(\mathcal{L})
;

•

\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ctr}})=\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}(\mathcal{L})
and
\#\big[\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ctr}}\cap\big(\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}\big)^{-1}\big(\{\omega\}\big)\big]=1
for all
\omega\in\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}(\mathcal{L})
.

Both
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dir}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ctr}}
are compact semialgebraic subsets of
\Omega_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
of complexity
O_{d,\varepsilon}(1)
. Furthermore,

•

\dim\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dir}}=n-1
if
\mathbb{T}
is direction-separated;

•

\dim\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ctr}}=n-1
if
\mathbb{T}
is centre-separated.

Therefore, by Gromov’s algebraic lemma (see Lemma
A.6
) and pigeonholing, for
K=K(\varepsilon):=\lceil 1000n^{3}/\varepsilon\rceil
there exists a
C^{K}
function

h=(P,D)\colon[0,1]^{n-1}\mapsto\Omega_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}

with
\|h\|_{C^{K}}\leq 1
such that the following holds:

•

If
\mathbb{T}
is direction-separated, then

h([0,1]^{n-1})\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dir}}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad|h([0,1]^{n-1})|\sim_{d,\varepsilon}|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dir}}|.

•

If
\mathbb{T}
is centre-separated, then

h([0,1]^{n-1})\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ctr}}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad|h([0,1]^{n-1})|\sim_{d,\varepsilon}|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ctr}}|.

Since
|D([0,1]^{n-1})|\sim|\Pi_{\mathrm{dir}}(\mathcal{L})|
and
|P([0,1]^{n-1})|\sim|\Pi_{\mathrm{ctr}}(\mathcal{L})|
, we have

•

|D([0,1]^{n-1})|\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{n-1}\#\mathbb{T}[J]
if
\mathbb{T}
is direction-separated;

•

|P([0,1]^{n-1})|\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{n-1}\#\mathbb{T}[J]
if
\mathbb{T}
is centre-separated.

Now observe that, for every
(\omega,y)\in\mathcal{L}
, the point
\big(\widetilde{\Psi}(\omega;t;y),t\big)
lies in
N_{2\delta}G
for all
t\in J
and, in turn,
(P(x),D(x))\in\mathcal{L}
for every
x\in[0,1]^{n-1}
. Thus, the map

(4.12)

(x,t)\mapsto\big(\widetilde{\Psi}(P(x);t;D(x)),t\big)\in N_{2\delta}G,\qquad(x,t)\in[0,1]^{n-1}\times J

can be thought of as parametrising a subset of
N_{2\delta}G
.

Step 4: Constructing the parametrisation, part 2.
In order for (
4.12
) to be an effective parametrisation, we require that it be a
O_{d,\varepsilon}(1)
to
1
mapping. To ensure this, rather than work with (
4.12
), we work with a polynomial approximant. By pigeonholing, there exists an
x_{B}\in[0,1]^{n-1}
such that
B:=B(x_{B},\delta^{\varepsilon/(10n)})\cap[0,1]^{n-1}
satisfies

•

|D(B)|\gtrsim\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|D([0,1]^{n-1})|
if
\mathbb{T}
is direction-separated;

•

|P(B)|\gtrsim\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|P([0,1]^{n-1})|
if
\mathbb{T}
is centre-separated.

Let
\widetilde{D}
and
\widetilde{P}
denote the degree
K-1
Taylor polynomial of
D
and
P
, respectively, centred at the point
x_{B}
. Since
\|h\|_{C^{K}}\leq 1
, by the Lagrange form of the remainder,

|\widetilde{D}(x)-D(x)|<\delta^{10n}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad|\widetilde{P}(x)-P(x)|<\delta^{10n}\qquad\textrm{for all $x\in B$.}

In particular, provided
\delta>0
is chosen sufficiently small, we can ensure that

•

|\widetilde{D}(B)|\gtrsim\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|D([0,1]^{n-1})|
if
\mathbb{T}
is direction-separated;

•

|\widetilde{P}(B)|\gtrsim\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|P([0,1]^{n-1})|
if
\mathbb{T}
is centre-separated.

Furthermore, by (
4.12
) and the mean value theorem applied to
\widetilde{\Psi}
, the map

(4.13)

(x,t)\mapsto\big(\widetilde{\Psi}(\widetilde{P}(x);t;\widetilde{D}(x)),t\big)\in N_{3\delta}G,\qquad(x,t)\in B\times J

can be thought of as parametrising a subset of
N_{3\delta}G
. The advantage of the parametrisation (
4.13
) over (
4.12
) is that it is formed by polynomials of degree
O_{d,\varepsilon}(1)
and therefore has controlled multiplicity.

If
\mathbb{T}
is direction- (respectively, centre-) separated, then
|\widetilde{D}(B)|\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{n-1}>0
(respectively,
|\widetilde{P}(B)|\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{n-1}>0
) and so, by Sard’s theorem, the Jacobian determinant of
\widetilde{D}
(respectively,
\widetilde{P}
) cannot vanish identically. Therefore, by the effective inverse function theorem from Lemma
4.8
, there exists an open set
\widetilde{B}\subseteq B
such that

•

If
\mathbb{T}
is direction-separated, then
\widetilde{D}|_{\widetilde{B}}\colon\widetilde{B}\to\widetilde{D}(\widetilde{B})
is a diffeomorphism,

(4.14-I)

|\widetilde{D}(\widetilde{B})|\gtrsim_{d,\varepsilon}\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|D([0,1]^{n-1})|\quad\textrm{and}\quad\inf_{x\in\widetilde{B}}|\det\partial_{x}\widetilde{D}(x)|\gtrsim_{\phi}|\widetilde{D}(\widetilde{B})|;

•

If
\mathbb{T}
is centre-separated, then
\widetilde{P}|_{\widetilde{B}}\colon\widetilde{B}\to\widetilde{P}(\widetilde{B})
is a diffeomorphism,

(4.14-II)

|\widetilde{P}(\widetilde{B})|\gtrsim_{d,\varepsilon}\delta^{\varepsilon/2}|P([0,1]^{n-1})|\quad\textrm{and}\quad\inf_{x\in\widetilde{B}}|\det\partial_{x}\widetilde{P}(x)|\gtrsim_{\phi}|\widetilde{P}(\widetilde{B})|.

More precisely, Lemma
4.8
and pigeonholing amongst the resulting open sets produces an open set
U
satisfying the first two properties. The set
U
can then be further refined to ensure the Jacobian bound in (
4.14-I
) or (
4.14-II
). Indeed, such a refinement is possible due to quantitative Sard estimate

\big|G\big(\{x\in U:|\det\partial_{x}G(x)|<\gamma\}\big)\big|=\int_{\{x\in U:|\det\partial_{x}G(x)|<\gamma\}}|\det\partial_{x}G(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\gamma|U|

for
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open and
G\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
an injective
C^{1}
mapping. Specifically, this estimate is applied with
G=\widetilde{D}
and
\gamma=c_{\phi}\cdot|\widetilde{D}(U)|
to yield (
4.14-I
), and with
G=\widetilde{P}
and
\gamma=c_{\phi}\cdot|\widetilde{P}(U)|
to yield (
4.14-II
), for an appropriately small constant
c_{\phi}
in both cases.

Step 5: Freezing variables.
In light of (
4.13
), we may bound

|N_{3\delta}G\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times J)|\geq\int_{J}\big|\big\{\widetilde{\Psi}(\widetilde{P}(x),t,\widetilde{D}(x)):x\in\widetilde{B}\big\}\big|\,\mathrm{d}t.

For each fixed
t\in J
, the next step is to apply the change of variables formula to estimate the image of
\widetilde{B}
under our parametrisation. So far, we have only been working under the hypothesis that
\phi
is a Hörmander-type phase and
\mathbb{T}
is either direction- or centre-separated. At this juncture, we exploit the additional hypotheses in Case I) and Case II), as defined in Step 2. We shall treat these two cases in parallel. To this end, we let
W_{\mathrm{I}}:=\widetilde{D}(\widetilde{B})
and
W_{\mathrm{II}}:=\widetilde{P}(\widetilde{B})
.

•

In Case I), let

\mathfrak{p}(y):=\widetilde{P}\circ\widetilde{D}^{-1}(y)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathfrak{P}(y):=\partial_{y}\mathfrak{p}(y),\quad\text{for all $y\in W_{\mathrm{I}}$.}

•

In Case II), let

\mathfrak{d}(\omega):=\widetilde{D}\circ\widetilde{P}^{-1}(\omega)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathfrak{D}(\omega):=\partial_{\omega}\mathfrak{d}(\omega),\quad\text{for all $\omega\in W_{\mathrm{II}}$.}

Here the inverses are well-defined smooth functions by the construction of
\widetilde{B}
.

By combining the observations of the previous steps, in Case
\iota
) for
\iota\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{II}\}
, we have

(4.15)

|W_{\iota}|\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{n-1+\varepsilon/2}\#\mathbb{T}[J].

Our additional hypotheses also imply the existence of
\alpha(\iota),\beta(\iota)\in\binom{[n-1]}{n-m}
for which the following hold:

•

In Case I), for
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;y)
as in (
4.1
), we define

A_{\mathrm{I}}(t;y):=\Big[\mathfrak{P}(y)-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;y)\big)\Big]_{\alpha(\mathrm{I}),\beta(\mathrm{I})}.

Then we have a sublevel set estimate

(4.16-I)

\left|\left\{(t;y)\in\bar{I}\times Y_{\circ}:|\det A_{\mathrm{I}}(t;y)|<\sigma\right\}\right|\lesssim_{\phi}\sigma^{\kappa}\qquad\textrm{for all $0<\sigma<1$.}

•

In Case II), for
\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(\omega;t)
as in (
4.1
), we define

A_{\mathrm{II}}(t;\omega):=\Big[I_{n-1}-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(\omega;t)\big)\mathfrak{D}(\omega)\Big]_{\alpha(\mathrm{II}),\beta(\mathrm{II})}.

Then we have a uniform sublevel set estimate

(4.16-II)

\sup_{t\in\bar{I}}\left|\left\{\omega\in X_{\circ}:|\det A_{\mathrm{II}}(t;\omega)|<\sigma\right\}\right|\lesssim_{\phi}\sigma^{\kappa}\qquad\textrm{for all $0<\sigma<1$.}

Here we are using the submatrix notation introduced at the beginning of §
2.3
. To simplify the exposition, we assume in either case that
A_{\iota}
is formed from the top-left
(n-m)\times(n-m)
-submatrix: that is,
\alpha(\iota)=\beta(\iota)=\{1,\dots,n-m\}
. The general case, where the
A_{\iota}
are formed by deleting other rows and columns of the above matrices, is analogous.

Let
\Pi_{m}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
denote the orthogonal projection

\Pi_{m}\colon(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})\mapsto(y_{n-m+1},\dots,y_{n-1}),\qquad(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}.

Let
\iota\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{II}\}
and, given
v:=(v_{n-m+1},\dots,v_{n-1})\in\Pi_{m}(W_{\iota})
, let

U_{\iota}(v):=\big\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{n-m}:(u,v)\in W_{\iota}\big\}.

In Case
\iota
), we define
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}\colon U_{\iota}(v)\times J\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1}\to\mathbb{R}
for all
v\in\Pi_{m}(W_{\iota})
by

\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta):=\widetilde{\Psi}\circ V_{\iota}(t;u,v)+(\partial_{\omega}\widetilde{\Psi})\circ V_{\iota}(t;u,v)\big(\sum_{j=n-m+1}^{n-1}\eta_{j}e_{j}\big),

where
V_{\mathrm{I}}(t;y):=(\mathfrak{p}(y);t;y)
and
V_{\mathrm{II}}(t;\omega):=(\omega;t;\mathfrak{d}(\omega))
. Here
e_{j}
denotes the
j
th standard basis vector and
\eta=(\eta_{n-m+1},\dots,\eta_{n-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
.

For
\iota\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{II}\}
and
v\in\Pi_{m}(W_{\iota})
, by (
4.13
), the fact that
\|\partial_{\omega}\Psi\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\phi}1
, as well as the fact that
\widetilde{\Psi}
is an appropriate polynomial approximation of
\Psi
(see (
4.22
) for more details), in Case
\iota
) we have

\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\in N_{C_{\phi}\delta}G\qquad\textrm{for all $(u;t;\eta)\in U_{\iota}(v)\times J\times B(0,\delta)$,}

where here and below
B(0,\delta)
denotes a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
, and
C_{\phi}
is a constant that depends only on
\phi
. Therefore,

(4.17)

|N_{C_{\phi}\delta}G\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times J)|\gtrsim\int_{J}|E_{\iota,v}(t)|\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\textrm{for}\quad E_{\iota,v}(t):=\big\{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta):(u,\eta)\in U_{\iota}(v)\times B(0,\delta)\big\}.

Furthermore, by Sard’s theorem,
|E_{\iota,v}(t)|=|E_{\iota,v}^{\star}(t)|
where

E_{\iota,v}^{\star}(t):=\big\{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta):(u,\eta)\in U_{\iota}(v)\times B(0,\delta),\,\det J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\neq 0\big\}

where
J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)
is the
(n-1)\times(n-1)
Jacobian matrix of the mapping
(u;\eta)\mapsto\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)
, for
t
fixed.

For each
t\in J
, the change of variables formula
2
2

2

See, for instance,
[
17
, Theorem 3.2.3]
for the multiplicity version used here.
gives

(4.18)

\int_{E_{\iota,v}^{\star}(t)}\#\mathfrak{N}_{\iota,v}(t;x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{B(0,\delta)}\int_{U_{\iota}(v)}\big|\det J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\big|\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}\eta,

where
\mathfrak{N}_{\iota,v}(t;x)
is the preimage set

\mathfrak{N}_{\iota,v}(t;x):=\big\{(u,\eta)\in U_{\iota}(v)\times B(0,\delta):\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)=x,\,\det J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\neq 0\big\}.

Fix
t\in J
and
x\in E_{\iota,v}^{\star}(t)
and suppose
(u,\eta)\in\mathfrak{N}_{\iota,v}(t;x)
. It follows that
(u,\eta)
is an isolated root of the system of
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}(\,\cdot\,;t;\,\cdot\,)-x
, using the terminology of
[
13
, Definition 5.1]
. Since the maps
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\iota,v}(\,\cdot\,;t;\,\cdot\,)-x
are polynomials of degree
O_{d,\varepsilon}(1)
, by the formulation of Bezout’s theorem in
[
13
, Theorem 5.2]
it follows that

\#\mathfrak{N}_{\iota,v}(t;x)\lesssim_{d,\varepsilon}1\qquad\textrm{for all $x\in E_{\iota,v}^{\star}(t)$.}

Combining these observations with (
4.18
), we obtain the bound

(4.19)

|E_{\iota,v}(t)|\gtrsim_{d,\varepsilon}\int_{B(0,\delta)}\int_{U_{\iota}(v)}\big|\det J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\big|\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}\eta.

We shall bound the right-hand side of (
4.19
) using the sublevel set estimates from either hypothesis
S_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;m;\kappa)
or
S_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;m;\kappa)
.

Step 6: Bounding the Jacobian.
We will show that, up to a relabelling of variables,

(4.20)

|\det J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)|\gtrsim_{\phi}|\det A_{\iota}(t;u,v)|-C_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta|W_{\iota}|^{-(n-1)}

for all
\eta\in B(0,\delta)
and
u\in U_{\iota}(v)
, where
A_{\iota}(t;u,v)
is the matrix defined at the start of Step 5 and
C_{\phi,\varepsilon}>0
is a constant depending only on
\phi
and
\varepsilon
.

Recall the notation for submatrices introduced in §
2.3
. For
\alpha:=\{1,\ldots,n-m\}
and
\beta:=\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, we have

J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{\iota,v}(u;t)+\mathcal{E}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)

where
\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{\iota,v}(u;t)
,
\mathcal{E}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\in\mathrm{Mat}(\mathbb{R},n-1)
with

\widetilde{\mathfrak{J}}_{\iota,v}(u;t):=\begin{bmatrix}[\partial_{z}(\widetilde{\Psi}\circ V_{\iota})(t;u,v)]_{\beta,\alpha}&(\partial_{\omega}\widetilde{\Psi})\circ V_{\iota}(t;u,v)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}0_{(n-m)\times(m-1)}\\
I_{(m-1)\times(m-1)}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}

and
\mathcal{E}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)
has
(i,k)
entry
\big(\mathcal{E}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\big)_{i,k}
given by

\begin{cases}\sum_{j=n-m+1}^{n-1}\partial_{z_{k}}\big((\partial_{\omega_{j}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{i})\circ V_{\iota}\big)(t;u,v)\eta_{j}&\textrm{if $1\leq k\leq n-m$,}\\
0&\textrm{if $n-m+1\leq k\leq n-1$,}\end{cases}

for
1\leq i\leq n-1
. Here
\widetilde{\Psi}_{i}
denotes the
i
th entry of
\widetilde{\Psi}
and
z
represents the
y
variable in Case I) and the
\omega
variable in Case II).

To begin, we observe that

(4.21)

\det J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)=\det\big(\mathfrak{J}_{\iota,v}(u;t)+\mathcal{E}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\big)+O(\delta^{10n})

for all
(u;t;\eta)\in U_{\iota}(v)\times J\times B(0,\delta)
, where

\mathfrak{J}_{\iota,v}(u;t):=\begin{bmatrix}[\partial_{z}(\Psi\circ V_{\iota})(t;u,v)]_{\beta,\alpha}\par&(\partial_{\omega}\Psi)\circ V_{\iota}(t;u,v)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}0_{(n-m)\times(m-1)}\\
I_{(m-1)\times(m-1)}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix}.

Indeed, it is straightforward to see that, in Case I), the image of
\mathfrak{p}
lies in the ball
2\cdot\Omega_{\circ}
concentric to
\Omega_{\circ}
but with twice the radius. From the definition of
\widetilde{\Psi}
and the Lagrange form of the remainder, we have

(4.22)

\begin{cases}|(\partial_{y}\Psi)(\mathfrak{p}(y);t;y)-(\partial_{y}\widetilde{\Psi})(\mathfrak{p}(y);t;y)|<\delta^{10n^{2}}\\
|(\partial_{\omega}\Psi)(\mathfrak{p}(y);t;y)-(\partial_{\omega}\widetilde{\Psi})(\mathfrak{p}(y);t;y)|<\delta^{10n^{2}}\end{cases}\qquad\textrm{for all $(y;t)\in W_{\mathrm{I}}\times J$,}

provided
\delta
is chosen sufficiently small, depending only on
\phi
,
n
and
\varepsilon
. On the other hand,
|\partial_{y}\mathfrak{p}(y)|\lesssim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{-n}
for all
y\in W_{\mathrm{I}}
, which follows from the Jacobian bound (
4.14-I
) and the bound
\|P\|_{C^{K}}\leq 1
guaranteed by Gromov’s lemma. Combining these observations with the chain rule, and applying a completely analogous argument in Case II), we readily obtain (
4.21
).

We further show that

(4.23)

\det J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)=\det\mathfrak{J}_{\iota,v}(u;t)+O_{\phi,\varepsilon}(\delta|W_{\iota}|^{-(n-1)}).

Due to (
4.21
), this holds as long as

\big|\partial_{z_{k}}(\Psi_{i}\circ V_{\iota})(t;z)\big|\lesssim_{\phi}|W_{\iota}|^{-1}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\big|\big(\mathcal{E}_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)\big)_{i,k}\big|\lesssim_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta|W_{\iota}|^{-1}

for each
1\leq i,k\leq n-1
, where
\Psi_{i}
denotes the
i
th component of
\Psi
. We proceed to verify that this is indeed the case.

•

In Case I), using the bounds
\|\partial_{y_{k}}\partial_{\omega_{j}}\widetilde{\Psi}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\phi,\varepsilon}1
and
|\eta|<\delta
, we see that

\big|\partial_{y_{k}}(\Psi_{i}\circ V_{\mathrm{I}})(t;y)\big|\lesssim_{\phi}1+\||\partial_{y_{k}}\mathfrak{p}|\|_{L^{\infty}(\widetilde{B})},\quad\big|\big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{I},v}(u;t;\eta)\big)_{i,k}\big|\lesssim_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta+\delta\||\partial_{y_{k}}\mathfrak{p}|\|_{L^{\infty}(\widetilde{B})}.

To establish (
4.23
), it therefore suffices to show

|\partial_{y_{k}}\mathfrak{p}(y)|\lesssim_{\phi}|\widetilde{D}(\widetilde{B})|^{-1}=|W_{\mathrm{I}}|^{-1}\qquad\textrm{for all $y\in\widetilde{B}$.}

However, since
\|P\|_{C^{K}},\|D\|_{C^{K}}\leq 1
, the entries of the matrix

\big[\det\partial_{y}\widetilde{D}(y)\big]\partial_{y}\mathfrak{p}(y)=(\partial_{y}\widetilde{P})(\widetilde{D}^{-1}(y)){\rm adj}(\partial_{y}\widetilde{D}(y))

have magnitude
O_{\phi}(1)
. Thus, (
4.14-I
) implies the desired estimate.

•

In Case II), using the bounds
\|\partial_{\omega_{k}}\partial_{\omega_{j}}\widetilde{\Psi}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\phi,\varepsilon}1
and
|\eta|<\delta
, we see that

\big|\partial_{\omega_{k}}(\Psi_{i}\circ V_{\mathrm{II}})(t;\omega)\big|\lesssim_{\phi}1+\||\partial_{\omega_{k}}\mathfrak{d}|\|_{L^{\infty}(\widetilde{B})},\;\;\big|\big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{II},v}(u;t;\eta)\big)_{i,k}\big|\lesssim_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta+\delta\||\partial_{\omega_{k}}\mathfrak{d}|\|_{L^{\infty}(\widetilde{B})}.

To conclude the proof of (
4.23
), it therefore suffices to show

|\partial_{\omega_{k}}\mathfrak{d}(\omega)|\lesssim_{\phi}|\widetilde{P}(\widetilde{B})|^{-1}=|W_{\mathrm{II}}|^{-1}\qquad\textrm{for all $\omega\in\widetilde{B}$.}

However, this follows in a completely analogous manner to Case I).

Finally we show that, up to relabelling variables,

|\det\mathfrak{J}_{\iota,v}(u,t)|\sim_{\phi}|\det A_{\iota}(t;u,v)|.

Note that, once proved, this directly implies (
4.20
) when combined with (
4.23
). It is during the proof of this last estimate that the significant differences between Case I) and Case II) manifest.

By differentiating the defining equation
\partial_{y}\phi(\Psi(\omega;t;y),t;y)=\omega
of
\Psi
independently with respect to
\omega
and
y
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\omega}\Psi(\omega;t;y)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{xy}^{2}\phi)^{-1}(\Psi(\omega;t;y),t;y),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\Psi(\omega;t;y)

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{xy}^{2}\phi)^{-1}(\Psi(\omega;t;y),t;y)(\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi)(\Psi(\omega;t;y),t;y).

From this and the chain rule, we readily deduce the following formulæ:

•

In Case I), for
\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;y)=(\Psi\circ V_{\mathrm{I}}(t;y),t;y)
as defined in (
4.1
), we have

\partial_{y}(\Psi\circ V_{\mathrm{I}})(t;y)=(\partial_{xy}^{2}\phi)^{-1}\big(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;y)\big)\big(\mathfrak{P}(y)-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;y)\big)\big).

From this, we deduce that the matrix
\mathfrak{J}_{\iota,v}(u;t)
equals

(\partial^{2}_{xy}\phi)^{-1}\big(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;u,v)\big)\begin{bmatrix}\big[\mathfrak{P}(u,v)-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;u,v)\big)\big]_{\beta,\alpha}&\begin{pmatrix}0_{(n-m)\times(m-1)}\\
I_{(m-1)\times(m-1)}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix},

and therefore

|\det\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}_{\iota,v}(u;t)|\sim_{\phi}|\det\big[\mathfrak{P}(u,v)-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;u,v)\big)\big]_{\alpha,\alpha}|=|\det A_{\mathrm{I}}(t;u,v)|.

Indeed, up to relabelling the variables,
[\mathfrak{P}(u,v)-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon_{\mathfrak{p}}(t;u,v)\big)]_{\alpha,\alpha}
equals
A_{\mathrm{I}}(t;u,v)
.

•

In Case II), for
\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(\omega;t)=(\Psi\circ V_{\mathrm{II}}(t;\omega),t;\mathfrak{d}(\omega))
as defined in (
4.1
), we have

\partial_{\omega}(\Psi\circ V_{\mathrm{II}})(t;\omega)=(\partial_{xy}^{2}\phi)^{-1}\big(\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(\omega;t)\big)\Big(I_{n-1}-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(\omega;t)\big)\mathfrak{D}(\omega)\Big).

We deduce that the matrix
\mathfrak{J}_{\iota,v}(u;t)
equals

(\partial_{xy}^{2}\phi)^{-1}\big(\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(u,v;t)\big)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\big[I_{n-1}-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(u,v;t)\big)\mathfrak{D}(u,v)\big]_{\beta,\alpha}&\begin{pmatrix}0_{(n-m)\times(m-1)}\\
I_{(m-1)\times(m-1)}\end{pmatrix}\end{bmatrix},

and therefore

|\det\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}_{\iota,v}(u;t)|\sim_{\phi}|\det[I_{n-1}-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(u,v;t)\big)\mathfrak{D}(\omega)]_{\alpha,\alpha}|=|\det A_{\mathrm{II}}(t;u,v)|.

Indeed, up to relabelling the variables,
[I_{n-1}-(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)\big(\Upsilon^{\mathfrak{d}}(u,v;t)\big)\mathfrak{D}(u,v)]_{\alpha,\alpha}
equals
A_{\mathrm{II}}(t;u,v)
.

Step 7: Concluding the argument.
Due to (
4.20
), for every
\sigma>2C_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta|W_{\iota}|^{-(n-1)}
we have that

|\det A_{\iota}(t;u,v)|\geq\sigma\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|\det J_{\iota,v}(u;t;\eta)|\gtrsim_{\phi}|\det A_{\iota}(t;u,v)|.

Combining this with (
4.17
) and (
4.19
), we obtain

|N_{C_{\phi}\delta}G\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times J)|\gtrsim_{\phi}\delta^{m-1}\int_{\{(t;u)\in J\times U_{\iota}(v):|\det A_{\iota}(t;u,v)|\geq\sigma\}}|\det A_{\iota}(t;u,v)|\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}u

whenever
\sigma>2C_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta|W_{\iota}|^{-(n-1)}
.

The inequality in the previous display holds for all
v\in\Pi_{m}(W_{\iota})
. Integrating over this set, we deduce that

(4.24)

|N_{C_{\phi}\delta}G\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times J)|\gtrsim_{\phi}\delta^{m-1}\int_{\{(t;z)\in J\times W_{\iota}:|\det A_{\iota}(t;z)|\geq\sigma\}}|\det A_{\iota}(t;z)|\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}z

for all
\sigma>2C_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta|W_{\iota}|^{-(n-1)}
. We now treat Case I and Case II separately.

Case I). For
c_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}>0
a constant, depending only on
\phi
,
d
and
\varepsilon
, take
\sigma:=c_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\lambda^{1/\kappa}|W_{\mathrm{I}}|^{1/\kappa}
. Provided
c_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}
is sufficiently small, by (
4.16-I
) and the fact that
|J|\sim_{d,\varepsilon}\lambda
we deduce that

|\{(t;y)\in J\times W_{\mathrm{I}}:|\det A_{\mathrm{I}}(t;y)|<\sigma\}|\leq\frac{|J\times W_{\mathrm{I}}|}{2}.

Furthermore,
\sigma>2C_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta|W_{\mathrm{I}}|^{-(n-1)}
, as otherwise (
4.15
) implies that

\#\mathbb{T}[J]\lesssim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{(n-1)\kappa+1}}\delta^{-(n-1)+\frac{\kappa}{(n-1)\kappa+1}-\varepsilon/2}\leq\lambda^{-\frac{1}{\kappa+1}}\delta^{-(n-1)+\frac{\kappa}{(n-1)\kappa+1}-\varepsilon/2}

which, provided the constant
\mathbf{C}_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}
in the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\lambda)
in Step 2 is large enough, contradicts the assumption that
J\in\mathcal{J}_{2}(\lambda)
.

Combining the observations of the previous paragraph with (
4.24
), we obtain

|N_{C_{\phi}\delta}G\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times J)|\gtrsim_{\phi}\delta^{m-1}\sigma|J\times W_{\mathrm{I}}|.

By (
4.15
), this further implies that

|N_{C_{\phi}\delta}G\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times J)|\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{m-1}\lambda^{1+1/\kappa}|W_{\mathrm{I}}|^{1+1/\kappa}\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{m-1}(\lambda\delta^{n-1+\varepsilon/2}\#\mathbb{T}[J])^{(\kappa+1)/\kappa}.

Thus, we have precisely the desired bound (
4.10
).

Case II). For
c_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}>0
a constant, depending only on
\phi
,
d
and
\varepsilon
, take
\sigma:=c_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\lambda^{1/\kappa}
. Provided
c_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}
is sufficiently small, by (
4.16-II
) and the fact that
|J|\sim_{d,\varepsilon}\lambda
we deduce that

\sup_{\omega\in\Omega_{\phi}}|\{t\in J:|\det A_{\mathrm{II}}(t;\omega)|<\sigma\}|\leq\frac{|J|}{2}.

Furthermore,
\sigma>2C_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta|W_{\mathrm{II}}|^{-(n-1)}
, as otherwise (
4.15
) implies that

\#\mathbb{T}[J]\lesssim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{(n-1)\kappa}}\delta^{-(n-1)+1/(n-1)-\varepsilon/2}\leq\lambda^{-1/\kappa}\delta^{-(n-1)+1/(n-1)-\varepsilon/2},

which, provided the constant
\mathbf{C}_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}
in the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{1}(\lambda)
is large enough, contradicts the assumption that
J\in\mathcal{J}_{2}(\lambda)
.

Combining the observations of the previous paragraph with (
4.24
), we have

|N_{C_{\phi}\delta}G\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times J)|\gtrsim_{\phi}\delta^{m-1}\sigma|J\times W_{\mathrm{II}}|.

By (
4.15
), this further implies that

|N_{C_{\phi}\delta}G\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times J)|\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{m-1}\lambda^{1+1/\kappa}|W_{\mathrm{II}}|\gtrsim_{\phi,d,\varepsilon}\delta^{m-1}\lambda^{1+1/\kappa}(\delta^{n-1+\varepsilon/2}\#\mathbb{T}[J]).

Thus, we have precisely the desired bound (
4.10
).

Having deduced (
4.10
) in either case, the result now follows from Wongkew’s theorem
[
40
]
, as discussed in Step 2 above.

∎

5.
Uniform sublevel set estimates

5.1.
Sublevel sets and linear combinations of real analytic functions

Given a connected, nonempty open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
C^{\omega}(U)
denote the space of real-valued, real analytic functions on
U
. We say
f\in C^{\omega}(U)
is a
linear combination
of
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in C^{\omega}(U)
if there exist
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

f(u)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}g_{j}(u)\qquad\textrm{for all $u\in U$.}

The key results of this section roughly state that if
f\in C^{\omega}(-1,1)
is
not
a linear combination of
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in C^{\omega}(-1,1)
, then for any fixed choice of
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m}\in\mathbb{C}
the function
t\mapsto f(t)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}g_{j}(t)
can only be small on a small set. The precise statements involve analytic families of functions
f(\,\cdot\,;y)
and
g_{j}(\,\cdot\,;y)
,
1\leq j\leq m
, parametrised by
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Suppose
f\colon(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g_{j}\colon(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, are real analytic and such that
f(\,\cdot\,;y)
is not a linear combination of
g_{1}(\,\cdot\,;y),\dots,g_{m}(\,\cdot\,;y)
for
\mathcal{L}^{N}
-almost every
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
.

Let
I_{\circ}\subseteq(-1,1)
and
Y_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{B}^{N}
be compact sets containing the origin. Then there exist an exponent
\kappa=\kappa_{\mathscr{E}}\in(0,1)
and a constant
C=C_{\mathscr{E}}\geq 1
, which depend only on the ensemble
\mathscr{E}:=\{f,g_{1},\dots,g_{m},I_{\circ},Y_{\circ}\}
, such that sublevel set estimate

(5.1)

\Big|\Big\{(t;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}:\big|f(t;y)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}(y)g_{j}(t;y)\big|<\sigma\Big\}\Big|\leq C\sigma^{\kappa}

holds uniformly over all
\sigma>0
and all choices of measurable functions
\mu_{j}\colon\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq j\leq m
.

We also establish a variant of Proposition
5.1
, which attains a stronger conclusion under stronger hypotheses.

Definition 5.2
.

Suppose
g_{j}\colon(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, are real analytic and
0\leq r\leq m
. Define the infinite matrix

(5.2)

\mathfrak{B}(s;y):=(b_{i,j}(s;y))_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i<\infty\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\qquad\textrm{where}\qquad b_{i,j}(s;y):=\frac{(\partial_{t}^{i}g_{j})(s;y)}{i!}.

We say the tuple
(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})
is
of constant rank
r
if

\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{B}(s;y)=r\qquad\textrm{for all $(s,y)\in(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}$.}

With the above definition, and recalling the notation
P_{d}[f,s]
from §
2
, our second sublevel estimate reads as follows.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
0\leq r\leq m
and suppose
f\colon(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g_{j}\colon(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq j\leq m
, are real analytic such that:

a)

There exists some
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
P_{d}[f(\,\cdot\,;y),s]
is not a linear combination of
P_{d}[g_{1}(\,\cdot\,;y),s],\dots,P_{d}[g_{m}(\,\cdot\,;y),s]
for every
(s,y)\in(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}
;

b)

(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})
is of constant rank
r
.

Let
I_{\circ}\subseteq(-1,1)
and
Y_{\circ}\subset\mathbb{B}^{N}
be compact sets containing the origin. Then there exist an exponent
\kappa=\kappa_{\mathscr{E}}\in(0,1)
and a constant
C=C_{\mathscr{E}}\geq 1
, which depend only on the ensemble
\mathscr{E}:=\{f,g_{1},\dots,g_{m},I_{\circ},Y_{\circ}\}
, such that sublevel set estimate

(5.3)

\Big|\Big\{t\in I_{\circ}:\big|f(t;y)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}g_{j}(t;y)\big|<\sigma\Big\}\Big|\leq C\sigma^{\kappa}

holds uniformly over all
\sigma>0
,
y\in Y_{\circ}
and all scalars
\mu_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq j\leq m
.

Remark 5.4
.

The case
r=0
corresponds to each
g_{i}\equiv 0
and
f(\,\cdot\,;y)\not\equiv 0
for every
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
. In this case, the bound (
5.3
) is the sublevel slice bound for
f
alone which holds under the assumption that
f
is real analytic and
f(\,\cdot\,;y)\not\equiv 0
for every
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
. This is already a non-trivial result and can be proved using the Weierstrass Preparation Theorem; the argument below, in the case
r=0
, gives an alternative proof. The example
f(t;y)=yt
shows the necessity that a) holds for every
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
and not just for a.e.
y
.

Clearly, the uniform slice estimate (
5.3
) from Proposition
5.3
, whenever it holds, is stronger than the averaged estimate (
5.1
) from Proposition
5.1
. However, it is not possible to prove (
5.3
) under hypothesis a) alone.

Example 5.5
.

Consider the case
m=1
,
N=1
and the real analytic functions
f(t;y):=t^{2}
and
g(t;y):=t^{2}y^{2}+ty^{3}
. Then
f
is not a linear combination of
g
. However, there is no uniform sublevel set estimate of the form

\Big|\Big\{t\in I_{\circ}:\big|f(t;y)-\mu g(t;y)\big|<\sigma\Big\}\Big|\leq C\sigma^{\kappa}.

Indeed, for
y\neq 0
, if we simply take
\mu:=y^{-2}
, then matters are reduced to estimating the size of the sublevel set
S_{\sigma}:=\{t\in I_{\circ}:|t||y|<\sigma\}
. However, for
\sigma:=|y|
, it follows that
S_{\sigma}
is the whole interval
I_{\circ}
.

Proposition
5.1
and Proposition
5.3
can be used to relate the hypotheses of Theorem
2.8
to the uniform sublevel set estimates
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi,m;\kappa)
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi,m;\kappa)
from Definition
4.1
. In particular, together they imply Proposition
4.2
.

Proof (of Proposition
4.2
).

Let
n\geq 3
and suppose

\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a real analytic non-degenerate phase.

I) Suppose
\phi
is translation invariant and satisfies Hypothesis I). Our goal is to show
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n);\kappa)
holds for some
0<\kappa\leq 1
. Under the translation invariant hypothesis,
\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi(x,t;y)
is independent of
x
; therefore, the sublevel set condition simplifies significantly to the following statement.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n);\kappa)
:
For all measurable
\mathfrak{P}\colon\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\to\mathrm{Mat}(\mathbb{R},n-1)
, there exist
\alpha
,
\beta\in\mathcal{P}(n-1,d_{\mathrm{crit}}(n))
such that

(5.4)

\Big|\Big\{(t;y)\in I_{\phi}\times Y_{\phi}:\Big|\det\Big[\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi(0,t;y)-\mathfrak{P}(y)\Big]_{\alpha,\beta}\Big|<\sigma\Big\}\Big|\leq C_{\phi}\sigma^{\kappa}

holds uniformly over all
\sigma>0
. Here the constant
C_{\phi}
depends only on
\phi
and, in particular, is independent of
\mathfrak{P}
.

Since
\phi
satisfies Hypothesis I), there exist
\alpha
,
\beta\in\mathcal{P}(n-1,d_{\mathrm{crit}}(n))
such that
\mathcal{L}^{n-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;\alpha,\beta))=0
, where
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;\alpha,\beta)
is the set introduced in Definition
2.6
. Fixing a choice of
\mathfrak{P}
as above and expanding out the determinant in (
5.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle\det[\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi(0,t;y)]_{\alpha,\beta}+

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{\rm crit}(n)}\det[\mathfrak{P}(y)]_{\alpha,\beta}

\displaystyle-\sum_{(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)}\mu_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(y)\det[\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi(0,t;y)]_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}},

where each
\mu_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
is a measurable function, specifically a polynomial in the entries of
\mathfrak{P}
.

Given
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;\alpha,\beta)
, the function
\det[\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi(0,\,\cdot\,;y)]_{\alpha,\beta}
is not a
C^{\omega}
-linear combination of the functions
1
and the
\det[\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi(0,\,\cdot\,;y)]_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
for
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)
(that is, for all
\alpha^{\prime}\subset\alpha
,
\beta^{\prime}\subset\beta
with
0<|\alpha^{\prime}|=|\beta^{\prime}|<d_{\mathrm{crit}}(n)
, according to the notation of Section
2
). Indeed, if this failed for some
y_{0}\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;\alpha,\beta)
, there would exist
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)
, such that

\det[\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi(0,t;y_{0})]_{\alpha,\beta}=\lambda+\sum_{{(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)}}\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\det[\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi(0,t;y_{0})]_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}

holds for all
t\in(-1,1)
. Since
\phi(0,0;y)\equiv 0
in (
2.1
), setting
t=0
, the above implies that
\lambda=0
, and hence that
\det\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi(0,\,\cdot\,;y_{0})
is a linear combination of the functions
\det[\partial^{2}_{yy}\phi(0,\,\cdot\,;y_{0})]_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}
alone, contradicting the fact that
y_{0}\notin\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;\alpha,\beta)
. Therefore, the hypothesis
\mathcal{L}^{n-1}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;\alpha,\beta))=0
is precisely what is required to invoke Proposition
5.1
in order to deduce the existence of some
\kappa=\kappa(\phi)\in(0,1)
for which (
5.4
) holds.

II) Suppose
\phi
satisfies Hypothesis II). Our goal is to show
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n);\kappa)
holds for some
0<\kappa\leq 1
. We recall the statement.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n);\kappa)
:
For all
\mathfrak{D}\colon\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\to\mathrm{Mat}(\mathbb{R},n-1)
and
\mathfrak{d}\colon\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{B}^{n-1}
measurable, there exist
\alpha
,
\beta\in\mathcal{P}(n-1,d_{\mathrm{crit}}(n))
such that

(5.5)

\sup_{\omega\in\Omega_{\phi}}\Big|\Big\{t\in I_{\phi}:\Big|\det\Big[(\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi)(\Psi(\omega;t;\mathfrak{d}(\omega)),t;\mathfrak{d}(\omega))\mathfrak{D}(\omega)-I_{n-1}\Big]_{\alpha,\beta}\Big|<\sigma\Big\}\Big|\leq C_{\phi}\sigma^{\kappa}

holds uniformly over all
\sigma>0
. Here the constant
C_{\phi}
depends only on
\phi
and, in particular, is independent of
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{d}
.

Since
\phi
satisfies Hypothesis II), there exist
\alpha
,
\beta\in\mathcal{P}(n-1,d_{\mathrm{crit}}(n))
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;\alpha,\beta;d)=\emptyset
, where
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;\alpha,\beta;d)
is the set introduced in Definition
2.7
. Fix a choice of
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{d}
as above and
\omega\in\Omega_{\phi}
and set
y:=\mathfrak{d}(\omega)
. As in Definition
2.7
, let

g_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(t;\omega;y):=\det[\partial_{yy}^{2}\phi(\Psi(\omega;t;y),t;y)]_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}

for
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)
. Expanding out the determinant in (
5.5
), we obtain

(-1)^{d_{\rm crit}(n)}+\sum_{(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)}\mu_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(\omega)g_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(t;\omega;y)

where each
\mu_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
is a measurable function which is a certain polynomial in the entries of
\mathfrak{D}
.

By condition a) of Hypothesis II), for any fixed
(\omega;s;y)\in\mathbb{D}^{n}
, the constant function
1
is not a linear combination of the polynomials
P_{d}[g_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(\,\cdot\,;\omega;y),s]
for
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)
. In addition, condition b) of Hypothesis II) is precisely the remaining rank condition required to invoke Proposition
5.3
with
f\equiv 1
. This proposition therefore guarantees the existence of some
\kappa=\kappa(\phi)\in(0,1)
for which (
5.5
) holds.
∎

The remainder of this section deals with the proofs of Proposition
5.1
and Proposition
5.3
. The arguments are postponed until §§
5.4
-
5.5
. Here we discuss some preliminaries on linear independence of functions and elementary sublevel set bounds.

5.2.
Linear independence in the real analytic category

Given a connected, nonempty open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we say
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in C^{\omega}(U)
are
linearly dependent
if there exist
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
not all zero such that

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}g_{j}(u)=0\qquad\textrm{for all $u\in U$.}

Otherwise, we say
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in C^{\omega}(U)
are
linearly independent
.

For a nonempty open interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and
g_{i}\in C^{m-1}(I)
,
1\leq i\leq m
, we define the
Wronskian

W(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})\in C(I)
by

W(g_{1},\ldots,g_{m})(t):=\det\begin{bmatrix}g_{1}(t)&g_{2}(t)&\cdots&g_{m}(t)\\
g_{1}^{\prime}(t)&g_{2}^{\prime}(t)&\cdots&g_{m}^{\prime}(t)\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
g_{1}^{(m-1)}(t)&g_{2}^{(m-1)}(t)&\cdots&g_{m}^{(m-1)}(t)\end{bmatrix}.

We recall the following classical theorem of Bôcher
[
5
]
.

Theorem 5.6

(Bôcher
[
5
]
)
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an open interval and
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in C^{\omega}(I)
. The following are equivalent:

a)

g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
are linearly dependent;

b)

The Wronskian
W(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})
vanishes identically on
I
.

It is trivial to show that if
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in C^{m-1}(I)
are linearly dependent, then
W(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\equiv 0
. However, the converse fails in general, so the hypothesis that the
g_{j}
are real analytic is essential in Bôcher’s theorem.

The following useful lemma relates linear independence to linear independence of the underlying Taylor polynomials in the finite-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{R}_{\leq d}[T]
of real univariate polynomials of degree at most
d
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be a non-trivial open interval and
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in C^{\omega}(I)
. Then the following are equivalent:

a)

The functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
are linearly independent.

b)

Given any compact interval
I_{\circ}\subseteq I
, there exists some
d\geq m
such that the polynomials
P_{d}[g_{1},s],\ldots,P_{d}[g_{m},s]
are linearly independent for all
s\in I_{\circ}
.

c)

Given any compact interval
I_{\circ}\subseteq I
, there exist some
d\geq m
and
s\in I_{\circ}
such that the polynomials
P_{d}[g_{1},s],\ldots,P_{d}[g_{m},s]
are linearly independent.

We emphasise that the choice of
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
in b) is uniform over all
s\in I_{\circ}
.

Proof.

It is clear that b)
\Rightarrow
c), so that it suffices to show a)
\Rightarrow
b) and c)
\Rightarrow
a).

c)
\Rightarrow
a).
Suppose a) fails so that
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
are linearly dependent; that is, there exist scalars
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
, not all zero, such that
\mu_{1}g_{1}+\cdots+\mu_{m}g_{m}\equiv 0
on
I
. Since forming the Taylor polynomial is a linear operation, it follows that
\mu_{1}P_{d}[g_{1},s]+\dots+\mu_{m}P_{d}[g_{m},s]\equiv 0
for all
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and all
s\in I
. This implies that c) fails.

a)
\Rightarrow
b).
Suppose b) fails for some choice of compact interval
I_{\circ}\subseteq I
where, without loss of generality, we assume
I_{\circ}
has positive length. Thus, for each
d\geq m
there exists some
s_{d}\in I_{\circ}
such that the polynomials
P_{d}[g_{1},s_{d}],\ldots,P_{d}[g_{m},s_{d}]
are linearly dependent. By compactness, we can pass to a strictly increasing sequence of integers
(d_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
such that
s_{d_{k}}\to s_{\circ}
as
k\to\infty
for some
s_{\circ}\in I_{\circ}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
d_{1}\geq m
.

Define
P_{j,k}:=P_{d_{k}}[g_{j};s_{d_{k}}]
for each
1\leq j\leq m
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. By the trivial implication in Theorem
5.6
, we have

W(P_{1,k},\dots,P_{m,k})\equiv 0.

We wish to take a limit in
k
to conclude that
W(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\equiv 0
. To do this, we should be slightly careful since the Taylor polynomials
P_{j,k}
are defined with respect to varying centres.

Since the
g_{j}
are real analytic on
I
, there exists some constant
C_{g}\geq 1
such that

\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|g_{j}^{(\ell)}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{\circ})}\leq C_{g}^{\ell+1}\ell!\qquad\textrm{for all $\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$.}

By Taylor’s theorem, for
0\leq\ell\leq m-1
we have

(5.6)

\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|g_{j}^{(\ell)}(t)-P_{j,k}^{(\ell)}(t)|\leq(2C_{g})^{d_{k}+2}|t-s_{d_{k}}|^{d_{k}+1-\ell}\qquad\textrm{for all $t\in I_{\circ}$}

whenever
k
is sufficiently large.

Let
\delta:=1/(8C_{g})>0
and choose
K\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large so that
|s_{d_{k}}-s_{\circ}|<\delta
and (
5.6
) holds for all
k\geq K
. If
t\in I_{\circ}
satisfies
|t-s_{\circ}|<\delta
, then
|t-s_{d_{k}}|\leq 2\delta
for all
k\geq K
and so (
5.6
) implies that

\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|g_{j}^{(\ell)}(t)-P_{j,k}^{(\ell)}(t)|\leq(4C_{g})^{m}2^{-d_{k}}\qquad\textrm{for all $k\geq K$ and $0\leq\ell\leq m-1$.}

In particular, for each
1\leq j\leq m
and
0\leq\ell\leq m-1
, we have
P_{j,k}^{(\ell)}(t)\to g_{j}^{(\ell)}(t)
as
k\to\infty
uniformly over
t\in U
, where
U\subseteq I_{\circ}\cap(s_{\circ}-\delta,s_{\circ}+\delta)
is a choice of non-trivial open interval. Plugging this into the definition of the Wronskian, we see that

W(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\equiv 0\ \ \textrm{on $U$}\ \ \textrm{and hence}\ \ W(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\equiv 0\ \ \textrm{on $I$}

since
W(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})
is analytic.
We now apply Theorem
5.6
to conclude that the functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
are linearly dependent.
∎

It is useful to reformulate Lemma
5.7
in terms of matrices.

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be a non-trivial open interval and
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in C^{\omega}(I)
. Let
b_{i,j}(s):=g_{j}^{(i)}(s)/i!
denote the coefficients of the power series expansion of
g_{j}
centred at
s\in I
and

B_{\leq d}(s):=(b_{i,j}(s))_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq d\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\in\mathrm{Mat}(\mathbb{R},(d+1)\times m).

Then the following are equivalent:

a)

The functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
are linearly independent.

b)

Given any compact interval
I_{\circ}\subseteq I
, there exists some
d\geq m
with the following property. For every
s\in I_{\circ}
there exists an
m\times m
minor
\mathfrak{M}_{B}(s)
of
B_{\leq d}(s)
with non-vanishing determinant.

c)

Given any compact interval
I_{\circ}\subseteq I
, there exist some
d\geq m
and
s\in I_{\circ}
with the following property. There exists an
m\times m
minor
\mathfrak{M}_{B}(s)
of
B_{\leq d}(s)
with non-vanishing determinant.

Proof.

This follows directly from Lemma
5.7
, since the polynomials
P_{d}[g_{j},s]
are linearly independent in the vector space
\mathbb{R}_{\leq d}[T]
if and only if their coefficient column vectors
(b_{i,j}(s))_{i=0}^{d}
are linearly independent as vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
.
∎

5.3.
Elementary sublevel set estimates

A key ingredient in the proof of Proposition
5.1
is the following elementary inequality, which is valid in the
C^{k}
category.

Lemma 5.9

(van der Corput estimate)
.

For all
k\geq 1
there exists a constant
C_{k}
such that the following holds. If
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is an open interval and
u\in C^{k}(I)
satisfies
u^{(k)}(t)\geq 1
for all
t\in I
, then

|\{t\in I:|u(t)|<\sigma\}|\leq C_{k}\sigma^{1/k}\qquad\textrm{for all $\sigma>0$.}

The above bound holds for
k=0
(so that
u(t)\geq 1
on
I
) when
\sigma<1
. In this case, the left-hand side and right-hand side are both equal to zero.

The proof of Lemma
5.9
is implicit in the proof of the oscillatory integral estimate in
[
36
, Proposition 2, p. 332]
(see also
[
14
, Lemma 3.3]
for an explicit treatment).

The significance of Lemma
5.9
is that it provides a
uniform
estimate over all choices of interval
I
and all choices of function
u
satisfying
u^{(k)}(t)\geq 1
. This uniformity will play an essential role later in the proof of Proposition
5.1
. For now, we note that Lemma
5.9
readily implies the following non-uniform bound for analytic functions of several variables.

Corollary 5.10
.

Suppose
f\in C^{\omega}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is not identically zero and let
Y\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{n}
be a compact, convex set. Then there exists an exponent
\kappa=\kappa_{f,Y}\in(0,1)
and
C=C_{f,Y}\geq 1
such that

(5.7)

|\{y\in Y:|f(y)|<\sigma\}|\leq C\sigma^{\kappa}\qquad\textrm{for all $\sigma>0$.}

Note, in contrast with Lemma
5.9
, here the exponent
\kappa
and constant
C
are non-uniform, in the sense that they are allowed to depend on
f
and
Y
.

Proof (of Corollary
5.10
).

Since
f
is not identically zero, by a compactness argument we can find a finite family of open balls
\{B_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
and multiindices
(\alpha_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
such that

Y\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in\mathcal{J}}B_{j},\quad B_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{n}\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\partial^{\alpha_{j}}_{y}f(y)|\geq c>0\quad\textrm{for all $y\in B_{j}$ and all $j\in\mathcal{J}$.}

Here the constant
c>0
depends on
f
and
Y
. We may now apply a slicing argument on each ball
B_{j}
to deduce that

(5.8)

|\{y\in B_{j}:|f(y)|<\sigma\}|\lesssim_{f,Y}\sigma^{1/|\alpha_{j}|}\qquad\textrm{for all $\sigma>0$ and $j\in\mathcal{J}$.}

Indeed, (
5.8
) follows by applying Lemma
5.9
in one variable (after a suitable rotation of coordinates), as in
[
14
, Lemma 3.4]
. Summing over the finite indexing set
\mathcal{J}
and choosing
\kappa
to be the minimum of the exponents
1/|\alpha_{j}|
concludes the proof.
∎

By forming the intersection of the sublevel sets in (
5.7
) over all
\sigma>0
, we deduce the following well-known result.

Lemma 5.11
.

If
f\in C^{\omega}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is not identically zero, then the zero set
Z(f):=\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:f(x)=0\}
has
\mathcal{L}^{n}
-measure zero.

Finally, in order to apply Lemma
5.9
, we shall make use of the following elementary inequality relating the size of the coefficients of a polynomial to pointwise lower bounds for its derivatives.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
P\in\mathbb{R}_{\leq d}[T]
be a real polynomial of degree
d
and
s\in[-1,1]
. There exists some
0\leq k\leq d
such that

|P^{(k)}(t)|\gtrsim_{d}\big(\sum_{i=0}^{d}|P^{(i)}(s)|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\qquad\textrm{for all $t\in[-1,1]$.}

Here the implied constant depends only on the degree of
P
and, in particular, not on the size of the coefficients.

Lemma
5.12
can be proved by pigeonholing in the size of the terms of the Taylor expansion of
P
around
s
. Alternatively, the result follows from the last displayed equation in
[
11
, p.124]
and equivalence of norms on the finite-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{R}_{\leq d}[T]
.

5.4.
Proof of Proposition
5.1

With the above preliminaries in hand, we can turn to the proof of the first main result of this section.

Proof (of Proposition
5.1
).

Throughout the argument, we shall say an object is
\mathscr{E}
-admissible
if it depends only on
\mathscr{E}=\{f,g_{1},\dots,g_{m},I_{\circ},Y_{\circ}\}
. Fix measurable functions
\mu_{i}\colon\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
,
1\leq i\leq m
, and define

F_{\mu}\colon(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad F_{\mu}(t;y):=f(t;y)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}(y)g_{i}(t;y).

Our goal is to show that there exists an
\mathscr{E}
-admissible exponent
\kappa\in(0,1)
such that

|E_{\mu}(\sigma)|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{\kappa}\quad\textrm{for all $\sigma>0$,}\quad\textrm{where}\quad E_{\mu}(\sigma):=\{(t;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}:|F_{\mu}(t;y)|<\sigma\}.

Clearly we may assume
0<\sigma<1
. We carry out the argument in steps.

Step 1: Power series expansion and basic definitions.
Since the functions
f
and
g_{j}
are real analytic, given
s\in(-1,1)
we may write

(5.9)

f(t;y)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}(s;y)(t-s)^{i}\quad\textrm{and}\quad g_{j}(t;y)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}b_{i,j}(s;y)(t-s)^{i}

for
1\leq j\leq m
and all
t\in(-1,1)
belonging to a suitable neighbourhood of
s
. Note that the coefficient functions
a_{i}(s;\,\cdot\,)
,
b_{i,j}(s;\,\cdot\,)
are real analytic functions on
\mathbb{B}^{N}
. For any index set
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\{1,\dots,m\}
, let
B_{\mathcal{J}}
denote the matrix-valued function on
I\times\mathbb{B}^{N}
given by

B_{\mathcal{J}}(s;y):=\big(b_{i,j}(s;y)\big)_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i<\infty\\
j\in\mathcal{J}\end{subarray}}\qquad\textrm{for $y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}$}.

If
\mathcal{J}=\{1,\dots,m\}
, then, as in (
5.2
), we write
\mathfrak{B}
for
B_{\mathcal{J}}
.

There exists a maximal value of
0\leq r\leq m
with the following property. For some
y_{\star}\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
there exists a choice of index set
\mathcal{J}(y_{\star})\subseteq\{1,\dots,m\}
with
\#\mathcal{J}(y_{\star})=r
such that the set of functions

\big\{g_{j}(\,\cdot\,;y_{\star}):j\in\mathcal{J}(y_{\star})\big\}

is linearly independent. The case
r=0
corresponds to each
g_{j}\equiv 0
in which case we conclude
f\not\equiv 0
by hypothesis and therefore the sublevel set bound (
5.1
) in Proposition
5.1
follows from Corollary
5.10
. Hence we may assume
r\geq 1
.

Fix
r
and
y_{\star}
with these properties and, by relabelling if necessary, assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{J}(y_{\star})=\{1,\dots,r\}
. Let

\mathcal{G}(y):=\big\{g_{j}(\,\cdot\,;y):1\leq j\leq r\big\}\qquad\textrm{for all $y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}$}

and
B(s;y):=B_{\mathcal{J}(y_{\star})}(s;y)
.

Apply Corollary
5.8
to the collection of functions
\mathcal{G}(y_{\star})
, the interval
I_{\circ}
and
s=0
(see part b). This guarantees the existence of an
r\times r
minor
\mathfrak{M}_{B}(\,\cdot\,)
of
B(0;\,\cdot\,)
such that
\mathfrak{M}_{B}(y_{\star})
has non-vanishing determinant. We emphasise that the choice of rows forming the minor
\mathfrak{M}_{B}(y)
is independent of
y
.

The determinant
\det\mathfrak{M}_{B}(\,\cdot\,)
is an analytic function on
\mathbb{B}^{N}
. Since this function does not vanish identically, by Lemma
5.11
its zero set is of
\mathcal{L}^{N}
measure zero. Combining this with Corollary
5.8
, we have

(5.10)

\mathcal{L}^{N}(Y_{\mathrm{dep}})=0\quad\textrm{for}\quad Y_{\mathrm{dep}}:=\big\{y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}:\mathcal{G}(y)\textrm{ is a linearly dependent set}\big\}.

By the maximality of
r
, for any
r+1\leq k\leq m
and any
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}
, the function
g_{k}(\,\cdot\,;y)
is a linear combination of the functions belonging to
\mathcal{G}(y)
. We may therefore find an
\mathrm{Mat}(\mathbb{R},r\times m)
-valued function
M
defined on
\mathbb{B}^{N}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}
and with real analytic entries such that

(5.11)

\mathfrak{B}(s;y)=B(s;y)M(y)\qquad\textrm{for all $y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}$ and all $s\in(-1,1)$.}

Indeed, by the above discussion, there exist functions
c_{j}^{k}\colon\mathbb{B}^{N}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

g_{k}(t;y)=c_{1}^{k}(y)g_{1}(t;y)+\cdots+c_{r}^{k}(y)g_{r}(t;y)\quad\textrm{for all $r+1\leq k\leq m$, $y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}$.}

Thus, by writing both sides of the above identity in terms of the power series (
5.9
), we see that
M(y)
can be expressed in block form as

M(y)=\begin{bmatrix}I_{r\times r}&\mathfrak{C}(y)\end{bmatrix}\qquad\textrm{where}\qquad\mathfrak{C}(y):=\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}^{r+1}(y)&\cdots&c_{1}^{m}(y)\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
c_{r}^{r+1}(y)&\cdots&c_{r}^{m}(y)\end{bmatrix},

for
I_{r\times r}
the
r\times r
identity matrix.

Having obtained an expression for
M
, it remains to show that the entries are real analytic. Fix
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}
and take
s=0
in Corollary
5.8
part b) to find an
r\times r
minor
\mathfrak{M}(y)
of
B(0;y)
with nonzero determinant. Therefore
\mathfrak{G}(y):=\mathfrak{M}(y)\mathfrak{C}(y)
is an
r\times(m-r)
submatrix of
\mathfrak{B}(0;y)
. The entries of
\mathfrak{G}(y)
consist of various derivatives of
g_{k}
for
r+1\leq k\leq m
and hence are analytic in
y
. The fact that the determinant of
\mathfrak{M}(y)
is not zero (and hence nonzero in a neighbourhood of
y
) implies by Cramer’s rule that the entries of the inverse of
\mathfrak{M}
are analytic in a neighbourhood of
y
. This shows the entries of
\mathfrak{C}
, and therefore the entries of
M
, are analytic near
y
.

For each fixed
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
, the function
F_{\mu}(\,\cdot\,;y)
is real analytic and so for each
s\in(-1,1)
can be expressed in terms of a power series

(5.12)

F_{\mu}(t;y)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{\mu,i}(s;y)(t-s)^{i}

for all
t\in(-1,1)
belonging to a suitable neighbourhood of
s
. Here the functions
c_{\mu,i}(s;\,\cdot\,)\colon\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
are merely measurable. We relate the vector-valued function
\vec{c}_{\mu}:=(c_{\mu,i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
to the coefficients
a_{i}
and
b_{i,j}
using the augmented matrix

A(s;y):=\begin{bmatrix}a_{0}(s;y)&b_{0,1}(s;y)&\cdots&b_{0,r}(y)\\
a_{1}(s;y)&b_{1,1}(s;y)&\cdots&b_{1,r}(s;y)\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\end{bmatrix}\qquad\textrm{for $y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}$.}

In particular, writing
\vec{\mu}:=(\mu_{j})_{j=1}^{m}
, by (
5.11
) we have the pointwise identity

(5.13)

\vec{c}_{\mu}(s;y)=A(s;y)W_{\mu}(y)\qquad\textrm{where}\qquad W_{\mu}(y):=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-M(y)\vec{\mu}(y)\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{r+1}

for all
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}
. In the analysis below, we will consider various
(r+1)\times(r+1)
minors
\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}}(s,y)
of the augmented matrix
A(s,y)
. These minors are of the form

\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}}(s;y)=\begin{bmatrix}a_{d_{1}}(s;y)&b_{d_{1},1}(s;y)&\cdots&b_{d_{1},r}(s;y)\\
a_{d_{2}}(s;y)&b_{d_{2},1}(s;y)&\cdots&b_{d_{2},r}(s;y)\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
a_{d_{r+1}}(s;y)&b_{d_{r+1},1}(s;y)&\cdots&b_{d_{r+1},r}(s;y)\end{bmatrix}

where the indices
{\underline{d}}=(d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{r+1})
satisfy
0\leq d_{1}<d_{2}<\cdots<d_{r+1}
.

Step 2: Removing the singular set.
In light of (
5.10
) and the hypotheses of the proposition, we may find some
y_{\circ}\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
such that the following properties hold:

i)

\mathcal{G}(y_{\circ})
is a linearly independent set of functions;

ii)

f(\,\cdot\,;y_{\circ})
does not lie in the linear span of
\mathcal{G}(y_{\circ})
.

Indeed, i) and ii) will in fact hold for
\mathcal{L}^{N}
almost every choice of
y_{\circ}\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
. Properties i) and ii) ensure that the set
\{f(\,\cdot\,;y_{\circ})\}\cup\mathcal{G}(y_{\circ})
is linearly independent. Thus, Corollary
5.8
implies that there exists some admissible
d\geq r+1\geq 2
(recall we are assuming
r\geq 1
) such that the following holds. For all
s\in I_{\circ}
, there is some choice of integer indices
{\underline{d}}(s)=(d_{1}(s),\ldots,d_{r+1}(s))

satisfying
0\leq d_{1}(s)<d_{2}(s)<\cdots<d_{r+1}(s)\leq d
and some
(r+1)\times(r+1)
minor
\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s,\,\cdot\,)
of the augmented matrix
A(s,\,\cdot\,)

of the form described above whose determinant does not vanish identically as a function of
y
(in particular, it is non-zero at
y=y_{\circ}
). We emphasise that, whilst the row indices
d_{j}=d_{j}(s)
may depend on
s
, the upper bound
d
for these indices is uniform over all
s\in I_{\circ}
.

Given
0<\sigma<1
and
s\in(-1,1)
, consider the
singular set

(5.14)

Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma):=\big\{y\in Y_{\circ}:|\det\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)|<\sigma^{1/2d}\big\}\cup Y_{\mathrm{dep}}.

We first note that the measure of
Y_{\mathrm{dep}}
is zero by (
5.10
). For the sublevel set, we
would like for this to be uniformly (in
s
) small with respect to
\sigma
.
However, the following example shows this is not possible.

Example 5.13
.

Consider the example
f(t;y):=t^{2}
and
g(t;y):=yt
where
N=1
and
m=r=1
. The choice of integer indices
{\underline{d}}(s)=(d_{1}(s),d_{2}(s))=(0,1)
for all
s\in[-1/2,1/2]\setminus\{0\}
has the property that the
2\times 2
minor
\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)
has determinant
-s^{2}y
, which does not vanish identically as a function of
y
. Moreover, for all
\sigma\ll 1
, we have

\sup_{s\in[-1/2,1/2]}\,|Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)|\simeq_{\mathscr{E}}1,

which is
not
small as
\sigma\to 0
.

Nevertheless, we will show that the measure of
Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)
is small outside a small set in
s
. In fact,
we claim there is an
exponent
\kappa_{1}\in(0,1)
depending only on the
\mathscr{E}
-admissible data and a set
E\subseteq I_{\circ}
with measure
|E|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{\kappa_{1}}

such that

(5.15)

\sup_{s\in I_{\circ}\setminus E}|Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\ \sigma^{\kappa_{1}}.

To see this, let

\mathcal{D}:=\bigl\{{\underline{d}}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{r+1}):0\leq d_{1}<\cdots<d_{r+1}\leq d\ \ {\rm and}\ \det\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}}\not\equiv 0\bigr\}

and note that by our choice of integer indices above,

\{{\underline{d}}(s):s\in I_{\circ}\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.
For each
{\underline{d}}\in\mathcal{D}
, the map
(s;y)\mapsto\det\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}}(s;y)
is a nonzero real analytic function and so Corollary
5.10
implies there is an exponent
\rho_{{\underline{d}}}\in(0,1)
depending only on the
\mathscr{E}
-admissible data such that

|\{(s,y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}:|{\rm det}\,\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}}(s;y)|<\sigma^{1/2d}\}|\ \lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\ \sigma^{\rho_{{\underline{d}}}}.

Furthermore, note that

\displaystyle\big\{(s,y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}:|\det\,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)|<\sigma^{1/2d}\big\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{{\underline{d}}\in\mathcal{D}}\bigl\{(s,y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}:|{\rm det}\,\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}}(s;y)|<\sigma^{1/2d}\bigr\}

and so

\bigl|\bigl\{(s,y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}:|\det\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)|<\sigma^{1/2d}\bigr\}\bigr|\ \lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\ \sigma^{2\kappa_{1}}

where
\kappa_{1}:=(1/2)\min_{{\underline{d}}\in\mathcal{D}}\rho_{{\underline{d}}}
(recall
\sigma<1
).
Let

E:=\{s\in I_{\circ}:|Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)|>C\sigma^{\kappa_{1}}\}.

Then

\displaystyle\sigma^{2\kappa_{1}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}\bigl|\big\{(s,y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}:|\det\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)|<\sigma^{1/2d}\big\}\bigr|

\displaystyle\geq\int_{E}|Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)|\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\geq C\sigma^{\kappa_{1}}|E|,

which implies
|E|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{\kappa_{1}}

and (
5.15
) holds for the above choice of
E
, as claimed.

Recall the set
E_{\mu}(\sigma)
defined right above Step 1. We split
E_{\mu}(\sigma)=E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)\cup E^{2}_{\mu}(\sigma)
, where
E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)=\{(t,y)\in E_{\mu}(\sigma):t\in I_{\circ}\setminus E)\}
, so that

|E^{2}_{\mu}(\sigma)|\leq|E||Y_{\circ}|\ \lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\ \sigma^{\kappa_{1}}.

It remains to treat
E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)
.

For each
s\in(-1,1)
, we contain the sublevel set
E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)
within the disjoint union

(5.16)

E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)\subseteq E_{\mu,\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)\cup E_{\mu,\mathrm{main}}(s;\sigma)

where

(5.17)

\displaystyle E_{\mu,\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)

\displaystyle:=\{(t;y)\in(I_{\circ}\setminus E)\times Y_{\circ}:y\in Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)\},

\displaystyle E_{\mu,\mathrm{main}}(s;\sigma)

\displaystyle:=E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)\setminus E_{\mu,\mathrm{sing}}(\sigma).

Below, we shall use (
5.15
) to give a suitable bound for
|E_{\mu,\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)|
. It then remains to estimate the main contribution
|E_{\mu,\mathrm{main}}(s;\sigma)|
.

Step 3: Bounds for the Taylor polynomial.
Fix
s\in I_{\circ}
and for
y\in\mathbb{B}^{N}
, let
P_{\mu}(\,\cdot\,;s;y)
denote the order
d
Taylor polynomial of
F_{\mu}(\,\cdot\,;y)
centred at
s
. Using the notation from (
5.12
), we have

P_{\mu}(t;s;y)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}c_{\mu,i}(s;y)(t-s)^{i}.

Writing
\tilde{c}_{\mu,\ell}:=c_{\mu,d_{\ell}(s)}
for
1\leq\ell\leq r+1
, we claim that

(5.18)

\big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}|\tilde{c}_{\mu,\ell}(s;y)|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}|\det\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)|\big(1+|M(y)\vec{\mu}|\big)\quad\textrm{for all $y\in Y_{\circ}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}$.}

Once this is established, Lemma
5.12
implies that for each
y\in Y_{\circ}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{dep}}
there exists some
0\leq k=k_{s,y}\leq d
such that

|\partial_{t}^{k}P_{\mu}(t;s;y)|\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}|\det\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)|\big(1+|M(y)\vec{\mu}|\big)\qquad\textrm{for all $t\in I_{\circ}$.}

In particular, recalling the definition of
Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma)
from (
5.14
), this implies that

(5.19)

|\partial_{t}^{k}P_{\mu}(t;s;y)|\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{1/2d}\big(1+|M(y)\vec{\mu}|\big),\quad t\in I_{\circ},\,y\in Y_{\circ}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s;\sigma).

Below we shall use this derivative bound, in conjunction with Lemma
5.9
, to obtain favourable sublevel set estimates.

Turning to the proof of (
5.18
), it follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)
that
\tilde{c}_{\mu}(s;y)=\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)W_{\mu}(y)
, where
\tilde{c}_{\mu}=(\tilde{c}_{\mu,\ell})_{\ell=1}^{r+1}
. Hence

W_{\mu}(y)=[\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)]^{-1}\tilde{c}_{\mu}(s;y)\ \ {\rm and\ so}\ \ |\det\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)||W_{\mu}(y)|\lesssim_{\mathcal{E}}|\tilde{c}_{\mu}(s;y)|

by Cramer’s rule and noting that the entries of
\mathfrak{M}_{A,{\underline{d}}(s)}(s;y)
are bounded by a constant depending only on
\mathcal{E}
. This clearly implies the claimed estimate (
5.18
).

Step 4: Controlling the remainder.
We now turn to controlling the remainder term

R_{\mu}(t;s;y):=F_{\mu}(t;y)-P_{\mu}(t;s;y)=\sum_{i=d+1}^{\infty}c_{\mu,i}(s;y)(t-s)^{i}.

In particular, we seek upper bounds for the derivatives
\partial_{t}^{k}R_{\mu}(t;s;y)
for
0\leq k\leq d
.

In light of (
5.13
), the coefficient
c_{\mu,i}(s;y)
corresponds precisely to the
i
th entry of
A(s;y)W_{\mu}(y)
. If we write

\Sigma_{d,k}(s;y):=\sum_{i=d+1}^{\infty}\Big(|a_{i}(s;y)|+\sum_{j=1}^{r}|b_{i,j}(s;y)|\Big)k!\binom{i}{k}|t-s|^{i-k},

then it follows from Cauchy–Schwarz and the embedding
\ell^{1}\hookrightarrow\ell^{2}
that

|\partial^{k}_{t}R_{\mu}(t;s;y)|\leq|W_{\mu}(y)|\Sigma_{d,k}(s;y).

Consequently, there exists some admissible constant
c_{\mathscr{E}}>0
such that

(5.20)

|\partial^{k}_{t}R_{\mu}(t;s;y)|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\big(1+|M(y)\vec{\mu}|\big)|t-s|\qquad\textrm{for $|t-s|<c_{\mathscr{E}}$ and $0\leq k\leq d$,}

where the inequality holds with an
\mathscr{E}
-admissible constant uniformly over all
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
. Indeed, by a standard characterisation of real analytic functions of several variables (see, for example,
[
27
, p.24, p.281]
), there exists some admissible
C_{\mathscr{E}}\geq 1
such that

|a_{i}(s;y)|+\sum_{j=1}^{r}|b_{i,j}(s;y)|\leq C_{\mathscr{E}}^{i+1}\qquad\textrm{for all $(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}$ and all $i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$}

and so

\Sigma_{d,k}(s;y)\leq C_{\mathscr{E}}^{k+2}\Big(\sum_{i=d+1}^{\infty}\big(C_{\mathscr{E}}|t-s|\big)^{i-k-1}i^{k}\Big)|t-s|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}|t-s|

whenever
|t-s|\leq c_{\mathscr{E}}:=1/(2C_{\mathscr{E}})
.

Step 5: Concluding the argument.

Let
\mathfrak{J}=\{J\}
be a decomposition of
I_{\circ}
into essentially disjoint intervals of equal length (we shall fix the common length later in the argument) and denote by
\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}

the set of
J\in\mathfrak{J}
such that
J\cap(I_{\circ}\setminus E)\neq\emptyset
. For each
J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}
, choose some
s_{J}\in J\cap(I_{\circ}\setminus E)
. Applying the partition (
5.16
) for each
s_{J}
, we may write

\displaystyle|E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}}|E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)\cap(J\times\mathbb{B}^{N})|

(5.21)

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}}|E_{\mu,\mathrm{main}}(s_{J};\sigma)\cap(J\times\mathbb{B}^{N})|+\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}}|E_{\mu,\mathrm{sing}}(s_{J};\sigma)\cap(J\times\mathbb{B}^{N})|.

Recalling the definition from (
5.17
) and the bound (
5.15
), we have

\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}}|E_{\mu,\mathrm{sing}}(s_{J};\sigma)\cap(J\times\mathbb{B}^{N})|\lesssim\sigma^{\kappa_{1}}\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}}|J|\lesssim\sigma^{\kappa_{1}}.

Combining this with (
5.21
), we obtain

(5.22)

|E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)|\lesssim\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}}|E_{\mu,\mathrm{main}}(s_{J};\sigma)\cap(J\times\mathbb{B}^{N})|+\sigma^{\kappa_{1}}.

It remains to estimate the main term on the right-hand side of (
5.22
). Fixing
J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}
, we apply Fubini’s theorem to deduce that

(5.23)

|E_{\mu,\mathrm{main}}(s_{J};\sigma)\cap(J\times\mathbb{B}^{N})|\leq\int_{Y_{\circ}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s_{J};\sigma)}|\{t\in J:|F_{\mu}(t;y)|<\sigma\}|\,\mathrm{d}y.

Combining (
5.19
) with (
5.20
), there exists some
\mathscr{E}
-admissible
c_{0}>0
with the following property: for all
y\in Y_{\circ}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s_{J};\sigma)
there exists some
0\leq k_{J,y}\leq d
such that

|\partial_{t}^{k_{J,y}}F_{\mu}(t;y)|\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{1/2d}\big(1+|M(y)\vec{\mu}|\big)\gtrsim\sigma^{1/2d}\quad\textrm{for $|t-s_{J}|<2c_{0}\sigma^{1/2d}$.}

The van der Corput sublevel set estimate from Lemma
5.9
implies that

(5.24)

|\{t\in I:|F_{\mu}(t;y)|<\sigma\textrm{ and }|t-s_{J}|<2c_{0}\sigma^{1/2d}\}|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{1/d-1/2d^{2}}

holds for each fixed
y\in Y_{\circ}\setminus Y_{\mathrm{sing}}(s_{J};\sigma)
. We remark that the uniformity in the univariate van der Corput bound ensures that the above inequality holds with an
\mathscr{E}
-admissible implied constant.

Since
d\geq 2
and we are free to assume
0<\sigma<1
, we may bound
\sigma^{1/d-1/2d^{2}}<\sigma^{3/4d}
. Thus, if we choose the
J\in\mathfrak{J}
to have length
c_{0}\sigma^{1/2d}
and apply (
5.24
) on each interval, we deduce that

(5.25)

\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{J}^{\prime}}|\{t\in J:|F_{\mu}(t;y)|<\sigma\}|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{\kappa_{2}}\qquad\textrm{for $\kappa_{2}:=\frac{1}{4d}$.}

Here we have used the fact that
\#\mathfrak{J}=O_{\mathscr{E}}(\sigma^{-1/2d})
. We may now combine (
5.22
), (
5.23
) and (
5.25
) to obtain

|E^{1}_{\mu}(\sigma)|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{\kappa},\qquad\textrm{where $\kappa:=\min\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}\in(0,1)$}

is an
\mathscr{E}
-admissible exponent. This concludes the proof.
∎

5.5.
Proof of Proposition
5.3

A variant of the argument used in the previous subsection also yields Proposition
5.3
.

Proof (of Proposition
5.3
).

As in the proof of Proposition
5.1
, we will say an object is
\mathscr{E}
-admissible
if it depends only on
\mathscr{E}:=\{f,g_{1},\dots,g_{m},I_{\circ},Y_{\circ}\}
. As before, we define

F_{\mu}\colon(-1,1)\times\mathbb{B}^{N}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad F_{\mu}(t;y):=f(t;y)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}g_{j}(t;y)

and write

\displaystyle f(t;y)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}(s;y)(t-s)^{i},\quad g_{j}(t;y)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}b_{i,j}(s;y)(t-s)^{i},

\displaystyle F_{\mu}(t;y)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{\mu,i}(s;y)(t-s)^{i}.

By the hypotheses of the proposition, we can find some
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

(5.26)

P_{d}[f(\,\cdot\,;y),s]\textrm{ is not a linear combination of }P_{d}[g_{j}(\,\cdot\,;y),s],\,1\leq j\leq m,

for all
(s,y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
. By a simple compactness argument, after possibly increasing the value of
d
(whilst still keeping this quantity admissible), we may assume without loss of generality that the matrix

B_{\leq d}(s;y):=(b_{i,j}(s;y))_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq d\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}

satisfies
\mathrm{rank}\,B_{\leq d}(s;y)=\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{B}(s;y)=r
for all
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
, where the matrix
\mathfrak{B}
is as defined in (
5.2
). If we let
\vec{a}(s;y):=(a_{i}(s;y))_{i=0}^{d}
,
\vec{c}_{\mu}(s;y):=(c_{\mu,i}(s;y))_{i=0}^{d}
and
\vec{\mu}:=(\mu_{j})_{j=1}^{m}
, then it follows from the definitions that

(5.27)

\vec{c}_{\mu}(s;y)=\vec{a}(s;y)-B_{\leq d}(s;y)\vec{\mu}.

We can reinterpret (
5.26
) as the statement that the vector
\vec{a}(s;y)
lies outside the
r
-dimensional linear subspace
\Pi(s;y)
given by the image of the matrix
B_{\leq d}(s;y)
. In particular, we have

|\vec{c}_{\mu}(s;y)|\geq\mathrm{dist}\big(\vec{a}(s;y),\Pi(s;y)\big)=:\rho(s;y)>0

for all
\vec{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and all
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
. By continuity and compactness,

(5.28)

|\vec{c}_{\mu}(s;y)|\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}1\qquad\textrm{for all $(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}$.}

Indeed, using the rank hypothesis, it is a simple matter to check that the distance function
\rho(s;y)
is continuous in
(s;y)
, from which (
5.28
) follows.

On the other hand, since
B_{\leq d}(s;y)
has rank
r
, it follows that the kernel
N(s;y):=\ker B_{\leq d}(s;y)
has dimension
m-r
. Let
V(s;y):=N(s;y)^{\perp}
denote the orthogonal complement of the kernel in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. It is straightforward consequence of the singular value decomposition of the matrix
B_{\leq d}(s;y)
that

|B_{\leq d}(s;y)\vec{\mu}|\geq\sigma_{\min}(s;y)|\vec{\mu}|\qquad\textrm{for all $\vec{\mu}\in V(s;y)$,}

where
\sigma_{\min}(s;y)
denotes the smallest non-zero singular value of
B_{\leq d}(s;y)
. Moreover, if
\vec{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then the orthogonal projection
\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;y)}\vec{\mu}
of
\vec{\mu}
onto
V(s;y)
satisfies
B_{\leq d}(s;y)\vec{\mu}=B_{\leq d}(s;y)\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;y)}\vec{\mu}
. Thus, the previous display implies

|B_{\leq d}(s;y)\vec{\mu}|\geq\sigma_{\min}(s;y)|\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;y)}\vec{\mu}|\qquad\textrm{for all $\vec{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$.}

Furthermore, the constant rank hypothesis implies that
\sigma_{\min}(s;y)=\sigma_{r}(s;y)
where
\sigma_{r}(s;y)>0
denotes the
r
th largest singular value of
B(s;y)
, which is always non-zero. As a simple consequence of the min-max representation of the singular values,
\sigma_{r}(s;y)
is a continuous function of
(s;y)
. Consequently, by compactness,

|B_{\leq d}(s;y)\vec{\mu}|\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}|\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;y)}\vec{\mu}|\qquad\textrm{for all $(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}$ and all $\vec{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}$.}

Note that the supremum of
|\vec{a}(s;y)|
over all
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
is an
\mathscr{E}
-admissible quantity. Thus, in light of (
5.27
) and (
5.28
), we therefore obtain the bound

(5.29)

|\vec{c}_{\mu}(s;y)|\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}1+|\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;y)}\vec{\mu}|\qquad\textrm{for all $(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}$.}

By combining (
5.29
) with Lemma
5.12
, for each
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
there exists some
k=k(s;y)
satisfying
0\leq k\leq d
such that

(5.30)

|\partial_{t}^{k}P_{d}[F_{\mu}(\,\cdot\,;y),s](t)|\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}1+|\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;y)}\vec{\mu}|\qquad\textrm{for all $t\in(-1,1)$.}

It remains to control the remainder term. Write

\displaystyle R_{\mu}(s;y;t)

\displaystyle:=F_{\mu}(t;y)-P_{d}[F_{\mu}(\,\cdot\,;y),s](t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=d+1}^{\infty}\Big((\partial_{t}^{i}f)(s;y)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}(\partial_{t}^{i}g_{j})(s;y)\Big)\frac{(t-s)^{i}}{i!}.

Observe that the expression
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}(\partial_{t}^{i}g_{j})(s;y)/i!
corresponds precisely to the
i
th entry of
\mathfrak{B}(s;y)\vec{\mu}
, where
\mathfrak{B}(s;y)
is the matrix in (
5.2
) with infinitely many rows. Since
\mathfrak{B}(s;y)
and
B_{\leq d}(s;y)
have the same rank and the same number of columns, their kernels have the same dimension. Moreover, it is clear that
\ker\mathfrak{B}(s;y)
is a subspace of
\ker B_{\leq d}(s;y)
, from which we deduce that
\ker\mathfrak{B}(s;y)=\ker B_{\leq d}(s;y)=N(s;y)
. If we write
\mu^{\prime}:=\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;y)}\vec{\mu}
where
V(s;y):=N(s;y)^{\perp}
as above, then it follows that

R_{\mu}(s;y;t)=\sum_{i=d+1}^{\infty}\Big((\partial_{t}^{i}f)(s;y)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\partial_{t}^{i}g_{j})(s;y)\Big)\frac{(t-s)^{i}}{i!}.

In particular, for all
0\leq k\leq d
we have, by differentiation of power series,

\displaystyle|\partial^{k}_{t}R_{\mu}(s;y;t)|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;t)}\vec{\mu}|\sum_{i=d+1}^{\infty}\Big(|(\partial_{t}^{i}f)(s;y)|+\sum_{j=1}^{m}|(\partial_{t}^{i}g_{j})(s;y)|\Big)\frac{|t-s|^{i-k}}{(i-k)!}

(5.31)

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1+|\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;t)}\vec{\mu}|\big)|t-s|,

where the last inequality holds with an admissible constant over all
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
whenever
|t-s|<c_{\mathscr{E}}
for some admissible constant
c_{\mathscr{E}}>0
. Indeed, this can be verified using a similar argument to that applied at the end of Step 4 in the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Combining (
5.30
) with (
5.31
), there exists some
\mathscr{E}
-admissible
c_{0}>0
such that that for each
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
there exists some
k=k(s;y)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
satisfying
0\leq k\leq d
such that

|\partial_{t}^{k}F_{\mu}(t;y)|\gtrsim 1+|\mathrm{proj}_{V(s;y)}\vec{\mu}|\gtrsim_{\mathscr{E}}1\qquad\textrm{for all $t\in I_{\circ}$ with $|t-s|<c_{0}$.}

This allows us to apply the sublevel set estimate from Lemma
5.9
for each fixed
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
and
\vec{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
to deduce that

|\{t\in I_{\circ}:|F_{\mu}(t;y)|<\sigma\textrm{ and }|t-s|<c_{0}\}|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{1/d}.

The uniformity in the univariate van der Corput bound ensures that the above inequality holds with an
\mathscr{E}
-admissible implied constant: in particular, the constant is uniform over all choices of
\vec{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
(s;y)\in I_{\circ}\times Y_{\circ}
and
\sigma>0
. By covering
I
using
O_{\mathscr{E}}(1)
open intervals of length
2c_{0}
, we conclude the uniform sublevel set bound

\sup_{y\in Y_{\circ}}\sup_{\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}|\{t\in I_{\circ}:|F_{\mu}(t;y)|<\sigma\}|\lesssim_{\mathscr{E}}\sigma^{1/d}

for all
\sigma>0
, as required.
∎

6.
Geometric maximal estimates I: Preliminaries

6.1.
Multiplicity formulation

Recall the maximal function estimates in Definition
1.2
. Via
\ell^{p}
duality, these estimates are equivalent to
L^{p}
norm bounds for multiplicity functions associated to families of tubes. The latter bounds take the shape

(6.1)

\big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}\chi_{T}\big\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\phi,\varepsilon}\delta^{-\beta-\varepsilon}\big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\big)^{1/p},

where
\mathbb{T}
is a family of tubes associated to some phase
\phi
. To make this more precise, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-degenerate phase,
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
\beta\geq 0
.

I)

We let
\mathbf{K}_{p\to q}^{*}(\phi;\beta)
denote the statement:

For all
\varepsilon>0
, the inequality
(
6.1
) holds uniformly over all
0<\delta<1
and all direction-separated families
\mathbb{T}
of
\delta
-tubes associated to
\phi
.

II)

We let
\mathbf{N}_{p\to q}^{*}(\phi;\beta)
denote the statement:

For all
\varepsilon>0
, the inequality
(
6.1
) holds uniformly over all
0<\delta<1
and all centre-separated families
\mathbb{T}
of
\delta
-tubes associated to
\phi
.

By a standard duality argument,

(6.2)

\mathbf{K}_{p\to q}(\phi;\beta)\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{K}_{q^{\prime}\to p^{\prime}}^{*}(\phi;\beta)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{N}_{p\to q}(\phi;\beta)\Leftrightarrow\mathbf{N}_{q^{\prime}\to p^{\prime}}^{*}(\phi;\beta)

for all choices of exponent
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
.

6.2.
Multilinear Kakeya/Nikodym estimates

Rather than work directly with inequalities of the form (
6.1
), we apply the standard
broad-narrow
argument from
[
9
]
to reduce to a multilinear inequality involving transverse families of tubes.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-degenerate phase and
[\phi;a]
a phase-amplitude pair. For
2\leq k\leq n
,
\nu>\delta>0
and
G
the generalised Gauss map defined in (
1.1
), we make the following definitions.

a)

We say a
k
-tuple of
\delta
-tubes
(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
associated to
\phi
is
\nu
-transversal
if

(6.3)

\big|\bigwedge_{\ell=1}^{k}G(\mathbf{x};y(T_{\ell}))\big|\geq\nu>0\qquad\textrm{for all $\mathbf{x}\in T_{1}\cap\cdots\cap T_{k}$.}

(Here, recalling (
1.4
),
y(T_{l})
is the direction of
T_{l}
.)
For
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we say
(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
is
\nu
-transversal on
X
if the inequality (
6.3
) holds on the more restrictive range
\mathbf{x}\in T_{1}\cap\cdots\cap T_{k}\cap X
.
3
3

3

We remark that
G(\mathbf{x};y(T_{\ell}))
can be throught of as the tangent direction of
T_{\ell}
at
\mathbf{x}
. See
[
22
, Lemma 5.2]
.

b)

We say the families
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}
of
\delta
-tubes associated to
\phi
are
\nu
-transversal
(respectively,
\nu
-transversal on
X
) if every
k
-tuple
(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
with
T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
,
1\leq\ell\leq k
, is
\nu
-transversal.

Given
K\geq 1
, we let
\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
denote a cover of
\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\times(-1,1)
by essentially disjoint, axis-parallel cubes of side-length
K^{-1}
. The multilinear estimates take the shape

(6.4)
