\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(Q)}\lesssim_{\phi,\varepsilon}K^{A}\delta^{-\beta-\varepsilon}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}

where
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
is a cube,
\mathbb{T}
is a family of tubes associated to some phase
\phi
, the sets
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
are
K^{-(k-1)}
-transversal and
A=A_{n,k,p}>0
. To make this more precise, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-degenerate phase,
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
\beta\geq 0
.

I)

We let
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{K}_{p,k}^{*}(\phi;\beta)
denote the statement:

For all
\varepsilon>0
,
K\geq 1
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
, the inequality
(
6.4
) holds uniformly over all
0<\delta<1
, all direction-separated families
\mathbb{T}
of
\delta
-tubes associated to
\phi
, and all
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
which are
K^{-(k-1)}
-transversal on
Q
.

II)

We let
\mathbf{MN}_{p,k}^{*}(\phi;\beta)
denote the statement:

For all
\varepsilon>0
,
K\geq 1
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
, the inequality
(
6.4
)
holds uniformly over all
0<\delta<1
, all centre-separated families
\mathbb{T}
of
\delta
-tubes associated to
\phi
, and all
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
which are
K^{-(k-1)}
-transversal on
Q
.

Here and throughout what follows, the exponent
A=A_{n,k,p}>0
in (
6.4
) is understood to be a fixed quantity which depends only on
n
,
k
and
p
only. In particular, it is the same exponent as that appearing in Theorem
6.11
below.

Remark 6.4
.

Here we use the classical setup for our multilinear Kakeya/Nikodym estimates, in the spirit of the foundational work of Bennett–Carbery–Tao
[
4
]
. However, one could alternatively work with weaker
broad norms
as in the Kakeya argument in
[
25
]
. The broad norm approach is slightly easier, but we opt for the multilinear formalism since it produces stronger intermediary results.

6.3.
Multilinear reduction

In what follows we let
p^{\prime}(n):=\frac{n+1}{n-1}
denote the Hölder conjugate of the exponent from (
1.7
) and
s^{\prime}(n;p)
the Hölder conjugate of
s(n;p)
from (
1.8
).

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
n\geq 2
,
2\leq k<\frac{n+3}{2}
and
1\leq p\leq p^{\prime}(n)
. Given
0\leq\beta<\beta(n;p^{\prime})
, there exists some
0\leq\beta_{\circ}<\beta(n;p^{\prime})
such that the following holds. If
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Hörmander-type phase, then:

I)

\mathbf{M}\mathbf{K}_{p,k}^{*}(\phi;\beta)\Rightarrow\mathbf{K}_{p\to p}^{*}(\phi;\beta_{\circ})
;

II)

\mathbf{M}\mathbf{N}_{p,k}^{*}(\phi;\beta)\Rightarrow\mathbf{N}_{p\to p}^{*}(\phi;\beta_{\circ})
.

Proposition
6.5
follows from a very simple variant of the broad/narrow analysis of
[
9
]
. We remark that, since we are not looking to precisely quantify our estimates, the full machinery of
[
9
]
is not required.

In order to present the proof of Proposition
6.5
, we make some preliminary definitions and recall some observations from the literature. For
K\geq 1
, let
\Theta(K^{-1})
be a finitely-overlapping covering of
\mathbb{B}^{n-1}
by balls of radius
K^{-1}
. For each
\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1})
let

\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]:=\big\{T\in\mathbb{T}:y(T)\in\theta\big\},\qquad\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{N}}[\theta]:=\big\{T\in\mathbb{T}:\omega(T)\in\theta\big\}.

Given a linear subspace
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, define

(6.5)

\Theta(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x}):=\big\{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1}):\mathrm{dist}(G(\mathbf{x};y_{\theta}),V)\leq C_{\phi}K^{-1}\big\},

where
y_{\theta}
denotes the centre of the
K^{-1}
-ball
\theta
and
C_{\phi}\geq 1
is an admissible constant, chosen large enough to satisfy the forthcoming requirements of the proof.

For
2\leq k\leq n
a
k
-tuple
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\in\Theta(K^{-1})^{k}
is
transversal at
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
if

|G(\mathbf{z};y_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge G(\mathbf{z};y_{k})|\geq K^{-(k-1)}\qquad\textrm{for all $y_{\ell}\in\theta_{\ell}$, $1\leq\ell\leq k$ and $|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{x}|_{\infty}<K^{-1}$.}

We let
\Theta^{k}_{\mathrm{trans}}(K^{-1};\mathbf{x})
denote the collection of
k
-tuples which are transversal at
\mathbf{x}
. For simplicity, denote

\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{S}}\chi_{T}(\mathbf{x})

for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
.

Lemma 6.6

(Broad-narrow decomposition)
.

Let
2\leq k\leq n
,
\delta>0
,
1\leq K\ll\delta^{-1}
and
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
a non-degenerate phase. Given
\mathbb{T}
a family of
\delta
-tubes associated to
\phi
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{x})\lesssim\max_{V\in\mathrm{Gr}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\qquad+K^{n-1}\max_{(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\in\Theta^{k}_{\mathrm{trans}}(K^{-1};\mathbf{x})}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta_{\ell}]}(\mathbf{x})^{1/k}.

Here
\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
0\leq d\leq n
denotes the Grassmannian of all
d
-dimensional linear subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The proof is based on the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
0\leq d\leq n-1
and suppose
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a non-empty subset of the unit sphere
S^{n-1}
which satisfies the following condition:

(6.7)

for all
W\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n})
, there exists some
\omega\in\Omega
such that
\mathrm{dist}(\omega,W)\geq K^{-1}
.

Then there exist
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{d+1}\in\Omega
such that

|\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega_{d+1}|\geq K^{-d}.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
d
, noting that the base case
d=0
is immediate.

Suppose the result holds for
d-1
for some
1\leq d\leq n-1
and let
\Omega\subseteq S^{n-1}
be a set of points satisfying (
6.7
). Since any linear subspace of dimension
d-1
is contained in a linear subspace of dimension
d
, the set
\Omega
also satisfies (
6.7
) with
d
replaced with
d-1
. Hence, we may apply the induction hypothesis to conclude that there exist some
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{d}\in\Omega
such that

|\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega_{d}|\geq K^{-d+1}.

Let
W:=\mathrm{span}\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{d}\}
, so that
W
is a
d
-dimensional linear subspace. For any
\omega\in\Omega
, the ‘base
\times
perpendicular height’ formula for the volume of a parallelepiped gives

|\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega_{d}\wedge\omega|=|\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega_{d}||\mathrm{dist}(\omega,W)|\geq K^{-d+1}|\mathrm{dist}(\omega,W)|.

However, by hypothesis we can find some
\omega_{d+1}\in\Omega
such that
\mathrm{dist}(\omega_{d+1},W)\geq K^{-1}
. Plugging this value into the preceding display closes the induction.
∎

We now turn to the proof of the broad-narrow decomposition.

Proof (of Lemma
6.6
).

The first step is to identify some
V_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathrm{Gr}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n})
which ‘captures’ as many of the large values of
\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})
as possible. More precisely, define the
broad part
of the multiplicity function

(6.8)

\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x}):=\min_{V\in\mathrm{Gr}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n})}\max_{\theta\notin\Theta(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})

and suppose
V_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathrm{Gr}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n})
realises the minimum in (
6.8
). For slightly technical reasons, we also define

(6.9)

\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x}):=\big\{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x}):\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})\geq\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x})\big\}

and further choose
V_{\mathbf{x}}
so that, of all spaces realising the minimum in (
6.8
), the space
V_{\mathbf{x}}
also maximises
\#\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x})
.

From the definition, the normals
G(\mathbf{x};y_{\theta})
associated to the caps
\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})
are aligned around the
(k-1)
-dimensional subspace
V_{\mathbf{x}}
. However, they do not align around any lower dimensional subspace.

Claim.
There does not exist an affine subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
of dimension
k-2
such that
\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})\subseteq\Theta(K^{-1};W;\mathbf{x})
.

Assuming the claim, it is a simple matter to conclude the proof of Lemma
6.6
. We define the collections of
narrow
and
broad
caps (for
\mu_{\mathbb{T}}
at
\mathbf{x}
) by

\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\Theta(K^{-1})\setminus\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x}),

respectively. By the defining properties of
V_{\mathbf{x}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{x}}}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})+\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{B}_{x}}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{V\in\mathrm{Gr}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Big[\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})\Big]+K^{n-1}\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x}).

In view of the claim, there exists a
(k-1)
-tuple of caps

(\theta_{\mathbf{x},1},\dots,\theta_{\mathbf{x},k-1})\in\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})^{k-1}

which is transverse at
\mathbf{x}
in the sense that

\inf\Big\{\Big|\bigwedge_{\ell=1}^{k-1}G(\mathbf{z};y_{\ell})\Big|:y_{\ell}\in\theta_{x,\ell},\,1\leq\ell\leq k-1,\,|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{x}|_{\infty}<2K^{-1}\Big\}\geq K^{-(k-2)}.

To see this, we simply apply Lemma
6.7
with

\Omega:=\big\{G(\mathbf{x};y_{\theta}):\theta\in\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})^{k-1}\big\},

using the claim to verify the hypothesis (
6.7
) with
d=k-2
. The constant
C_{\phi}
in the definition of (
6.5
) can be used to pass from bounds on the the size of wedge products of the
G(\mathbf{x};y_{\theta})
to more general
G(\mathbf{z};y_{\theta})
.

By definition, there exists a cap
\theta_{\mathbf{x},k}\notin\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})
such that

\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta_{\mathbf{x},k}]}(\mathbf{x})=\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x}).

It follows that the
k
-tuple
(\theta_{\mathbf{x},1},\dots,\theta_{\mathbf{x},k})
is transversal at
\mathbf{x}
and satisfies

\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x})\leq\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta_{\mathbf{x},\ell}]}(\mathbf{x})^{1/k}\leq\max_{(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\in\Theta^{k}_{\mathrm{trans}}(K^{-1};\mathbf{x})}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta_{\ell}]}(\mathbf{x})^{1/k}.

It remains to prove the claim. We argue by contradiction, assuming such a subspace
W
exists. Let
\theta^{*}:=\theta_{\mathbf{x},k}
be as above, so that
\theta^{*}\notin\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})
realises the maximum in the definition (
6.8
) for
V=V_{\mathbf{x}}
. Define
V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*}
to be a linear subspace of dimension
k-1
which contains
W
and
G(\mathbf{x};y^{*})
, where
y^{*}
is the centre of
\theta^{*}
.

First note that
V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*}
realises the minimum in (
6.8
). Indeed, by the definition (
6.9
), if
\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})\setminus\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})
, then
\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})<\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x})
. Consequently,

\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x})=\max_{\theta\notin\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})=\max_{\theta\notin\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x}).

On the other hand, from the hypothesis on
W
and the definition of
V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*}
we have
\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})\subseteq\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*};\mathbf{x})
and so

\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x})\geq\max_{\theta\notin\Theta(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*};\mathbf{x})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})\geq\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta^{*}]}(\mathbf{x})=\mu_{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{Br}}(\mathbf{x}).

In light of the maximality of
V_{\mathbf{x}}
, it follows that
\#\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*},\mathbf{x})\leq\#\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{x})
. However, it is clear from the definitions that

\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})\subseteq\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*},\mathbf{x}),

whilst

\theta^{*}\in\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*},\mathbf{x})\quad\textrm{and}\quad\theta^{*}\notin\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x}).

Thus,
\#\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}};\mathbf{x})<\#\widetilde{\Theta}(K^{-1};V_{\mathbf{x}}^{*},\mathbf{x})
, which is a contradiction.
∎

The pointwise estimate from Lemma
6.6
implies the following
L^{p}
variant. Here
\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
is defined as before (
6.4
).

Corollary 6.8

(
L^{p}
Broad-narrow decomposition
[
9
]
)
.

Let
2\leq k\leq n
,
1\leq p<\infty
,
\delta>0
,
1\leq K\ll\delta^{-1}
and
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
a Hörmander-type phase. Suppose
\mathbb{T}
is a family of
\delta
-tubes associated to
\phi
and let
\mathrm{X}\in\{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}\}
. For each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
, there exist
\mathbb{T}_{1}[Q],\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}[Q]\subseteq\mathbb{T}
which are
K^{-(k-1)}
-transversal on
Q
such that

(6.10)

\displaystyle\big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}\chi_{T}\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\phi}K^{(k-2)/p^{\prime}}\Big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1})}\big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{X}}[\theta]}\chi_{T}\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\qquad+K^{(n-1)(1+k/p)}\Big(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[Q]}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(Q)}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}.

In what follows, we refer to (
6.10
) as the
L^{p}
broad-narrow decomposition
, and the first and second terms on the right-hand side of (
6.10
) as the
L^{p}
narrow
and
L^{p}
broad
terms, respectively.

Proof (of Corollary
6.8
).

We apply Lemma
6.6
and take the
L^{p}
norms of both sides of the resulting pointwise inequality. We apply the
L^{p}
triangle inequality to analyse separately the contributions arising from the first and second terms on the right-hand side of (
6.6
).

Consider the contribution arising from the first term in (
6.6
). Let
V\in\mathrm{Gr}(k-1,\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
N_{1},\dots,N_{n-k+1}
be a basis for the orthogonal complement of
V
. The caps belonging to
\Theta(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x})
lie in an
O(K^{-1})
neighbourhood of the set

M_{\mathbf{x}}:=\big\{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}:\langle G(\mathbf{x};y),N_{j}\rangle=0\textrm{ for }1\leq j\leq n-k+1\big\}.

Since
\phi
is Hörmander-type, it follows that
M_{\mathbf{x}}
is a
(k-2)
-dimensional submanifold. Thus,
\#\Theta(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x})\lesssim_{\phi}K^{k-2}
and, consequently,

(6.11)

\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1};V;\mathbf{x})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})\lesssim_{\phi}K^{(k-2)/p^{\prime}}\Big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})^{p}\Big)^{1/p}.

On the other hand, we claim that

(6.12)

\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})^{p}\lesssim_{\phi}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1})}\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{X}}[\theta]}(\mathbf{x})^{p}\qquad\textrm{for $\mathrm{X}\in\{\mathrm{K},\mathrm{N}\}.$}

Temporarily assuming this is the case, we combine (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) and take the
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-norm of both sides of the resulting estimate. From this we see that the contribution of the first term in (
6.6
) corresponds to the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.10
).

Turning to the proof of (
6.12
), it suffices to show that for any
\theta^{*}\in\Theta(K^{-1})
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have

(6.13)

\#\big\{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1}):\,\exists\,T\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{N}}[\theta]\cap\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta^{*}]\textrm{ s.t. }\mathbf{x}\in T\big\}\lesssim_{\phi}1.

Suppose that
\mathbf{x}\in T_{j}
for
j=1
,
2
, and
\mathbf{x}=(x,t)
,
y_{j}:=y(T_{j})
and
\omega_{j}:=\omega(T_{j})
. We therefore have
|\Psi(\omega_{1};t;y_{1})-\Psi(\omega_{2};t;y_{2})|<2\delta
, where
\Psi
is as defined in (
1.3
). Now, in addition, suppose
T_{j}\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta^{*}]
for
j=1
,
2
, so that
|y_{1}-y_{2}|\lesssim K^{-1}
. For
\delta\leq K^{-1}
, it follows that

|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|=\big|(\partial_{y}\phi)(\Psi(\omega_{1};t;y_{1}),t;y_{1})-(\partial_{y}\phi)(\Psi(\omega_{2};t;y_{2}),t;y_{2})\big|\lesssim_{\phi}K^{-1}.

From these observations, we deduce that (
6.13
), and therefore also (
6.12
), holds.

We now consider the contribution from the second term in (
6.6
). Given
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
and
\mathbf{x}\in Q
, we have
\Theta^{k}_{\mathrm{trans}}(K^{-1};\mathbf{x})\subseteq\Theta^{k}(Q)
where
\Theta^{k}(Q)
denotes the set of all
k
-tuples
\vec{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\in\Theta(K^{-1})^{k}
such that

|G(\mathbf{z};y_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge G(\mathbf{z};y_{k})|\geq K^{-(k-1)}\qquad\textrm{for all $y_{\ell}\in\theta_{\ell}$, $1\leq\ell\leq k$, and $\mathbf{z}\in Q$.}

We pointwise dominate the maximum appearing in the second term in (
6.6
) by the corresponding
\ell^{p}
-norm. In view of the above and pigeonholing, the
L^{p}(Q)
-norm of the resulting expression can be bounded by

\Big(\sum_{\vec{\theta}\in\Theta^{k}(Q)}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta_{\ell}]}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(Q)}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\lesssim K^{(n-1)k/p}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[Q]}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(Q)},

where
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[Q]:=\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{K}}[\theta_{\ell,Q}]
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
and some
(\theta_{1,Q},\dots,\theta_{k,Q})\in\Theta^{k}(Q)
. Taking the
\ell^{p}(\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}})
-norm of the above estimate, we see that the contribution of the second term in (
6.6
) corresponds to the second term on the right-hand side of (
6.10
).

∎

Proof (of Proposition
6.5
).

We treat cases I) and II) simultaneously, as the proofs are identical. Let
K\geq 1
and apply Corollary
6.8
, taking
\mathrm{X}:=\mathrm{K}
and
\mathrm{X}:=\mathrm{N}
in I) and II), respectively. We proceed to estimate the two terms on the right-hand side of (
6.10
).

L^{p}
Narrow term
. Since the tubes have
\delta
-separated directions in case I) and
\delta
-separated centres in case II), we see that in either case

(6.14)

\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{X}}[\theta]}|T|\lesssim_{\phi}K^{-(n-1)}.

We apply the dual form of the universal Kakeya and Nikodym bounds from Proposition
1.4
. In the regime
1\leq p\leq p^{\prime}(n)
, we have
\mathbf{K}_{s\to p}^{*}\big(\phi;\beta(n;p^{\prime})\big)
and
\mathbf{N}_{s\to p}^{*}\big(\phi;\beta(n;p^{\prime})\big)
for
s^{\prime}(n;p^{\prime})\leq s\leq\infty
. Taking
s=s^{\prime}(n;p^{\prime})
, and noting that this exponent satisfies
\frac{1}{s}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{2p^{\prime}}
, we therefore deduce that

\displaystyle\big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{X}}[\theta]}\chi_{T}\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\phi}\delta^{-\beta(n;p^{\prime})}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{X}}[\theta]}|T|\Big)^{1/p+1/(2p^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta^{-\beta(n;p^{\prime})}K^{-(n-1)/(2p^{\prime})}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{X}}[\theta]}|T|\Big)^{1/p},

where the second step is by (
6.14
).
Summing over all
\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1})
, we deduce that

(6.15)

K^{(k-2)/p^{\prime}}\Big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(K^{-1})}\big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{X}}[\theta]}\chi_{T}\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\lesssim_{\phi}\delta^{-\beta(n;p^{\prime})}K^{-e(n,k;p)}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p},

where

(6.16)

e(n,k;p):=\frac{n+3-2k}{2p^{\prime}}.

This is our estimate for the
L^{p}
narrow term in (
6.10
).

L^{p}
Broad term
. Write
\beta:=\beta(n;p^{\prime})-\eta
for some
\eta>0
. Since the families of tubes
\mathbb{T}_{1}[Q],\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}[Q]
are
K^{-(k-1)}
-transversal on
Q
, applying either the hypothesis
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{K}_{p,k}^{*}(\phi;\beta)
in case I) or the hypothesis
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{N}_{p,k}^{*}(\phi;\beta)
in case II), we obtain

(6.17)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[Q]}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(Q)}\lesssim_{\phi}K^{A}\delta^{-\beta(n;p^{\prime})+\eta}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}

for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
. Summing in
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
, which induces an additional factor of
K^{n/p}
, yields our estimate for the
L^{p}
broad term in (
6.10
).

Applying Corollary
6.8
, (
6.15
) and (
6.17
), it follows that

\big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}\chi_{T}\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\phi}\delta^{-\beta(n;p^{\prime})}\big(K^{-e(n,k;p)}+K^{A+(n-1)(1+k/p)+n/p}\delta^{\eta}\big)\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}.

The above inequality is valid for all choices of
K\geq 1
. Furthermore, under the condition
2\leq k<\frac{n+3}{2}
, the exponent
e(n,k;p)
defined in (
6.16
) is strictly positive. Thus, if we choose
K:=\delta^{-\eta/E}
where
E:=2(A+2n^{2})
, say, then it is clear that this yields an estimate of the desired form.
∎

6.4.
Universal multilinear estimate

In light of Proposition
6.5
, we shall henceforth focus on proving multilinear Kakeya/Nikodym estimates. The starting point is to consider certain
universal
multilinear inequalities, which hold for all non-degenerate phases. These universal bounds follow from a variant of the Bennett–Carbery–Tao
[
4
]
theorem.

It is convenient to work with a slightly more general setup, which allows us to treat both the Kakeya and Nikodym cases in a single framework. In what follows, we say a function
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
is a
C^{\infty}
(respectively,
polynomial
)
mapping
if the components
\gamma_{j}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are
C^{\infty}
functions (respectively, polynomials) for
1\leq j\leq n-1
.

Definition 6.9
.

We say
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}

defines a uniform family of
C^{\infty}
(respectively,
polynomial
)
curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
if
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is a set of
C^{\infty}
(respectively, polynomial) mappings
\gamma\colon(-1,1)\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and there exists some sequence
(M_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,\infty)
such that

(6.18)

\sup_{\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\sup_{-1<t<1}|\gamma^{(N)}(t)|\leq M_{N}\qquad\textrm{for all $N\in\mathbb{N}$.}

In this case, for each
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
we define a curve in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
given by the graph

\Gamma_{\gamma}=\big\{(\gamma(t),t):t\in(-1,1)\big\}.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
define a uniform family of
C^{\infty}
curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
0<\delta<\nu<1
.

i)

Given
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, we define
T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
the
\delta
-tube
associated to
\gamma
by

T:=\big\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\times(-1,1):|x-\gamma(t)|<\delta\big\}.

In this case, we write
\gamma_{T}:=\gamma
and
\Gamma_{T}:=\Gamma_{\gamma}=\{(\gamma_{T}(t),t):t\in(-1,1)\}
is referred to as the
core curve
of
T
.

ii)

We say
\mathbb{T}
is a
family of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes
if
\mathbb{T}
is a finite set of
\delta
-tubes associated to elements of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
.

iii)

Given
2\leq k\leq n
, we say
(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})\in\mathbb{T}^{k}
is
\nu
-transversal
if

(6.19)

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{\ell}\in\Gamma_{{T_{\ell}}},\,|\mathbf{x}-y_{\ell}|<\delta\\
1\leq\ell\leq k\end{subarray}}\big|\bigwedge_{\ell=1}^{k}T_{y_{\ell}}\Gamma_{T_{\ell}}\big|\geq\nu>0\qquad\textrm{for all $\mathbf{x}\in T_{1}\cap\cdots\cap T_{k}$.}

where
T_{y_{\ell}}\Gamma_{T_{\ell}}
denotes the tangent space to
\Gamma_{T_{\ell}}
at
y_{\ell}
and the infimum is taken over all tuples
(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})\in\Gamma_{T_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{T_{k}}
with
|\mathbf{x}-y_{\ell}|<\delta
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
. For
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we say
(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})\in\mathbb{T}^{k}
is
\nu
-transversal on
X
if the inequality (
6.19
) holds on the more restrictive range
\mathbf{x}\in T_{1}\cap\cdots\cap T_{k}\cap X
.
4
4

4

We remark that (
6.19
) agrees with (
6.3
) for an appropriate choice of
\nu
when the tubes are defined by a phase function.

iv)

We say
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subset\mathbb{T}
are
\nu
-transversal
(respectively,
\nu
-transversal on
X
) if every
k
-tuple
(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
with
T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
,
1\leq\ell\leq k
, is
\nu
-transversal (respectively,
\nu
-transversal on
X
).

Having made these preliminary definitions, we can now state the universal multilinear theorem.

Theorem 6.11

(Universal multilinear estimate)
.

For
2\leq k\leq n
and
1\leq p\leq\frac{k}{k-1}
, there exists a constant
A=A_{n,k,p}\geq 1
such that the following holds. Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
define a uniform family of
C^{\infty}
curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and, for
0<\delta<1
, let
\mathbb{T}
be a family of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes. Then, for all
\varepsilon>0
, the inequality

(6.20)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap B_{r})}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon}\nu^{-A}r^{k/p-(k-1)}\delta^{k-1+(n-k)/p-\varepsilon}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}\big)^{1/k}.

holds whenever
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subset\mathbb{T}
are
K^{-1}
-transversal on
X
for some
\nu>0
and a measurable set
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
B_{r}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a ball of radius
\delta<r<1
.

More precisely, one can ensure that the implicit constant in (
6.20
) depends on
n
,
\varepsilon
and the constant
M_{1}
in (
6.18
) only.

Proof (of Theorem
6.11
).

By log convexity of
L^{p}
-norms, it suffices to only consider the cases
p=1
and
p=\frac{k}{k-1}
. The
p=1
case follows simply by applying the multilinear Hölder inequality and exchanging the order of summation and integration, using the fact that
|T_{\ell}\cap B_{r}|\lesssim r\delta^{n-1}
for any
T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
. The
p=\frac{k}{k-1}
case is a curved variant of the Bennett–Carbery–Tao theorem
[
4
]
: see, for instance,
[
9
,
19
]
. We remark that the result of
[
19
]
ensures that the implicit constant depends on
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
in the manner described above.
∎

6.5.
Improved multilinear estimates via non-concentration

Our goal is to establish multilinear estimates which go beyond the universal bounds of Theorem
6.11
. To do so, we shall impose a non-concentration hypothesis on the underlying family of tubes
\mathbb{T}
, similar to that appearing in Definition
4.5
.

Definition 6.12
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
define a uniform family of
C^{\infty}
curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
0<\delta<1
,
0\leq m\leq n
and
\kappa>0
,
B=(B_{1},B_{2})\in[0,\infty)^{2}
. We say a family
\mathbb{T}
of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes is
(\kappa,B)
-non-concentrated in codimension
m
grains if the following holds.

We have
\#\mathbb{T}\leq\delta^{-(n-1)}
and, for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
, the inequality

(6.21)

\#\big\{T\in\mathbb{T}:|T\cap G|\geq\lambda|T|\big\}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon,d}(\lambda/\rho)^{-B_{1}}\rho^{-B_{2}}\delta^{-(n-1)+\kappa-\varepsilon}

holds whenever
G\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
(\delta,\rho,d)
-grain of codimension
m
for
\delta\leq\lambda\leq\rho\leq 1
.

More precisely, the implied constant in (
6.21
) depends on
n
,
\varepsilon
,
d
and a finite number (depending only on
n
and
\varepsilon
) of the
M_{N}
in (
6.18
) only.

Theorem 6.13

(Multilinear Kakeya/Nikodym estimate)
.

For
2\leq k\leq n
,
1<p<\frac{k}{k-1}
and
\kappa>0
,
B\in[0,\infty)^{2}
, there exists some
\eta(p)>0
such that the following holds. Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
defines a uniform family of
C^{\infty}
curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and, for
0<\delta<1
, that
\mathbb{T}
is a family of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes which is
(\kappa,B)
-non-concentrated in codimension
n-k
grains. Then the inequality

(6.22)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},p}\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta(p)}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}

holds whenever
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subset\mathbb{T}
are
\nu
-transversal families for some
\nu>0
.

More precisely, the implied constant in (
6.22
) can be taken to depend only on
n
and a finite number (depending only on
n
,
k
,
p
,
\kappa
and
B
) of the quantities
M_{N}
in (
6.18
). The exponent
A=A_{n,k,p}\geq 1
is the same as that appearing in Theorem
6.11
. Finally, the choice of exponent
\eta(p)
depends on
n
,
k
,
p
,
\kappa
and
B
. We remark that the theorem is vacuously true for
k=n
, since there do not exist
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes which are
(\kappa,B)
-non-concentrated in codimension
0
grains. We also remark that there is a gain of a factor
\delta^{\eta(p)}
in (
6.22
) compared with (
6.20
), as one may check the numerology by plugging in
\#\mathbb{T}=\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}\sim\delta^{-(n-1)},|T|\sim\delta^{n-1}
and
r=1
.

We shall prove Theorem
6.13
in the next section, by adapting the polynomial partitioning arguments of Guth
[
21
]
and their refinements from
[
25
]
.

7.
Geometric maximal estimates II: Polynomial partitioning

In this section, we adapt the polynomial partitioning arguments of
[
21
,
25
]
to apply to families of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes satisfying weak non-concentration hypotheses. At the end of the section, we apply the resulting partitioning algorithm to deduce Theorem
6.13
.

7.1.
Polynomial partitioning

Here we describe the main polynomial partitioning theorem from
[
21
]
. Before presenting the statement, we recall some preliminary definitions.

Given a polynomial
P\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
consider the collection
\mathrm{cell}(P)

of connected components of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus Z(P)
. Each
O^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cell}(P)
is referred to as a
cell
cut out by the variety
Z(P)
and the cells are thought of as partitioning the ambient Euclidean space into a finite collection of disjoint regions. In order to account for the choice of scale
\delta>0
appearing in the definition of the
\delta
-tubes, it will be useful to consider the family of
\delta
-shrunken cells
defined by

(7.1)

\mathcal{O}:=\big\{O^{\prime}\setminus N_{\delta}Z(P):O^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cell}(P)\big\}.

Finally, given a finite set
A
, we say
A^{\prime}
is a

refinement
of
A
if
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and
\#A^{\prime}\gtrsim\#A
.

Theorem 7.1

(Guth
[
21
]
)
.

Fix
0<\delta<\rho
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose
F\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is non-negative and supported on a
(2\delta,\rho,d)
-grain
G
of codimension
m
. There exists a dimensional constant
C_{\deg}\geq 1
such that at least one of the following cases holds:

Cellular case

There exists a polynomial
P\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
of degree at most
C_{\deg}d
with the following properties:

i)

\#\mathrm{cell}(P)\sim d^{n-m}
and each
O\in\mathrm{cell}(P)
has diameter at most
\rho/2
.

ii)

One may pass to a refinement
\mathrm{cell}(P)^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{cell}(P)
such that if
\mathcal{O}
is defined as in (
7.1
) with
\mathrm{cell}(P)
replaced with
\mathrm{cell}(P)^{\prime}
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F\lesssim d^{n-m}\int_{O}F\qquad\textrm{for all $O\in\mathcal{O}$.}

Algebraic case

There exists a
(\delta,\rho,C_{\deg}d)
-grain
G^{\prime}
of codimension
m+1
such that

\int_{G}F\,\lesssim\,\log d\int_{G^{\prime}}F.

The statement of this theorem does not explicitly appear in
[
21
]
, but it can be easily deduced from the argument described in Section 8.1 of that article together with simple pigeonholing arguments.

7.2.
Finding algebraic structure

Here we describe the main partitioning algorithm. We present this in two parts: roughly speaking, the first algorithm
[partition
m\to m+1
]
reduces a codimension
m
arrangement of tubes (that is, the tubes have large intersection with a small neighbourhood of a low degree codimension
m
variety) to a codimension
m+1
arrangement. The second algorithm
[partition]
involves repeated application of
[partition
m\to m+1
]
. We formulate the algorithm in terms of general families of tubes formed around polynomial curves (which do not necessarily arise from a phase-amplitude pair).

Definition 7.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
define a uniform family of polynomial curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
0<\delta<1
and
E\in\mathbb{N}
. We say a family of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes
\mathbb{T}
has
degree bounded by
E
if

\max\{\deg\gamma_{T,1},\dots,\deg\gamma_{T,n-1}\}\leq E\qquad\textrm{for all $T\in\mathbb{T}$,}

where the
\gamma_{T,j}
are the components of
\gamma_{T}
.

Given any finite family
\mathbb{T}
of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, as earlier we write

\mu_{\mathbb{T}}:=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}\chi_{T}.

Our algorithm essentially consists of repeated applications of Theorem
7.1
to
\mu_{\mathbb{T}}
.

The first algorithm.
With the above preliminaries in hand, we turn to describing the first partitioning algorithm
[partition
m\to m+1
]
.

Input
.

[partition
m\to m+1
]
will take as its input:

•

A set
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
defining a uniform family of polynomial curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

Parameters
1\leq p<\infty
,
0<\varepsilon_{\circ}
,
\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}<1
,
0<\delta\leq\rho\leq 1
and
1\leq E\leq d
. Furthermore,
d
is chosen large, depending on
\varepsilon_{\circ}
and
E
.

•

A family
\mathbb{T}
of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes of degree bounded by
E\geq 1
.

•

An integer
2\leq k\leq n
and subfamilies
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
.

•

A
(\delta,\rho,d)
-grain
G_{m}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of codimension
0\leq m\leq n-2
and a set
X:=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times I
for some choice of open interval
I\subseteq(-1,1)
.

Note that the process applies to essentially arbitrary families of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes: in particular, neither the direction-separated nor the centre-separated hypothesis appear at this stage, and nor does any transversality hypothesis.

Output
.

[partition
m\to m+1
]
outputs finite sequences

(\mathcal{O}_{j})_{j=0}^{J}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad(\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]:O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j})_{j=0}^{J}\qquad\textrm{for $1\leq\ell\leq k$,}

where:

•

Each
\mathcal{O}_{j}
is a family of measurable subsets of
G_{m}\cap X
, each of diameter at most
r_{j}:=2^{1-j}\rho
. The sets
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}
are referred to as
cells
.

•

\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
is a subfamily of
\delta
-tubes, for each
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}
and
1\leq\ell\leq k
.

Moreover, the above objects are defined so as to ensure that the following hold:

Property I.

(\mathrm{I})_{j}

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}^{p}\leq d^{\varepsilon_{\circ}j}\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p};

Property II.
For all
1\leq\ell\leq k
,

(\mathrm{II})_{j}

\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]\leq d^{(1+\varepsilon_{\circ})j}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell};

Property III.
For all
1\leq\ell\leq k
,

(\mathrm{III})_{j}

\max_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]\leq d^{-(n-m-1-\varepsilon_{\circ})j}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}.

Initial step
.
Take
\mathcal{O}_{0}:=\{G_{m}\}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]:=\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
. By definition, the grain
G_{m}
is contained in a ball of radius
\rho
and therefore has diameter at most
r_{0}:=2\rho
. Furthermore, Property I, II and III all vacuously hold.

Stopping conditions
.
The algorithm terminates with the completion of its
j
th step, for some
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, if either of two stopping conditions is met. These stopping conditions are labelled
[tiny]
and
[algebraic]
. To describe them, we let
C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)
be a constant depending only on
M_{1}
in (
6.18
) and
d
and
C_{\mathrm{alg}}\geq 1
be another constant which depends only on admissible parameters. Both
C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)
and
C_{\mathrm{alg}}
are chosen sufficiently large for the purposes of the forthcoming argument.

Stop:[tiny]
.
There exist

•

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
a family of measurable subsets of
X
with
\mathrm{diam}\,O\leq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
for all
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
;

•

\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
a subfamily of
\delta
-tubes for each
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
and
1\leq\ell\leq k
,

such that the following hold:

Property I
tiny
.

\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\leq C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O)}^{p};

Property II
tiny
.

\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\leq C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}];

Property III
tiny
.

\max_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\leq C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)\max_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}].

Stop:[algebraic]
.
Let
r_{j}:=2^{1-j}\rho
be as above and
C_{\deg}\geq 1
be the dimensional constant appearing in the statement of Theorem
7.1
. There exist

•

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{j}
;

•

G_{O}
a
(\delta,r_{j},C_{\deg}d)
-grain of codimension
m+1
for each
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}

such that

(7.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}

\displaystyle\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{alg}}\sum_{s=1}^{k}\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}}\Big\|\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{s}[G_{O}]}\big|^{1/k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\ell\leq k\\
\ell\neq s\end{subarray}}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{O})}^{p}

where

(7.3)

\mathbb{T}_{s}[G_{O}]:=\Big\{T_{s}\in\mathbb{T}_{s}[O]:|T_{s}\cap X\cap N_{2\delta}G_{O}|\geq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}|T_{s}|\Big\}

for
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
and
1\leq s\leq k
.

Recursive step
.
Suppose for some
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we are at the
j
th stage of the recursive process, so in particular
\mathcal{O}_{j}
and the
(\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]:O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j})_{\ell=1}^{k}
have been constructed and satisfy
(\mathrm{I})_{j}
,
(\mathrm{II})_{j}
and
(\mathrm{III})_{j}
. Further suppose that neither stopping condition
[tiny]
nor
[algebraic]
is met. Our goal is to construct the next generation of objects
\mathcal{O}_{j+1}
and
(\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]:O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1})_{\ell=1}^{k}
so that
(\mathrm{I})_{j+1}
,
(\mathrm{II})_{j+1}
and
(\mathrm{III})_{j+1}
hold.

Fix
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}
and recall that
O_{j}\subseteq X\cap G_{m}
. We apply the polynomial partitioning result from Theorem
7.1
with degree
d
to the function

\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{p/k}\chi_{O_{j}},

which, in particular, is supported in the codimension
m
grain
G_{m}
. For each
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}
either the cellular or the algebraic case holds, as defined in Theorem
7.1
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}
denote the subcollection of
\mathcal{O}_{j}
consisting of all cells for which the cellular case holds and
\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}:=\mathcal{O}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}
.

Claim 7.3
.

The cells in
\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}
dominate, in the sense that

(7.4)

\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\leq 2\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}.

We shall temporarily assume the above claim and show how to construct the cells
\mathcal{O}_{j+1}
. Using the claim together with
(\mathrm{I})_{j}
, we have

(7.5)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(G_{m}\cap X)}^{p}\leq 2d^{\varepsilon_{\circ}j}\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}.

The next generation of cells
\mathcal{O}_{j+1}
will now arise from the cellular decomposition guaranteed by Theorem
7.1
. Fix
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}
so that there exists some polynomial
P\in\mathbb{R}[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]
of degree at most
C_{\deg}d
with the following properties:

i)

\#\mathrm{cell}(P)\sim d^{n-m}
and each
O\in\mathrm{cell}(P)
has diameter at most
r_{j+1}=2^{-j}\rho
.

ii)

One may pass to a refinement
\mathrm{cell}(P)^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{cell}(P)
such that if

\mathcal{O}_{j+1}^{\prime}(O_{j}):=\big\{\big(O\setminus N_{\delta}Z(P)\big)\cap X\cap G_{m}:O\in\mathrm{cell}(P)^{\prime}\}

denotes the corresponding collection of
\delta
-shrunken cells, then

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\lesssim\,d^{n-m}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j+1})}^{p}

for all
O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}^{\prime}(O_{j})
.

Given
O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}^{\prime}(O_{j})
, define

\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]:=\big\{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]:T_{\ell}\cap O_{j+1}\neq\emptyset\big\}.

Recall that, for any
T\in\mathbb{T}
, the core curve
\Gamma_{T}
of
T
is parametrised by
(\gamma_{T}(t),t)
for
t\in(-1,1)
, where the components of
\gamma_{T}
are polynomials of degree at most
E
. Thus, by the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, any
T\in\mathbb{T}
can enter at most
Ed+1
cells
O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}^{\prime}(O_{j})
. Consequently,

(7.6)

\sum_{O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}^{\prime}(O_{j})}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]\lesssim_{E}d\cdot\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}],

hence one may pass to a refinement of
\mathcal{O}_{j+1}^{\prime}(O_{j})
such that

(7.7)

\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]\lesssim_{E}d^{-(n-m-1)}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]\qquad\textrm{for all $O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}(O_{j})$,}

since
\#\mathcal{O}_{j+1}^{\prime}(O_{j})\sim d^{n-m}
. Finally, define

\mathcal{O}_{j+1}:=\bigcup_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}}\mathcal{O}_{j+1}(O_{j}).

This completes the construction of
\mathcal{O}_{j+1}
and the
(\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]:O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1})_{\ell=1}^{k}
; it remains to check that these objects satisfy the desired properties.

Property I.
Fix
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}
and observe that
\#\mathcal{O}_{j+1}(O_{j})\sim d^{n-m}
and

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\lesssim d^{n-m}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j+1})}^{p}

for all
O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}(O_{j})
. Averaging,

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\lesssim\sum_{O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}(O_{j})}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j+1})}^{p}.

Recalling (
7.5
), one therefore deduces that

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}^{p}\leq Cd^{-\varepsilon_{\circ}}d^{\varepsilon_{\circ}(j+1)}\sum_{O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j+1})}^{p},

where
C\geq 1
is an admissible constant which is an amalgamation of the implicit constants arising in the above argument. Provided
d
is chosen large enough so as to ensure that the additional
d^{-\varepsilon_{\circ}}
factor absorbs the unwanted constant
C
, one deduces
(\mathrm{I})_{j+1}
.

Property II.
By the construction,

\displaystyle\sum_{O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]

\displaystyle\,=\,\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}}\;\sum_{O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}(O_{j})}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{E}d\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}],

where the inequality follows from a term-wise application of (
7.6
). Thus,
(\mathrm{II})_{j}
implies that

\sum_{O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]\leq C_{E}d^{-\varepsilon_{\circ}}d^{(1+\varepsilon_{\circ})(j+1)}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}.

Provided
d
is chosen sufficiently large, depending on
E
and admissible parameters, one deduces
(\mathrm{II})_{j+1}
.

Property III.
Let
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{cell}}
. Fix
O_{j+1}\in\mathcal{O}_{j+1}(O_{j})
and recall from (
7.7
) that

\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]\lesssim_{E}d^{-(n-m-1)}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}].

Thus,
(\mathrm{III})_{j}
implies that

\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j+1}]\leq C_{E}d^{-\varepsilon_{\circ}}d^{-(n-m-1-\varepsilon_{\circ})(j+1)}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}.

Provided
d
is chosen sufficiently large, one deduces
(\mathrm{III})_{j+1}
.

This concludes the description of the recursive step of
[partition
m\to m+1
]
, except that it remains to justify Claim
7.3
.

Proof (of Claim
7.3
).

If the condition (
7.4
) of the claim fails, then

(7.8)

\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\leq 2\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}.

We shall show that this implies either the stopping condition
[tiny]
or
[algebraic]
is met, contrary to our initial assumption. For this, we use some ideas from the proof of
[
23
, Proposition 3.1]
.

Fix
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}
. By definition, there exists some
(\delta,r_{j},C_{\deg}d)
-grain
G=G_{O_{j}}
of codimension
m+1
such that

(7.9)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\lesssim\log d\int_{X\cap G}\Big|\sum_{(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[O_{j}]}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\chi_{T_{\ell}}(\mathbf{x})\Big|^{p/k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

where
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[O_{j}]:=\mathbb{T}_{1}[O_{j}]\times\cdots\times\mathbb{T}_{k}[O_{j}]
. Given a set
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbf{x}\in S
, let
\mathrm{CC}(S;\mathbf{x})
denote the Euclidean connected component of
S
containing
\mathbf{x}
. For each
\mathbf{x}\in G
, we partition
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[O_{j}]
into two sets:

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{tiny}}[O_{j};\mathbf{x}]

\displaystyle:=\Big\{(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[O_{j}]:\max_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\mathrm{diam}(\mathrm{CC}(T_{\ell}\cap X\cap G;\mathbf{x}))\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\Big\},

(7.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{alg}}[O_{j};\mathbf{x}]

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}[O_{j}]\setminus\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{tiny}}[O_{j};\mathbf{x}],

where
C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\geq 1
is a constant which depends only on the quantity
M_{1}
in (
6.18
). Thus, we may dominate the right-hand side of (
7.9
) by

(7.11)

\int_{X\cap G}\Big|\sum_{\vec{T}\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{tiny}}[O_{j};\mathbf{x}]}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\chi_{T_{\ell}}(\mathbf{x})\Big|^{p/k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{X\cap G}\Big|\sum_{\vec{T}\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{alg}}[O_{j};\mathbf{x}]}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\chi_{T_{\ell}}(\mathbf{x})\Big|^{p/k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

If the first term in (
7.11
) is the largest of the two terms, then we shall see that the stopping condition
[tiny]
is met. If the second term is the largest, then we shall see
[algebraic]
is met. Either way, we shall arrive at a contradiction and thereby conclude the proof of the claim. To this end, we let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}
denote the collection of cells
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}
for which the second term in (
7.11
) is the largest of the two terms. The analysis now divides into two cases.

\blacktriangleright
Tiny-dominant case.
Suppose that the cells in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
do not dominate. More precisely, suppose that

(7.12)

\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\leq 4\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}.

Fix
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
and let
G=G_{O_{j}}
be the associated grain, satisfying (
7.9
). By the definition of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
, we have

(7.13)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\lesssim\int_{X\cap G}\Big|\sum_{\vec{T}\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{tiny}}[O_{j};\mathbf{x}]}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\chi_{T_{\ell}}(\mathbf{x})\Big|^{p/k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{G}
be a finitely-overlapping cover of
G
by balls of radius
\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}/2
. We let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j}):=\{B\cap X\cap G:B\in\mathcal{B}_{G}\}
, so that the right-hand side of (
7.13
) is bounded by

\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j})}\int_{O}\Big|\sum_{\vec{T}\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{tiny}}[O_{j};\mathbf{x}]}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\chi_{T_{\ell}}(\mathbf{x})\Big|^{p/k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

For each term in the sum, corresponding to a choice of
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j})
, we relax the summation over all tuples
\vec{T}=(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{tiny}}[O_{j};\mathbf{x}]
to summing over tuples
\vec{T}=(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})\in\mathbb{T}_{1}[O]\times\cdots\times\mathbb{T}_{k}[O]
where

\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]:=\Big\{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]:\exists\,\mathbf{x}\in T_{\ell}\cap O\textrm{ s.t. }\mathrm{diam}(\mathrm{CC}(T_{\ell}\cap X\cap G;\mathbf{x}))\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\Big\}.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
denote the disjoint union of the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j})
over all
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
. We shall show that this collection, along with the tube families
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]
introduced above, realise the stopping condition
[tiny]
. Note that
\mathrm{diam}\,O<\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
automatically holds for all
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
, so it remains to demonstrate Property I
tiny
, II
tiny
and III
tiny
.

Property I
tiny
.
By (
7.12
) and the preceding observations,

\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\leq C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O)}^{p},

provided
C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)\geq 1
is chosen sufficiently large, depending only on admissible parameters. This establishes Property I
tiny
.

Property II
tiny
.
We claim that, to prove Property II
tiny
, it suffices to show

(7.14)

\#\big\{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j}):T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\big\}\lesssim_{C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},d,E}1,\qquad T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}],\;O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}.

Indeed, it then follows that

\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j})}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]=\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\#\big\{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j}):T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\big\}\lesssim_{C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},d,E}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]

for all
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
. Summing, we obtain

\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]=\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}}\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j})}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\leq C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}],

provided
C_{\mathrm{tiny}}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d)\geq 1
is chosen sufficiently large, depending only on
d
,
C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
and admissible parameters (since
d\geq E
). This establishes Property II
tiny
.

Turning to the proof of (
7.14
), fix
O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j,\mathrm{wall}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
and
T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]
and let
G=G_{O_{j}}
be the grain associated to
O_{j}
. The goal is to show

(7.15)

\#\big\{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j}):\exists\,\mathbf{x}\in T_{\ell}\cap O\textrm{ s.t. }\mathrm{diam}(\mathrm{CC}(T_{\ell}\cap X\cap G;\mathbf{x}))\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\big\}\lesssim_{C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},d,E}1.

It is a simple consequence of the Tarski–Seidenberg projection theorem (see Theorem
A.4
) that
T_{\ell}
and
G
are both semialgebraic of complexity
O_{d,E}(1)
. It immediately follows that
T_{\ell}\cap X\cap G
is also semialgebraic of complexity
O_{d,E}(1)
. Thus, by the main theorem in
[
2
]
, the set
T_{\ell}\cap X\cap G
has
O_{d,E}(1)
connected components. Let
W
be one such connected component and further suppose that
\mathrm{diam}\,W\leq C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
. Since
\mathcal{B}_{G}
is a finitely-overlapping family of balls of radius
\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}/2
, it follows that
\#\{B\in\mathcal{B}_{G}:W\cap B\neq\emptyset\}\lesssim_{C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}1
. Thus,
W
intersects at most
O_{C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}(1)
of the sets
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j})
. Combining these observations, there can be at most
O_{C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},d,E}(1)
sets
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j})
which intersect some connected component of
T_{\ell}\cap X\cap G
of diameter at most
C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
. This is precisely the desired bound (
7.15
).

Property III
tiny
.
Given
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}(O_{j})
, by the definitions we see that
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]
and so Property III
tiny
is immediate.

Thus, we see that the stopping condition
[tiny]
is met, contrary to our earlier assumption.

\blacktriangleright
Algebraic-dominant case.
Now suppose that (
7.12
) fails. In light of (
7.8
), it follows that

(7.16)

\sum_{O_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{j}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O_{j}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O_{j})}^{p}\leq 4\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O)}^{p}.

Fix
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
and let
G=G_{O}
be the associated grain, satisfying (
7.9
). By the definition of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{alg}}
, we have

(7.17)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O)}^{p}\lesssim\int_{X\cap G}\Big|\sum_{\vec{T}\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{alg}}[O;\mathbf{x}]}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\chi_{T_{\ell}}(\mathbf{x})\Big|^{p/k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

Comparing the definition of
\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{alg}}[O;\mathbf{x}]
from (
7.10
) with the definition of the sets
\mathbb{T}_{s}[G]
from (
7.3
), we see that

(7.18)

\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{alg}}[O;\mathbf{x}]\subseteq\bigcup_{s=1}^{k}\Bigg(\prod_{\ell=1}^{s-1}\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\times\mathbb{T}_{s}[G]\times\prod_{\ell=s+1}^{k}\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\Bigg).

Indeed, to verify (
7.18
), it suffices to show that if
T\in\mathbb{T}
is such that
T\cap X\cap G
has a connected component with diameter larger than
C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
, then
|T\cap X\cap N_{2\delta}G|\geq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}|T|
. Assuming there exists such a component, matters further reduce to finding some
t_{0}<t_{1}
such that
|t_{1}-t_{0}|>2\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
and
T\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[t_{0},t_{1}])\subseteq X\cap N_{2\delta}G
. Since connected semialgebraic sets are path connected (see, for instance,
[
1
, Theorem 5.23]
), there exist continuous functions
\overline{x}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
\overline{t}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
t_{0}:=\overline{t}(0)
,
t_{1}:=\overline{t}(1)
satisfy
t_{0}\leq t_{1}
and

(\overline{x}(\sigma),\overline{t}(\sigma))\in T\cap X\cap G,\quad\sigma\in[0,1],\quad\big(|\overline{x}(0)-\overline{x}(1)|^{2}+|\overline{t}(0)-\overline{t}(1)|^{2}\big)^{1/2}>C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}.

We show that
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
satisfy the desired properties.

•

By the mean value theorem and the definition of
T
, we have

|\overline{x}(0)-\overline{x}(1)|\leq\|\gamma_{T}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}|t_{0}-t_{1}|+2\delta.

Thus,
|t_{0}-t_{1}|>2\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
provided the constant
C_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
in (
7.10
) is chosen appropriately.

•

Given any
(x,t)\in T
with
\overline{t}(0)\leq t\leq\overline{t}(1)
, by the intermediate value theorem there exists some
\sigma\in[0,1]
such that
(\overline{x}(\sigma),t)\in T\cap X\cap G
. In particular,
|\overline{x}(\sigma)-\gamma_{T}(t)|<\delta
, where
(\overline{x}(\sigma),t)\in G
. Thus,
|x-\overline{x}(\sigma)|<2\delta
, and so
(x,t)\in N_{2\delta}G
. On the other hand,
[\overline{t}(0),\overline{t}(1)]\subseteq I
and so
(x,t)\in X
.

In light of (
7.18
), we may bound

(7.19)

\int_{X\cap G}\Big|\sum_{\vec{T}\in\mathbb{T}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{alg}}[O;\mathbf{x}]}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\chi_{T_{\ell}}(\mathbf{x})\Big|^{p/k}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\lesssim\sum_{s=1}^{k}\Big\|\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{s}[G]}\big|^{1/k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\ell\leq k\\
\ell\neq s\end{subarray}}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G)}^{p}.

Combining (
7.19
) with (
7.17
) and (
7.16
), we obtain an inequality of a similar shape to that of (
7.2
). In particular, provided
C_{\mathrm{alg}}
is chosen sufficiently large, the stopping condition
[algebraic]
is met, contrary to our assumption.

In either the tiny-dominant or algebraic-dominant case we arrive at a contradiction. Thus, our initial assumption (
7.8
) must be false and therefore the claimed inequality (
7.4
) holds.
∎

The algorithm must terminate after at most
\lceil\log_{2}\delta^{-1}\rceil
steps. Indeed, suppose
J:=\lceil\log_{2}\delta^{-1}\rceil
and the algorithm has reached the
J
th step. The cells
O_{J}\in\mathcal{O}_{J}
have diameter at most
2^{1-J}\rho\leq 2\delta<\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
. Defining
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}:=\mathcal{O}_{J}
, it follows that Property I
tiny
, II
tiny
and III
tiny
vacuously hold with
j=J
. Hence, we realise all the conditions of stopping condition
[tiny]
and the algorithm terminates.

The second algorithm.
The algorithm
[partition
m\to m+1
]
is now applied repeatedly to arrive at a final decomposition. This process forms part of a second algorithm, referred to as
[partition]
.

Input

[partition]
will take as its input:

•

A set
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
defining a uniform family of polynomial curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

An integer
2\leq k\leq n
, parameters
\frac{k+1}{k}<p<\frac{k}{k-1}
,
0<\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{\circ}
,
\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}<1
,
0<\delta\leq 1
and
1\leq E\leq d
. Furthermore,
\varepsilon_{\circ}
is chosen sufficiently small, depending on
\varepsilon
,
p
, and
d
is chosen large, depending on
\varepsilon_{\circ}
and
E
.

•

A family
\mathbb{T}
of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes of degree bounded by
E
, satisfying
\#\mathbb{T}\leq\delta^{-(n-1)}
.

•

A set
X:=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times I
for some choice of open interval
I\subseteq(-1,1)
.

•

Subfamilies
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
which are
\nu
-transverse on
X
for some
\nu>0
. We also assume that the subfamilies are non-degenerate in the sense that

(7.20)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X)}\geq\mathbf{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon,d}\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)-\varepsilon}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}

for

(7.21)

\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p):=(k/p-k+1)\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}>0.

Here
\mathbf{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon,d}\geq 1
is a constant depending only on admissible parameters and
\varepsilon
,
d
and
M_{1}
from (
6.18
), chosen sufficiently large so as to satisfy the forthcoming requirements of the argument, and
A=A_{k,n,p}\geq 1
is as in the statement of Theorem
6.11
.

In contrast with the previous algorithm, we now assume that the
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}
are
transversal
families. However, the direction-separated and centre-separated hypotheses still do not appear. The hypothesis (
7.20
) is no real constraint: if this condition fails, then we shall see Theorem
6.13
essentially holds by default, provided
\varepsilon>0
is chosen sufficiently small. We remark that the
\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)
exponent corresponds to the gain in Theorem
6.11
when we localise to a ball of radius
r:=\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
.

To describe the output, we introduce some further terminology.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
G
be a grain and
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G]\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
be subfamilies of tubes. We say
(\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G])_{\ell=1}^{k}

intersects significantly with
X\cap G
if there exists some
1\leq s\leq k
such that

|T_{s}\cap X\cap N_{2\delta}G|\geq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}|T_{s}|\qquad\textrm{for all $T_{s}\in\mathbb{T}_{s}[G]$.}

Output

For
0\leq m\leq n-k
, the
m
th step of the recursion will produce:

•

An
m+1
-tuple of:

–

scales
(\rho_{0},\dots,\rho_{m})
satisfying
1=:\rho_{0}\geq\rho_{1}\geq\dots\geq\rho_{m}\geq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
;

–

parameters
(D_{0},\dots,D_{m})
with
1=:D_{0}\leq D_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq m
;

Each
m
-tuple is formed by adjoining a component to the corresponding
(m-1)
-tuple from the previous stage.

•

A family
\mathcal{G}_{m}
of
(\delta,\rho_{m},d_{m})
-grains of codimension
m
, where
d_{m}:=(C_{\deg})^{m}d
,
for
C_{\deg}
the constant appearing in Theorem
7.1
.

•

\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
a subfamily of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes, for each
G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}
and
1\leq\ell\leq k
, such that
(\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}])_{\ell=1}^{k}
intersects significantly with
X\cap G_{m}
.

These objects are chosen so that the following properties hold:

Property 1

(1)_{m}

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X)}^{p}\lesssim\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}^{\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\delta^{-m\varepsilon_{\circ}}\sum_{G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}^{p}.

Property 2
For
m>0
,

(2)_{m}

\sum_{G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]\lesssim\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}^{1+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}.

Property 3
For
m>0
,

(3)_{m}

\max_{G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]\lesssim\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}^{-(n-i)+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}.

Initial step

For the
m=0
step, we take
\mathcal{G}_{0}:=\{\mathbb{B}^{n}\}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[\mathbb{B}^{n}]:=\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
, noting that the unit ball is a
(\delta,1,0)
-grain of codimension
0
and that the families
(\mathbb{T}_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{k}
of tubes intersect significantly with
\mathbb{B}^{n}
. With these definitions, Property 1, 2 and 3 all hold vacuously.

(
m+1
)th step

Let
0\leq m\leq n-k-1
and suppose that the recursive algorithm has ran through
m
steps. We therefore have constructed a family
\mathcal{G}_{m}
of
(\delta,\rho_{m},d_{m})
-grains of codimension
m
and subfamilies
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes, for each
G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}
and
1\leq\ell\leq k
, satisfying Properties 1, 2 and 3. We show how to construct the next generation of grains and tube families.

For each
G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}
, provided our input
d
is large enough, we may apply
[partition
m\to m+1
]
with degree
d_{m}
to bound the norm

(7.22)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}.

One of two things can happen: either
[partition
m\to m+1
]
terminates due to the stopping condition
[tiny]
or it terminates due to the stopping condition
[algebraic]
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{tiny}}
denote the set of all grains
G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}
for which
[partition
m\to m+1
]
terminates due to
[tiny]
and
\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}:=\mathcal{G}_{m}\setminus\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{tiny}}
.

Claim 7.5
.

For
0\leq m\leq n-k-1
, the grains
\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}
dominate, in the sense that

(7.23)

\sum_{G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}^{p}\leq 2\sum_{G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}^{p}.

We shall temporarily assume the above claim and show how to construct the grains
\mathcal{G}_{m+1}
. Let
G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}
, so that
[partition
m\to m+1
]
applied to (
7.22
) does not terminate owing to
[tiny]
and therefore terminates owing to
[algebraic]
. Consequently,

(7.24)

\displaystyle\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim D_{m+1}^{\varepsilon_{\circ}}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}_{m+1}[G_{m}]}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G)}^{p};

(7.25)

\displaystyle\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}_{m+1}[G_{m}]}\#\mathbb{T}[G]

\displaystyle\lesssim D_{m+1}^{1+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\#\mathbb{T}[G_{m}];

(7.26)

\displaystyle\max_{G\in\mathcal{G}_{m+1}[G_{m}]}\#\mathbb{T}[G]

\displaystyle\lesssim D_{m+1}^{-(n-m-1)+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\#\mathbb{T}[G_{m}]

all hold for some choice of:

•

Scale
\rho_{m+1}
satisfying
\rho_{m}\geq\rho_{m+1}\geq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
and parameter
D_{m+1}
;

•

Family
\mathcal{G}_{m+1}[G_{m}]
of
(\delta,\rho_{m+1},d_{m+1})
-
grains of codimension
m+1
;

•

\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G]\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
a subfamily of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes, for each grain
G\in\mathcal{G}_{m+1}[G_{m}]
and
1\leq\ell\leq k
, such that
(\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G])_{\ell=1}^{k}
intersects significantly with
X\cap G
.

Each inequality (
7.24
), (
7.25
) and (
7.26
) is obtained by combining the definition of the stopping condition
[algebraic]
with Properties I, II and III from
[partition
m\to m+1
]
, respectively. Indeed, if
J
denotes the stopping time for the application of
[partition
m\to m+1
]
, then we take

r_{0}:=2\rho_{m},\qquad\rho_{m+1}:=r_{J}/2:=2^{-J}\rho_{m}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad D_{m+1}:=d_{m}^{J},

using the notation from
[partition
m\to m+1
]
.

Remark 7.6
.

The sets
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G]
do not necessarily coincide with the sets defined (
7.3
). The two sets will coincide for one choice of index
\ell=s
but for the remaining indices
\ell\neq s
the
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G]
will coincide with the sets
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]
as in (
7.2
).

The
\rho_{m+1}
and
D_{m+1}
depend on the choice of
G_{m}
, but this dependence can be essentially removed by pigeonholing. In particular, the stopping time
J
depends on
G_{m}
, but satisfies
J=O(\log\delta^{-1})
. Thus, since there are only logarithmically many possible different values, one may find a subset
\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}^{\prime}
of the
\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}
over which the stopping time
J
has a common value and, moreover, the inequality (
7.24
) holds with
\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}
replaced with
\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}^{\prime}
and an additional factor of
\delta^{-\varepsilon_{\circ}}
on the right-hand side. Since both
\rho_{m+1}
and
D_{m+1}
are determined by
J
, the desired uniformity is immediately inherited by these parameters.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{m+1}
denote the union of the sets
\mathcal{G}_{m+1}[G_{m}]
over all
G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m,\mathrm{alg}}^{\prime}
. It remains to observe that the desired properties hold for the newly constructed data:

•

(1)_{m+1}
in Property 1 follows from (
7.24
) (with an additional factor of
\delta^{-\varepsilon_{\circ}}
, as per the above discussion) and
(1)_{m}
;

•

(2)_{m+1}
in Property 2 follows from (
7.25
) and
(2)_{m}
;

•

(3)_{m+1}
in Property 3 follows from (
7.26
) and
(3)_{m}
.

This concludes the description of the recursive step of
[partition]
, except that it remains to justify Claim
7.5
.

Proof (of Claim
7.5
).

If the condition (
7.23
) of the claim fails, then

\sum_{G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}^{p}\leq 2\sum_{G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m,\textrm{{tiny}}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[G_{m}]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G_{m})}^{p}

holds, where the right-hand summation is restricted to those
G_{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{m}
for which
[partition
m\to m+1
]
terminates owing to the stopping condition
[tiny]
.

By combining
(\mathrm{I})_{J}
(where
J
denotes the stopping time) with Property I
tiny
, II
tiny
and III
tiny
from
[partition
m\to m+1
]
, we deduce that there exist

•

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
a family of measurable subsets of
X
with
\mathrm{diam}\,O\leq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
for all
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
;

•

\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]
a subfamily of
\delta
-tubes for each
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
and
1\leq\ell\leq k

such that

(7.27)

\displaystyle\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X)}^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}D_{i}^{\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\delta^{-m\varepsilon_{\circ}}\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O)}^{p};

(7.28)

\displaystyle\sum_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}D_{i}^{1+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell};

(7.29)

\displaystyle\max_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}D_{i}^{-(n-i-\varepsilon_{\circ})}\Big)\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}.

Note that here we have also used the properties
(1)_{m}
,
(2)_{m}
and
(3)_{m}
from the previous stage of the algorithm. The implicit constant depends on
M_{1}
from (
6.18
), but is otherwise independent of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
.

Fixing
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
, we apply the universal multilinear estimate from Theorem
6.11
to the corresponding term in the right-hand sum in (
7.27
). Since
\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\ell}
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
, it follows that
\mathbb{T}_{1}[O],\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}[O]
are
\nu
-transverse on
X
. Furthermore, the cell
O
is contained in a ball of radius
4\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}
. Thus, for all
\varepsilon>0
, and in particular for the value appearing in the non-degeneracy condition (
7.20
), Theorem
6.11
implies

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O)}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon}\nu^{-A}\delta^{k-1+(n-k)/p+\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)-\varepsilon/2}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\big)^{1/k},

where
\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)
is as defined in (
7.21
) and the dependence on
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is as above.

Using (
7.29
) and the bound
\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}\leq\#\mathbb{T}\leq\delta^{-(n-1)}
, we estimate

\displaystyle\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]

\displaystyle\leq\big(\max_{O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\big)^{(1-1/p)}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\big)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}D_{i}^{-(n-i-\varepsilon_{\circ})(1-1/p)}\Big)\delta^{-(n-1)(1-1/p)}\big(\#\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]\big)^{1/p}.

We therefore conclude the cell-wise bound

(7.30)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(O)}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d,\varepsilon}M(\delta;D)\delta^{-\varepsilon/2}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}[O]}|T|\Big)^{1/kp}

for

M(\delta;D):=\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}D_{i}^{-(n-i-\varepsilon_{\circ})(1-1/p)}\Big).

Now take the
\ell^{p}
-norm of both sides of (
7.30
) over all
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tiny}}
. By applying Hölder’s inequality to the resulting expression and (
7.27
), (
7.28
), we obtain

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X)}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},d,\varepsilon}M(\delta;D)\delta^{-\varepsilon}\big(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}D_{i}^{(1+\varepsilon_{\circ})/p+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p},

where we have assumed that
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
is sufficiently small so that the
\delta^{-\varepsilon_{\circ}}
powers can be absorbed into the
\delta^{-\varepsilon}
factor.

Note that

M(\delta;D)\big(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}D_{i}^{(1+\varepsilon_{\circ})/p+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)=\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{m+1}D_{i}^{-(n-i)+(n-i+1)/p+2\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big).

Provided
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
is chosen sufficiently small, using the hypothesis
0\leq m\leq n-k-1
, we can ensure

\frac{n-i+1}{n-i}\leq\frac{k+1}{k}<p\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad(n-i)-\frac{n-i+1}{p}>2\varepsilon_{\circ}\quad\textrm{for $1\leq i\leq m+1$.}

Thus, we conclude that

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X)}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon,d}\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)-\varepsilon}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}.

However, provided
\mathbf{C}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon,d}
in (
7.20
) is chosen sufficiently large, we see that this contradicts the non-degeneracy hypothesis. This contradiction arose from assuming (
7.23
) fails, so (
7.23
) must hold.
∎

7.3.
The proof of the multilinear Kakeya/Nikodym estimate

We now apply the algorithm developed in the previous two subsections to conclude the proof of Theorem
6.13
.

Proof (of Theorem
6.13
).

Let
2\leq k\leq n
and
\kappa>0
,
B=(B_{1},B_{2})\in[0,\infty)^{2}
and fix
\frac{k+1}{k}<p<\frac{k}{k-1}
. Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
defines a uniform family of
C^{\infty}
curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and, for
0<\delta<1
, that
\mathbb{T}
is a family of
(\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\delta)
-tubes which is
(\kappa,B)
-non-concentrated in codimension
(n-k)
grains. Let
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}\subset\mathbb{T}
be
\nu
-transverse families for some
\nu>0
.

The first step is to form a family of polynomial approximants to the curves in
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
. In particular, fix
\varepsilon>0
an admissible parameter, chosen sufficiently small to satsify the forthcoming requirements of the proof. By pigeonholing there exists an open interval
I\subseteq(-1,1)
of length
\delta^{\varepsilon}
such that

(7.31)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\delta^{-\varepsilon}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X)}\qquad\textrm{for $X:=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times I$.}

Here we use the fact that each
T\in\mathbb{T}
satisfies
T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(-1,1)
.

Write
I=(a,a+\delta^{\varepsilon})
for some
a\in(-1,1)
and define

N(\varepsilon):=\lceil 100n/\varepsilon\rceil
. For each
C^{\infty}
map
\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, let
\widetilde{\gamma}
denote the degree
N(\varepsilon)
Taylor polynomial of
\gamma
centred at
a
and define
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}:=\{\widetilde{\gamma}:\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\}
. We may assume without loss of generality that
\delta
is chosen sufficiently small, depending on the constants
(M_{N})_{N=1}^{N(\varepsilon)}
from (
6.18
). In particular, we can ensure that

(7.32)

|\gamma(t)-\widetilde{\gamma}(t)|<\delta\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\gamma^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(t)|<\delta\quad\textrm{for all $t\in I$ and all $\gamma\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}$.}

If
X:=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times I
, then it follows that

(7.33)

T\cap X\subseteq\widetilde{T}:=\big\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}\times(-1,1):|x-\widetilde{\gamma}_{T}(t)|<2\delta\big\}.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}:=\{\widetilde{T}:T\in\mathbb{T}\}
is a family of
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},2\delta)
-tubes.

We shall apply
[partition]
with the following inputs:

•

The set
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
, which defines a family of polynomial curves in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

The parameters
2\leq k\leq n
,
\frac{k+1}{k}<p<\frac{k}{k-1}
and
\delta>0
as above. We further choose

(7.34)

\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}:=\frac{\kappa}{2(B_{1}+B_{2})}>0

and take
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
to be small, depending on
p
and
\varepsilon
, and
d\in\mathbb{N}
to be large, depending on
\varepsilon_{\circ}
and
N(\varepsilon)
.

•

The set
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}:=\{\widetilde{T}:T\in\mathbb{T}\}
, which is a family of
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},2\delta)
-tubes of degree bounded by
N(\varepsilon)
and satisfies
\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}\leq\delta^{-(n-1)}
.

•

The set
X:=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times I
as above.

•

The subfamilies
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}:=\{\widetilde{T}_{\ell}:T\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}\}
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
. Since the original tube families
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{k}
are
\nu
-transversal, it follows from the derivative bounds in (
7.32
) that
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{k}
are
\nu/2
-transverse on
X
.

The quantities
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
are chosen admissibly, so as to ensure they satisfy the conditions required by
[partition]
and also the forthcoming requirements of the proof. To run
[partition]
, we shall temporarily assume that the tube families
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{k}
satisfy the non-degeneracy hypothesis (
7.20
), with the fixed parameter
\varepsilon>0
as introduced above.

From the output of the
n-k
stage, we deduce that there exist

•

\mathcal{G}
a family of
(\delta,\rho,d)
-grains of codimension
n-k
for some
\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\leq\rho\leq 1
;

•

\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]\subseteq\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}
subfamilies of
\delta
-tubes for each
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
1\leq\ell\leq k

with the following properties:

Property 1
.

(7.35)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\,\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X)}^{p}\lesssim\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\delta^{-(n-k)\varepsilon_{\circ}}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\,\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G)}^{p}.

Property 2
.

(7.36)

\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]\lesssim\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{1+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\#\mathbb{T}.

Property 3
.

(7.37)

\max_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]\lesssim\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{-(n-i)+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\#\mathbb{T}.

Furthermore, we have the following crucial feature of the
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]
.

Property: significant intersection
. For each
G\in\mathcal{G}
, there exists
1\leq s=s_{G}\leq k
such that

(7.38)

|\widetilde{T}\cap X\cap N_{2\delta}G|\geq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}|\widetilde{T}|\qquad\textrm{for all $\widetilde{T}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]$.}

Fixing
G\in\mathcal{G}
, since
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]\subseteq\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}
for
1\leq\ell\leq k
, it follows that
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}[G],\dots,\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{k}[G]
are
\nu/2
-transverse on
X
. Thus, the universal multilinear estimate from Theorem
6.11
implies that

(7.39)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\,\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G)}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon}\nu^{-A}\delta^{k-1+(n-k)/p-\varepsilon/3}\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big(\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]\big)^{1/k}.

It is convenient to consider the union
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}[G]:=\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}[G]\cup\dots\cup\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{k}[G]
. Let
