s=s_{G}
, so that the tube family
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]
satisfies (
7.38
). By our hypotheses on
p
, we have
1-1/p<1/k
and may therefore bound

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big(\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]\big)^{1/k}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]\big)^{1-1/p}\big(\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]\big)^{1/p-(k-1)/k}\big(\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}[G]\big)^{(k-1)/k}

(7.40)

\displaystyle\leq\big(\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]\big)^{1-1/p}\big(\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}[G]\big)^{1/p}.

In view of (
7.32
), we have
\widetilde{T}\cap X\subseteq 2\cdot T
for each
T\in\mathbb{T}
, where
2\cdot T
denotes the tube which shares the same core curve as
T
, but with width
2\delta
rather than
\delta
. In particular, (
7.38
) implies that

|2\cdot T\cap N_{2\delta}G|\geq\delta^{\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}}|T|\qquad\textrm{whenever $T\in\mathbb{T}$ is such that $\widetilde{T}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]$.}

Since we are assuming
\mathbb{T}
is
(B,\kappa)
-non-concentrated in codimension
n-k
grains, recalling (
6.21
), we conclude that

(7.41)

\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]\lesssim_{d,\varepsilon}\delta^{-\kappa_{\mathrm{tiny}}(B_{1}+B_{2})}\delta^{-(n-1)+\kappa-\varepsilon/3}\leq\delta^{-(n-1)+\kappa/2-\varepsilon/3}

where we used the definition (
7.34
). Recall, we also have the bound from (
7.37
) for
\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]
. Thus, given
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
, we may take a weighted geometric mean of these bounds (
7.37
) and (
7.41
) to deduce that

\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{s}[G]\lesssim_{d,\varepsilon}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{-(n-i-\varepsilon_{\circ})\sigma}\Big)\delta^{\kappa(1-\sigma)/2}\delta^{-(n-1)-\varepsilon/3}.

Combining this with (
7.39
) and (
7.40
), we obtain

(7.42)

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\mu_{\,\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\ell}[G]}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(X\cap G)}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon}M(\delta;D)\delta^{-2\varepsilon/3}\Big(\sum_{T\in\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}[G]}|T|\Big)^{1/p}

where

M(\delta;D):=\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta_{\mathrm{alg}}(\sigma,p)}\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{-(n-i-\varepsilon_{\circ})\sigma(1-1/p)}

for

\eta_{\mathrm{alg}}(\sigma,p):=\frac{\kappa(1-\sigma)}{2p^{\prime}}.

On the other hand, by (
7.36
), we have

(7.43)

\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\#\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}[G]\lesssim\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{1+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\#\mathbb{T}.

We may assume that
0<\varepsilon_{\circ}<\varepsilon/(3n)
. Taking the
\ell^{p}
sum of both sides of (
7.42
) over all
G\in\mathcal{G}
and combining the resulting inequality with (
7.31
), (
7.35
) and (
7.43
), we then obtain

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon}M(\delta;D)\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{(1+\varepsilon_{\circ})/p+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\delta^{-\varepsilon}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}

where

M(\delta;D)\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{(1+\varepsilon_{\circ})/p+\varepsilon_{\circ}}\Big)\leq\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta_{\mathrm{alg}}(\sigma,p)}\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}D_{i}^{-(n-i)\sigma+((n-i)\sigma+1)/p+2\varepsilon_{\circ}}.

Recall that
\frac{k+1}{k}<p<\frac{k}{k-1}
. Thus, provided
\varepsilon_{\circ}>0
is chosen sufficiently small (depending on
p
), we can find some
0<\sigma_{\circ}<1
such that
k\sigma_{\circ}-(k\sigma_{\circ}+1)/p\geq 2\varepsilon_{\circ}
. This further implies that

(n-i)\sigma_{\circ}-\frac{(n-i)\sigma_{\circ}+1}{p}\geq 2\varepsilon_{\circ}\qquad\textrm{for $1\leq i\leq n-k$.}

Since
1-\sigma_{\circ}>0
and
p>1
, it follows that
\eta_{\mathrm{alg}}(p):=\eta_{\mathrm{alg}}(\sigma_{\circ},p)>0
. Thus, by choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, we can ensure that

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta_{\mathrm{alg}}(p)/2}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}.

All of the above is under the hypothesis that the tube families
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{k}
satisfy the non-degeneracy hypothesis (
7.20
). Suppose this condition is not satisfied. In this case, using the containment (
7.33
) and the pigeonholing (
7.31
), we have

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/k}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varepsilon}\nu^{-A}\delta^{-(n-k)/p^{\prime}+\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)-2\varepsilon}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}.

Thus, by choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small in the above estimate, in either case we obtain the desired bound for
\frac{k+1}{k}<p<\frac{k}{k-1}
with the exponent

\eta(p):=\frac{\min\{\eta_{\mathrm{alg}}(p),\eta_{\mathrm{tiny}}(p)\}}{2}.

Finally, we extend the range of
p
by interpolating with the trivial
L^{1}
bound.
∎

8.
Geometric maximal estimates III: Concluding the argument

In this section we apply the somewhat general theory of the previous two sections to conclude the proof of Theorem
2.9
.

8.1.
Linear estimates under sublevel set hypotheses

Recall the definition of
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;m;\kappa)
and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;m;\kappa)
from Definition
4.1
and the critical codimension
m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n)
from (
4.2
). Using duality, we first note that Theorem
2.9
is implied by the following proposition.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
be odd. For all
1<p<p^{\prime}(n)
and
0<\kappa\leq 1
, there exists some
0<\beta_{\kappa}(p^{\prime})<\beta(n;p^{\prime})
such that the following holds. For
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
a real analytic, translation-invariant Hörmander-type phase:

I)

\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n);\kappa)\Rightarrow\mathbf{K}_{p\to p}^{*}(\phi;\beta_{\kappa}(p^{\prime}))
;

II)

\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n);\kappa)\Rightarrow\mathbf{N}_{p\to p}^{*}\big(\phi;\beta_{\kappa}(p^{\prime}))
.

Proof (Proposition
8.1

\Rightarrow
Theorem
2.9
).

By the duality (
6.2
) and interpolation, Theorem
2.9
is equivalent to the following statement. For all
1<p<p^{\prime}(n)
there exists some
\beta_{\phi}(p^{\prime})<\beta(n;p^{\prime})
such that

I)

If
\phi
satisfies Hypothesis I), then
\mathbf{K}_{p\to p}^{*}(\phi;\beta_{\phi}(p^{\prime}))
holds;

II)

If
\phi
satisfies Hypothesis II), then
\mathbf{N}_{p\to p}^{*}(\phi;\beta_{\phi}(p^{\prime}))
holds.

On the other hand, we know from Proposition
4.2
that

I)

Under Hypothesis I),
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n);\kappa)
holds for some
0<\kappa\leq 1
;

II)

Under Hypothesis II),
\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n);\kappa)
holds for some
0<\kappa\leq 1
.

Combining these facts concludes the reduction.
∎

8.2.
Mutlilinear estimates under sublevel set hypotheses

By the multilinear reduction in Proposition
6.5
, we may reduce Proposition
8.1
to the following multilinear estimate.

Proposition 8.2

(Multilinear Kakeya/Nikodym estimate)
.

Let
n\geq 3
be odd and
1\leq m\leq n-2
. For all
1<p<\frac{n-m}{n-m-1}
and
0<\kappa\leq n-1
, there exists some
0<\beta_{m,\kappa}(p^{\prime})<m/p^{\prime}
such that the following holds. For
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
a real analytic, translation-invariant Hörmander-type phase:

I)

\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;m;\kappa)\Rightarrow\mathbf{M}\mathbf{K}_{p,n-m}^{*}(\phi;\beta_{m,\kappa}(p^{\prime}))
;

II)

\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;m;\kappa)\Rightarrow\mathbf{M}\mathbf{N}_{p,n-m}^{*}(\phi;\beta_{m,\kappa}(p^{\prime}))
.

Proof (Proposition
8.2

\Rightarrow
Proposition
8.1
).

If we choose
m=m_{\mathrm{crit}}(n)
, then for
n\geq 3
we have
m\leq n-2
and

\frac{n-m}{n-m-1}=p^{\prime}(n),\qquad n-m=d_{\mathrm{crit}}(n)<\frac{n+3}{2},\qquad\frac{m}{p^{\prime}}=\beta(n;p^{\prime}),

using the parity of
n
. The reduction now immediately follows from Proposition
6.5
.
∎

Combining this with the reduction from §
8.1
, to prove Theorem
2.9
, it suffices to show Proposition
8.2
. However, the latter is a direct consequence of the multilinear theory developed in §
7
and the non-concentration properties established in §
4
.

Proof (of Proposition
8.2
).

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a family of
\delta
-tubes associated to
\phi
. Suppose that we are in either one of the following cases:

I)

\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{K}}(\phi;m;\kappa)
holds and
\mathbb{T}
is direction-separated;

II)

\mathbf{S}_{\mathrm{N}}(\phi;m;\kappa)
holds and
\mathbb{T}
is centre-separated.

By Theorem
4.6
, there exists some
\kappa_{\circ}>0
and
B\in[0,\infty)^{2}
such that
\mathbb{T}
is
(\kappa_{\circ},B)
-non-concentrated on codimension
m
grains. We may therefore apply Theorem
6.13
to the family of tubes
\mathbb{T}
with
k:=n-m
. From this we conclude that, for all
1<p<\frac{n-m}{n-m-1}
there exists some
\eta(p)>0
such that the inequality

\Big\|\prod_{\ell=1}^{n-m}\big|\sum_{T_{\ell}\in\mathbb{T}_{\ell}}\chi_{T_{\ell}}\big|^{1/(n-m)}\Big\|_{L^{p}(Q)}\lesssim_{\phi}K^{A}\delta^{-m/p^{\prime}+\eta(p)}\Big(\sum_{T\in\mathbb{T}}|T|\Big)^{1/p}

holds whenever
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{K^{-1}}
is a cube and
\mathbb{T}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{T}_{n-m}\subset\mathbb{T}
are
K^{-(n-m-1)}
-transversal on
Q
for some
K\geq 1
. Note that Theorem
6.13
involves a global estimate, but the localised version easily follows by a rescaling argument. Letting
\beta_{m,\kappa}(p^{\prime}):=m/p^{\prime}-\eta(p)<m/p^{\prime}
, the desired result follows from the definitions.
∎

9.
From geometric maximal functions to oscillatory operators

In this section we discuss the proof of Proposition
2.10
, which follows a classical argument of Bourgain
[
7
,
8
]
.

9.1.
Universal estimates revisited

We begin by clarifying the proof of the universal estimates in Theorem
1.3
, which was briefly sketched in §
1
.

Proof (of Theorem
1.3
).

Since I) follows directly by interpolating classical estimates of Hörmander
[
26
]
and Stein
[
35
]
, we focus on II).

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Hörmander-type phase and
U^{\lambda}:=U^{\lambda}[\phi;a]
for some choice of amplitude function. By Theorem
1.3
I) and Plancherel’s theorem,

(9.1)

\|U^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{p}\lambda^{\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,q)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\qquad\textrm{for all $2\leq q\leq\infty$. }

Given
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
, we may write

U^{\lambda}f(x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}K^{\lambda}(x,t;y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

where

K^{\lambda}(x,t;y):=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}e^{i(\phi^{\lambda}(x,t;\xi)-\langle y,\xi\rangle)}a^{\lambda}(x,t;\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

There exists a constant
C_{\phi}\geq 1
such that

\big|\partial_{\xi}\big[\phi^{\lambda}(x,t;\xi)-\langle y,\xi\rangle]\big|\geq|y|-\lambda|(\partial_{\xi}\phi)(x/\lambda,t/\lambda;\xi)|\geq|y|/2\qquad\textrm{whenever $|y|\geq C\lambda$}

holds for all
(x,t;\xi)\in\mathrm{supp}\,a^{\lambda}
. A repeated integration-by-parts argument then shows that

|K^{\lambda}(x,t;y)|\lesssim_{N}|y|^{-N}\qquad\textrm{whenever $|y|\geq C\lambda$.}

Thus, we may write

(9.2)

U^{\lambda}f(x,t)=U^{\lambda}f_{\mathrm{loc}}(x,t)+\lambda^{-100n}\|f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\quad\textrm{where}\quad f_{\mathrm{loc}}:=f\cdot\chi_{B(0,C\lambda)}.

Applying (
9.1
) with
f
replaced with
f_{\mathrm{loc}}
and combining the resulting inequality with (
9.2
), we deduce that

\|U^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{q}\lambda^{\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,q)}\|f_{\mathrm{loc}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+\lambda^{-10n}\|f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

Here we have also used the fact that
U^{\lambda}f
is supported on a set of measure
O(\lambda^{n})
to control the error term. Finally, we apply Hölder’s inequality to deduce that

\|U^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{p}\lambda^{\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,q)+(n-1)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q})}\|f\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

Recalling the definition of
\alpha_{\mathrm{LS}}(n,q)
from (
1.6
), we see this is precisely the desired inequality.
∎

9.2.
Reverse square function bounds

Let
0<\rho<1
and
\Theta(\rho)
be a finitely-overlapping family of
\rho
-balls covering
\mathbb{B}^{n-1}
, with centres in the lattice
\rho\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
. We refer to the
\theta\in\Theta(\rho)
as
\rho
-caps
. Let
(\psi_{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta(\rho)}
be a smooth partition of unity adapted to the family of
\rho
-caps
\Theta(\rho)
, so that each
\psi_{\theta}
satisfies
|\partial_{y}^{\beta}\psi_{\theta}(y)|\lesssim_{\beta}\rho^{-|\beta|}
for
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n-1}
. Given
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})
, we write
f_{\theta}:=f\cdot\psi_{\theta}
for all
\theta\in\Theta(\rho)
, so that
f=\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(\rho)}f_{\theta}
.

Proposition 9.1

(Reverse square function
[
8
]
)
.

Let
1\leq R\leq\lambda
and
S^{\lambda}:=S^{\lambda}[\phi;a]
be a Hörmander-type operator. Given
\varepsilon>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
M=M(N,\varepsilon)
and symbols
a_{\theta,\gamma}
, indexed by
\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
with
|\gamma|\leq M
, satisfying

\mathrm{supp}\,a_{\theta,\gamma}\subseteq 2\cdot\theta\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}a_{\theta,\gamma}(\mathbf{x};y)|\lesssim_{N,\varepsilon,\alpha,\beta}R^{-|\beta|/2},\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n},\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n-1},

and such that for
S_{\theta,\gamma}^{\lambda}:=S^{\lambda}[\phi;a_{\theta,\gamma}]
the following holds. For all
2\leq q\leq q(n)
, we have

(9.3)

\|S^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q}(B_{R})}\lesssim_{N,\varepsilon}R^{\frac{n-1}{4}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q})+\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\
|\gamma|\leq M\end{subarray}}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}|S_{\theta,\gamma}^{\lambda}f_{\theta}|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{q}(B_{2R})}+R^{-N}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})},

for any
R
-ball
B_{R}
and
B_{2R}
is the
2R
-ball concentric to
B_{R}
.

Proposition
9.1
is a consequence of Stein’s oscillatory integral theorem
[
35
]
and essentially appears in
[
8
]
. The precise formulation used here is similar to
[
34
, Proposition 8.2.2]
. Whilst
[
34
, Proposition 8.2.2]
treats operators with homogeneous phase functions, the argument easily adapts to give the desired result for Hörmander-type operators.

9.3.
Wave packet decomposition

Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Hörmander-type phase and
1\leq R\leq\lambda
. Given
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})
, write
f_{\theta}:=f\cdot\psi_{\theta}
for all
\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})
as in the previous subsection. Let
\tilde{\psi}_{\theta}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
be a family of functions satisfying
\tilde{\psi}_{\theta}(y)=1
if
y\in\mathrm{supp}\,\psi_{\theta}
and
\mathrm{supp}\,\tilde{\psi}_{\theta}\subseteq 2\cdot\theta
, where
2\cdot\theta
denotes the cap concentric to
\theta
but with twice the radius. We may choose the functions
\tilde{\psi}_{\theta}
so that

|\partial_{y}^{\beta}\tilde{\psi}_{\theta}(y)|\lesssim_{\beta}R^{|\beta|/2}\qquad\textrm{for all $\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n-1}$.}

By performing a Fourier series decomposition, we may express
f_{\theta}=f_{\theta}\cdot\tilde{\psi}_{\theta}
as

f_{\theta}(y)=\sum_{\omega\in R^{1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}}f_{\theta,\omega}(y)\quad\textrm{where}\quad f_{\theta,\omega}(y):=R^{(n-1)/2}(f_{\theta})\;\widecheck{}\;(\omega)e^{-i\langle\omega,y\rangle}\tilde{\psi}_{\theta}(y)

for Fourier coefficients

(f_{\theta})\;\widecheck{}\;(\omega):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}f_{\theta}(y)e^{i\langle y,\omega\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}y.

At this juncture it is useful to introduce some additional notation. Fix a ball
B_{R}=B(\mathbf{x}_{R},R)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and define the recentred phase

\phi_{B}(\mathbf{x};y):=\phi(\mathbf{x}_{B}+\mathbf{x};y)-\phi(\mathbf{x}_{B};y).

Given
\varepsilon>0
and
(\theta,\omega)\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})\times R^{1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
, define the associated
R
-tube
(over
B_{R}
with error
\varepsilon)
by

(9.4)

T_{\theta,\omega}:=\big\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(-2R,2R):|\partial_{y}\phi_{B}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};y_{\theta})-\omega|<R^{1/2+\varepsilon/100n}\big\};

here
y_{\theta}
denotes the centre of the cap
\theta
. We let
\mathbf{T}(R)
denote the set of all such
R
-tubes and write

f_{T}:=e^{i\phi^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{B};y)}f_{\theta,\omega}\qquad\textrm{for}\quad T=T_{\theta,\omega}\in\mathbf{T}(R).

Note that both
\mathbf{T}(R)
and the
f_{T}
depend on a choice of
R
-ball
B
(and the definition of the tubes depends on a choice of error parameter
\varepsilon>0
), but we suppress this in the notation. Fixing
\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})
, we also let
\mathbf{T}_{\theta}(R):=\{T_{\theta,\omega}:\omega\in R^{1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}\}
be the set of all tubes associated to
\theta
. With these definitions,

f=\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}f_{\theta}=e^{-i\phi^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{B};y)}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}_{\theta}(R)}f_{T}=e^{-i\phi^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}_{B};y)}\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}(R)}f_{T};

we refer to this as the
wave packet decomposition of
f
.

For
T=T_{\theta,\omega}\in\mathbf{T}(R)
, observe that

|(f_{\theta})\;\widecheck{}\;(\omega)|^{2}\sim R^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\|f_{T}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}

and moreover, by Plancherel’s theorem,

(9.5)

\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}_{\theta}(R)}\|f_{T}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}\sim\|f_{\theta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}.

Finally, we observe localisation properties of the functions
S^{\lambda}f_{T}
.

Lemma 9.2

(Spatial/temporal localisation of the wave packets)
.

Let
1\leq R\leq\lambda
,
\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})
with centre
y_{\theta}
and suppose
a_{\theta}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{D}^{n})
satisfies

\mathrm{supp}\,a_{\theta}\subseteq 2\cdot\theta\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}a_{\theta}(\mathbf{x};y)|\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}R^{-|\beta|/2},\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n},\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n-1}.

If
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Hörmander-type phase and
T=T_{\theta,\omega}\in\mathbf{T}_{\theta}(R)
and
f_{T}
are defined with respect to
\phi
as above, we have

(9.6)

|S^{\lambda}[\phi;a_{\theta}]f_{T}(\mathbf{x}_{B}+\mathbf{x})|\lesssim_{N}R^{-\frac{n-1}{4}}\|f_{T}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\big(1+R^{-1/2}|\partial_{y}\phi_{B}^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};y_{\theta})-\omega|\big)^{-N}

for all
\mathbf{x}\in B(0,2R)
and all
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

The bound (
9.6
) follows from a straightforward non-stationary phase (integration-by-parts) argument. Minor variants of Lemma
9.2
appear throughout the literature on oscillatory integral operators and we omit the details.

9.4.
Kernel estimates

Given
[\phi;a]
a phase-amplitude pair,
U^{\lambda}[\phi;a]
can be expressed as

U^{\lambda}[\phi;a]f(x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}K^{\lambda}[\phi;a](x,t;\omega)f(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\omega,

where the kernel
K^{\lambda}
is given by

K^{\lambda}[\phi;a](x,t;\omega):=\int_{\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}e^{i(\phi^{\lambda}(x,t;y)-\langle\omega,y\rangle)}a^{\lambda}(x,t;y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

We note the following pointwise estimates for the Fourier localised kernels.

Lemma 9.3

(Kernel estimate)
.

Let
\lambda\geq 1
,
\theta\in\Theta(\lambda^{-1/2})
with centre
y_{\theta}
and suppose
a_{\theta}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{D}^{n})
satisfies

\mathrm{supp}\,a_{\theta}\subseteq 2\cdot\theta\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}a_{\theta}(\mathbf{x};y)|\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\lambda^{-|\beta|/2},\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n},\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n-1}.

If
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Hörmander-type phase, then the inequality

(9.7)

|K^{\lambda}[\phi;a_{\theta}](\mathbf{x};\omega)|\lesssim_{N}\lambda^{-(n-1)/2}\big(1+\lambda^{-1/2}|\partial_{y}\phi^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};y_{\theta})-\omega|\big)^{-N}

holds for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

The bound (
9.7
) follows from a non-stationary phase argument, similar to that used in the proof of Lemma
9.2
. We omit the details.

9.5.
Oscillatory operators vs geometric maximal functions

By a standard duality argument, the square function appearing in the right-hand side of (
9.3
) can be estimated in terms of the corresponding Kakeya maximal function. This observation leads to the following lemma.

Lemma 9.4

(Bourgain
[
8
]
)
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
p(n)<p<\infty
. For any Hörmander-type phase
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\eta>0
,
\varepsilon>0
, we have

I)

\mathbf{K}_{p\to 1}\big(\phi;\beta(n,p)-\eta\big)\Rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{\infty\to s}(\phi;\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,s)-\eta/4+\varepsilon)
,

II)

\mathbf{N}_{p\to p}\big(\phi;\beta(n,p)-\eta\big)\Rightarrow\mathbf{LS}_{s}(\phi;\alpha_{\mathrm{LS}}(n,s)-\eta/4+\varepsilon)
,

where
2<s<q(n)
satisfies
p=(s/2)^{\prime}
.

Here the exponents
\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,s)
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{LS}}(n,s)
are as defined in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, and
\beta(n,p)
is as defined in (
1.7
). In particular, for the range of exponents
p
and
s
in Lemma
9.4
, we have

(9.8)

\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,s)=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{n+1}{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s}\Big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\alpha_{\mathrm{LS}}(n,s)=\frac{1}{s}+\frac{n-1}{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s}\Big)

whilst

\beta(n,p):=\frac{n-1}{2p}.

We remark that only Lemma
9.4
I) is treated in
[
8
]
(for
n=3
), but II) follows by a similar argument (as does the extension to
n\geq 2
).

Proof (of Lemma
9.4
).

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
N:=100n
and
1\leq R\leq\lambda
. Apply Proposition
9.1
to find some
M=M(n,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{N}
and symbols
a_{\theta,\gamma}
, indexed by
\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}
with
|\gamma|\leq M
, such that (
9.3
) holds with
\varepsilon
replaced with
\varepsilon/2
.

I) Assume
\mathbf{K}_{p\to 1}\big(\phi;\beta(n,p)-\eta\big)
. Fix
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})
and an
R
-ball
B_{R}=B(\mathbf{x}_{R},R)
and, by scaling, further assume that
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}=1
. By pigeonholing, we can find some choice of
\gamma_{\circ}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}_{0}
, depending on
f
, so that
S_{\theta}^{\lambda}:=S_{\theta,\gamma_{\circ}}^{\lambda}
satisfies

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\
|\gamma|\leq M\end{subarray}}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}|S_{\theta,\gamma}^{\lambda}f_{\theta}|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{s}(B_{2R})}\lesssim\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}|S_{\theta}^{\lambda}f_{\theta}|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{s}(B_{2R})};

here
B_{2R}
is the
2R
-ball concentric to
B_{R}
. We now apply the wave packet decomposition to
f
. By our scaling and combining (
9.5
) and Lemma
9.2
, we may assume that

|S_{\theta}^{\lambda}f_{T}(\mathbf{x}_{B}+\mathbf{x})|\lesssim_{N,\varepsilon}R^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}a_{T}\chi_{T}(\mathbf{x})+R^{-N}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})},\qquad\mathbf{x}\in B(0,2R),T\in\mathbf{T}(R),

where the coefficients
a_{T}\geq 0
satisfy

\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}_{\theta}(R)}|a_{T}|^{2}=1\qquad\textrm{for all $\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})$.}

Thus, by translating the variables,

\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}|S_{\theta}^{\lambda}f_{\theta}|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{s}(B_{2R})}\lesssim R^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\Big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}(R)}|a_{T}|^{2}\chi_{T}\Big\|_{L^{s/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{1/2}

holds up to a negligible error term. Combining this with the square function estimate from Proposition
9.1
, we deduce that

(9.9)

\|S^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{s}(B_{R})}\lesssim_{N,\varepsilon}R^{\frac{n-1}{4}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s})-\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\Big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}(R)}|a_{T}|^{2}\chi_{T}\Big\|_{L^{s/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{1/2}.

The expression appearing in the norm on the right-hand side of (
9.9
) looks similar to the multiplicity function appearing in the dual formulation of the Kakeya maximal bound, except that it involves a sum over many parallel tubes. To deal with this, we use an elementary probability trick that the authors learned from the course notes of Tao
[
37
]
. For each
\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})
, we choose a tube
T_{\theta}\in\mathbf{T}_{\theta}(R)
independently at random, where each tube
T\in\mathbf{T}_{\theta}(R)
has probability
|a_{T}|^{2}
of being selected. It follows that

\mathbb{E}\big[\chi_{T_{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})\big]=\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}_{\theta}(R)}|a_{T}|^{2}\chi_{T}(\mathbf{x})

and, by linearity of expectation,

(9.10)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}\chi_{T_{\theta}}(\mathbf{x})\Big]=\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}(R)}|a_{T}|^{2}\chi_{T}(\mathbf{x})

By combining (
9.10
) and Minkowski’s inequality,

\Big\|\sum_{T\in\mathbf{T}(R)}|a_{T}|^{2}\chi_{T}\Big\|_{L^{s/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\mathbb{E}\Big\|\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}\chi_{T_{\theta}}\Big\|_{L^{s/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

However, by applying the dual form of the Kakeya inequality and scaling, we have

(9.11)

\Big\|\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}\chi_{T_{\theta}}\Big\|_{L^{s/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim R^{\frac{2n}{s}+\varepsilon}\|\mathcal{K}^{R^{-1/2}}\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}.

Here we assume that the amplitude
a
in the operator is sufficiently localised, so that the resulting rescaled tubes can be parametrised by
(y,\omega)\in Y_{\phi}\times\Omega_{\phi}
as in §
1.2
. Thus, (
9.9
) and (
9.11
) give

(9.12)

\|S^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}R^{\frac{n-1}{4}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s})-\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{n}{s}+\varepsilon}\|\mathcal{K}^{R^{-1/2}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}^{1/2}.

Unpacking the statement
\mathbf{K}_{p\to 1}\big(\phi;\beta(n,p)-\eta\big)
, we have

\|\mathcal{K}^{R^{-1/2}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}^{1/2}\lesssim_{\phi,a}R^{\beta(n,p)/4-\eta/4}=R^{\frac{n-1}{4}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s})-\frac{\eta}{4}}.

Combining this with (
9.12
), simplifying the resulting
R
exponent using (
9.8
) and recalling our choice of normalisation for
f
, we conclude that

\|S^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}R^{\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,s)-\frac{\eta}{4}+\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}.

The desired result now follows from the definition of
\mathbf{H}_{\infty\to s}(\phi;\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,s)-\eta/4+\varepsilon)
.

II) Now assume
\mathbf{N}_{p\to p}\big(\phi;\beta(n,p)-\eta\big)
. We fix
R:=\lambda
, write
U_{\theta,\gamma}^{\lambda}:=U^{\lambda}[\phi;a_{\theta,\gamma}]
and let
P_{\theta}
denote the Littlewood–Paley projector with multiplier
\psi_{\theta}
for each
\theta\in\Theta(\lambda^{-1/2})
. Fixing
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
, we may apply the square function estimate to deduce that

(9.13)

\|U^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{N,\varepsilon}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\
|\gamma|\leq M\end{subarray}}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(\lambda^{-1/2})}|U_{\theta,\gamma}^{\lambda}\circ P_{\theta}f|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

holds up to the inclusion of a rapidly decaying error term.

As in the proof of part I), we apply pigeonholing to find some choice of
\gamma_{\circ}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}_{0}
, depending on
f
, so that
U_{\theta}^{\lambda}:=U_{\theta,\gamma_{\circ}}^{\lambda}
satisfies

(9.14)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\
|\gamma|\leq M\end{subarray}}\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}|U_{\theta,\gamma}^{\lambda}\circ P_{\theta}f|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})}|U_{\theta}^{\lambda}\circ P_{\theta}f|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Let
K^{\lambda}_{\theta}
denote the kernel of
U_{\theta}^{\lambda}
for each
\theta\in\Theta(R^{-1/2})
. By Lemma
9.3
, we have
\|K^{\lambda}_{\theta}(\mathbf{x};\,\cdot\,)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim 1
and so, by Cauchy–Schwarz,

|U_{\theta}^{\lambda}\circ P_{\theta}f(\mathbf{x})|^{2}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|K^{\lambda}_{\theta}(\mathbf{x};\omega)||P_{\theta}f(\omega)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega.

For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
we define a maximal function

\mathcal{N}_{N}^{\lambda^{-1/2}}g(\omega):=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\Theta(\lambda^{-1/2})\\
y_{\theta}\in Y_{\phi}\end{subarray}}\lambda^{-\frac{n+1}{2}}\int_{B(0,\rho\lambda)}\big(1+\lambda^{-1/2}|\partial_{y}\phi^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};y_{\theta})-\omega|\big)^{-N}|g(\mathbf{x})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

for
g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Here
Y_{\phi}
is the set appearing in §
1.2
and
0<\rho<1
depends on the size of the support of the underlying amplitude
a
. By combining (
9.13
) and (
9.14
) with a standard duality argument and Lemma
9.3
, we obtain

(9.15)

\|U^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\lesssim_{N,\varepsilon}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s})+1+\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(\lambda^{-1/2})}|P_{\theta}f(\omega)|^{2}\mathcal{N}_{N}^{\lambda^{-1/2}}g(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\omega

for some choice of
g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying
\|g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq 1
. Note that we pick up an extra factor of
\lambda
owing to the normalisation in the kernel estimate in Lemma
9.3
. In addition, here (as in the proof of I) we assume that the amplitude
a
in the operator is sufficiently localised.

It is well-known (see, for instance,
[
33
]
) that

(9.16)

\Big\|\big(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta(\lambda^{-1/2})}|P_{\theta}f|^{2}\big)^{1/2}\Big\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

Thus, matters reduce to showing

(9.17)

\|\mathcal{N}_{N}^{\lambda^{-1/2}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{p}(B^{n-1}(0,\lambda))}\lesssim_{N,\varepsilon}\lambda^{\frac{2}{s}-1+\varepsilon}\|\mathcal{N}^{\lambda^{-1/2}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}

for
N
sufficiently large. Indeed, once we have (
9.17
), we may apply Hölder’s inequality, (
9.16
) and (
9.17
) to estimate the right-hand side of (
9.15
), giving

\|U^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s})+\frac{1}{s}+\varepsilon}\|\mathcal{N}^{\lambda^{-1/2}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}^{1/2}\|f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

Unpacking the definition of
\mathbf{N}_{p\to p}\big(\phi;\beta(n,p)-\eta\big)
, we deduce that

\|U^{\lambda}f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{s})+\frac{1}{s}-\frac{\eta}{4}+\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

The desired result now follows from the definition of
\mathbf{LS}_{s}(\phi;\alpha_{\mathrm{LS}}(n,s)-\eta/4+\varepsilon)
.

Turning to the proof of (
9.17
), by choosing
N
large enough we can ensure

\mathcal{N}_{N}^{\lambda^{-1/2}}g(\omega)\lesssim\sup_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n-1}}\fint_{T_{y,\omega}}|g|+\lambda^{-10n}\|g\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

where
T_{y,\omega}
is the
\lambda
-tube with error
\varepsilon
as defined in (
9.4
) and
\omega\in B(0,\lambda)
. Note that any
\lambda
-tube with error
\varepsilon
can be covered by
O(\lambda^{\varepsilon/100})
parallel
\lambda
-tubes with error
0
. Combining this observation with a simple rescaling, and recalling that
-1/p=2/s-1
, we obtain the desired estimate (
9.17
).
∎

9.6.
The local-to-global principle

The final ingredient of the proof of Proposition
2.10
is the following ‘local-to-global’ lemma.

Lemma 9.5

(Bourgain
[
8
]
)
.

Let
2\leq s\leq q(n)
,
\alpha s<\tfrac{n+1}{2}
and

q(n,s,\alpha):=2+\frac{2s}{n+1-2\alpha s}.

For
q>q(n,s,\alpha)
and
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
a Hörmander-type phase,

\mathbf{H}_{\infty\to s}(\phi;\alpha)\Rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{\infty\to q}(\phi).

The
n=3
case of Lemma
9.5
is implicit in
[
8
]
. For completeness, we present a full proof, following the general scheme of
[
8
]
.

Proof (of Lemma
9.5
).

Fix
q>q(n,s,\alpha)
and let
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})
. Let
\mathfrak{R}z
denote the real part of
z\in\mathbb{C}
. For
\sigma>0
, define the superlevel set

F_{\sigma}:=\big\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\mathfrak{R}(S^{\lambda}f(\mathbf{x}))>\sigma\big\}.

By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\sigma|F_{\sigma}|\leq\mathfrak{R}\int_{F_{\sigma}}S^{\lambda}f(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\mathfrak{R}\langle S^{\lambda}f,\chi_{F_{\sigma}}\rangle.

Thus, by duality and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

(9.18)

\sigma|F_{\sigma}|\lesssim\|S^{\lambda,*}\chi_{F_{\sigma}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})},

where
S^{\lambda,*}
is the adjoint operator

S^{\lambda,*}g(y):=\int_{B^{n}}e^{-i\phi^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};y)}\overline{a^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};y)}g(\mathbf{x})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

By expanding out the square of the
L^{2}
-norm in (
9.18
), we have

\|S^{\lambda,*}\chi_{F_{\sigma}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}^{2}=\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}}\rangle

where

\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}g(\mathbf{x}):=S^{\lambda}S^{\lambda,*}g(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})g(\mathbf{z})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}

for the kernel

K^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}e^{i(\phi^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};y)-\phi^{\lambda}(\mathbf{z};y))}a^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x};y)\overline{a^{\lambda}(\mathbf{z};y)}\,\mathrm{d}y.

The hypotheses H1) and H2), together with a stationary phase argument, imply that

(9.19)

|K^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})|\lesssim(1+|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}|)^{-(n-1)/2}.

We now perform a Whitney decomposition in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with respect to the diagonal
\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
. In particular, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we let
\mathcal{Q}_{k}
denote the set of dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of side-length
2^{k}
. Given
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}
, we write
Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}
if
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are disjoint, but their parent intervals are not disjoint. It then follows that

1=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}\\
Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}\end{subarray}}\chi_{Q_{1}}(\mathbf{x})\chi_{Q_{2}}(\mathbf{z})

and so we may decompose

(9.20)

\|S^{\lambda,*}\chi_{F_{\sigma}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}\\
Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\rangle.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}
satisfying
Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}
. Using (
9.19
), we may estimate

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in Q_{2}\\
\mathbf{z}\in Q_{1}\end{subarray}}|K^{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})||F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}||F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\min\{1,2^{-k(n-1)/2}\}|F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}||F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}|,

since the hypothesis
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}
implies
\mathrm{dist}(Q_{1},Q_{2})\gtrsim 2^{k}
. Summing this bound therefore yields

(9.21)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}\\
Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}\end{subarray}}|\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\rangle|\lesssim\min\{1,2^{-k(n-1)/2}\}M|F_{\sigma}|\max_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}}|F_{\sigma}\cap Q|,

where
M:=\max_{Q_{1}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}}\#\{Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}:Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}\}
satisfies
M\lesssim 1
.

On the other hand, using Cauchy–Schwarz, we may bound

|\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\rangle|\leq\|S^{\lambda,*}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}\|S^{\lambda,*}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}.

We now apply Hörmander’s
L^{2}
estimate to deduce that

|\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\rangle|\lesssim 2^{k}|F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}|^{1/2}|F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}|^{1/2}

Summing this bound and multiple applications of Cauchy–Schwarz therefore yields

(9.22)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}\\
Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}\end{subarray}}|\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\rangle|\lesssim 2^{k}M|F_{\sigma}|.

Assume
\mathbf{H}_{\infty\to s}(\phi;\alpha)
, where
\alpha
and
s
satisfy the hypotheses of the lemma. Let
E\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{n-1}
be measurable and take
f:=i^{m}\chi_{E}
in the general framework, where
i
is the imaginary unit and
0\leq m\leq 3
. We estimate the local quantity
|F_{\sigma}\cap Q|
appearing in (
9.21
) using the hypothesised local estimate. In particular, by unpacking the definition of
\mathbf{H}_{\infty\to s}(\phi;\alpha)
and Chebyshev’s inequality,

\sigma^{s}|F_{\sigma}\cap Q|\leq\|S^{\lambda}\chi_{E}\|_{L^{s}(Q)}^{s}\lesssim 2^{ks\alpha}\qquad\textrm{for all $Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}$ and all $k\geq 0$.}

When
k<0
, we trivially have

\sigma^{s}|F_{\sigma}\cap Q|\leq\|S^{\lambda}\chi_{E}\|_{L^{s}(Q)}^{s}\leq|Q|\|S^{\lambda}\chi_{E}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}^{s}\lesssim|Q|\leq 1.

Combining this with (
9.21
), we obtain

(9.23)

\begin{split}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}\\
Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}\end{subarray}}|\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\rangle|\lesssim 2^{-k(\frac{n-1}{2}-s\alpha)}\sigma^{-s}M|F_{\sigma}|\qquad\textrm{when $k\geq 0$,}\\
&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{k}\\
Q_{1}\sim Q_{2}\end{subarray}}|\langle\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{1}},\chi_{F_{\sigma}\cap Q_{2}}\rangle|\lesssim\sigma^{-s}M|F_{\sigma}|\qquad\textrm{when $k<0$.}\end{split}

Together, (
9.20
), (
9.23
) and (
9.22
), and the fact that
M\lesssim 1
, imply that

(9.24)

\begin{split}\|S^{\lambda,*}\chi_{F_{\sigma}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}^{2}&\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z},k\geq 0}\min\{2^{-k(\frac{n-1}{2}-s\alpha)}\sigma^{-s},2^{k}\}|F_{\sigma}|+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z},k<0}\min\{\sigma^{-s},2^{k}\}|F_{\sigma}|\\
&\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z},k\geq 0}\min\{2^{-k(\frac{n-1}{2}-s\alpha)}\sigma^{-s},2^{k}\}|F_{\sigma}|+\min\{\sigma^{-s},1\}|F_{\sigma}|\\
&\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\min\{2^{-k(\frac{n-1}{2}-s\alpha)}\sigma^{-s},2^{k}\}|F_{\sigma}|.\end{split}

Here in the last step, we absorb the second term into the case
k=0
.
Let
k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that, for
k=k_{0}
, the two terms in the minimum are comparable; this is equivalent to choosing

2^{k_{0}}\sim\sigma^{-q+2+\eta}\qquad\textrm{where}\quad\eta:=q-q(n,s,\alpha)>0.

By partitioning the range of summation on the right-hand side of (
9.24
) according to whether
k>k_{0}
or
k\leq k_{0}
, we deduce that

\|S^{\lambda,*}\chi_{F_{\sigma}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n-1})}^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{-q+2+\eta}|F_{\sigma}|.

Finally, we combine the above inequality with (
9.18
) to obtain

\sigma^{2}|F_{\sigma}|^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{-q+2+\eta}|F_{\sigma}|,

which rearranges to give

(9.25)

|F_{\sigma}|\lesssim\sigma^{-q+\eta}.

Note that
\|S^{\lambda}\chi_{E}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{a}1
, where
a
denotes the amplitude associated to
S^{\lambda}
. Thus, in (
9.25
) it suffices to consider
0<\sigma\lesssim_{a}1
only, since otherwise the left-hand side is zero. Since
\eta>0
, the bound (
9.25
) therefore implies

|\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|S^{\lambda}\chi_{E}(\mathbf{x})|>\sigma\}|\lesssim_{\phi,a,q}\sigma^{-q}\qquad\textrm{for all $\sigma>0$.}

The desired result now follows by restricted weak-type interpolation.
∎

9.7.
Concluding the proof of Proposition
2.10

It remains to combine our earlier lemmas to conclude the proof of the geometric reduction.

Proof (of Proposition
2.10
).

Fix
p(n)<p<\infty
and
0\leq\beta<\beta(n;p)
, so that
\eta:=\beta(n,p)-\beta>0
. Let
2<s<q(n)
satisfy
p=(s/2)^{\prime}
.

I) Take
\alpha:=\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,s)-\eta/4
, where
\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,s)
is as in (
9.8
), so that

n+1-2\alpha s=\frac{(n-1+\eta)s}{2}>0.

It follows that the exponent
q(n,s,\alpha)
as featured in Lemma
9.5
satisfies

q(n,s,\alpha):=2+\frac{4}{n-1+\eta}<q(n)=2+\frac{4}{n-1}.

Furthermore,
\alpha s<\frac{n+1}{2}
for
n\geq 3
. Combining Lemma
9.4
and Lemma
9.5
, given any
q(n,s,\alpha)<q<q(n)
we may choose
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small so that

\mathbf{K}_{p\to 1}(\phi;\beta)\Rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{\infty\to s}(\phi;\alpha_{\mathrm{H}}(n,s)-\eta/4+\varepsilon)\Rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{\infty\to q}(\phi).

This concludes the proof of I).

II) Taking
\alpha:=\alpha_{\mathrm{LS}}(n,s)-\eta/8
, Lemma
9.4
already ensures that

\mathbf{N}_{p\to p}(\phi;\beta)\Rightarrow\mathbf{LS}_{s}(\phi;\alpha),

which concludes the proof of II).
∎

Appendix A
Tools from real algebraic geometry

For the reader’s convenience, here we recall the definitions and results from real algebraic geometry that play a role in our arguments in §
4
.

Definition A.1
.

A set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
semialgebraic
if there exists a finite collection of polynomials
P_{i,j}
,
Q_{i,j}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
for
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq j\leq s
such that

(A.1)

S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:P_{i,1}(x)=\cdots=P_{i,s}(x)=0,\,Q_{i,1}(x)>0,\dots,Q_{i,s}(x)>0\big\}.

Given a semialgebraic set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
the
complexity
of
S
is

\inf\Big(\sum_{i,j}\deg P_{i,j}+\deg Q_{i,j}\Big)

where the infimum is taken over all possible representations of
S
of the form (
A.1
).

Definition A.2
.

Let
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be semialgebraic sets. We say
f\colon T\rightarrow S
is a
semialgebraic map
if its graph is a semialgebraic subset of
\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
. The
dimension
of
S
is the largest
d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that there exists an injective semialgebraic map from
(0,1)^{d}
to
S
.

We briefly recount three key results concerning semialgbraic sets, used in §
4
.

Wongkew’s theorem

We make considerable use of the following theorem of Wongkew
[
40
]
, which bounds the volume of neighbourhoods of algebraic varieties.

Theorem A.3

(Wongkew
[
40
]
)
.

Suppose
\mathbf{Z}
is an
m
-dimensional variety in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\deg\mathbf{Z}\leq d
. For any
0<\rho\leq\lambda
and
\lambda
-ball
B_{\lambda}
the neighbourhood
N_{\!\rho}(\mathbf{Z}\cap B_{\lambda})
can be covered by
O_{d}((\lambda/\rho)^{m})
balls of radius
\rho
.

The Tarski–Seidenberg projection theorem

A fundamental result in the theory of semialgebraic sets is the Tarski–Seidenberg projection theorem, which is also referred to as
quantifier elimination
. A useful reference for this material is
[
1
]
.

Theorem A.4

(Tarski–Seidenberg)
.

Let
\Pi
be the orthogonal projection of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
into its first
n-1
coordinates. Then for every
E\geq 1
, there is a constant
C(n,E)>0
so that, for every semialgebraic
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of complexity at most
E
, the projection
\Pi(S)
has complexity at most
C(n,E)
.

We repeatedly use Theorem
A.4
to form semialgebraic
sections
.

Corollary A.5
.

Let
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
be a compact semialgebraic set of complexity at most
E
. Let
\Pi
be the orthogonal projection into the final
n
coordinates

(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{d})\mapsto\mathbf{d}.

Then there is a constant
C(n,E)>0
, depending only on
n
and
E
, and a semialgebraic set
Z
, of complexity at most
C(n,E)
, so that

Z\subset S,\quad\quad\Pi(Z)=\Pi(S),

and so that for each
\mathbf{d},
there is at most one
\mathbf{a}
with

(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{d})\in Z.

This is Lemma 2.2 from
[
28
]
. It is a direct consequence of Theorem
A.4
, as discussed in
[
28
]
.

Gromov’s algebraic lemma

The final key tool is the existence of useful parameterisations of semialgebraic sets, as guaranteed by the following lemma.

Lemma A.6

(Gromov)
.

For all integers
E,n,r\geq 1
, there exists
M(E,n,r)<\infty
with the following
properties. For any compact semialgebraic set
A\subset[0,1]^{n}
of dimension
m
and complexity at most
E
, there exists
an integer
N\leq M(E,n,r)
and
C^{r}
maps
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{N}:[0,1]^{m}\longrightarrow[0,1]^{n}
such that

\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}\phi_{j}([0,1]^{m})=A\quad\text{and}\quad\|\phi_{j}\|_{C^{r}}:=\max_{|\alpha|\leq r}\|\partial^{\alpha}\phi_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq 1.

This result was originally stated by Gromov. Detailed proofs were later given by Pila and Wilkie
[
31
]
and Burguet
[
10
]
.
